On a generalisation of Cameron’s base size conjecture
Marina Anagnostopoulou-Merkouri

Abstract
. Let G ⩽ Sym ⁡ ( Ω ) G\leqslant{\rm Sym}(\Omega) be a finite transitive permutation group with point stabiliser H H . A base for G G is a subset of Ω \Omega whose pointwise stabiliser is trivial, and the minimal cardinality of a base is called the base size of G G , denoted by b ⁡ ( G , Ω ) b(G,\Omega) . Equivalently, b ⁡ ( G , Ω ) b(G,\Omega) is the minimal positive integer k k such that G G has a regular orbit on the Cartesian product Ω k \Omega^{k} . A well-known conjecture of Cameron from the 1990s asserts that if G G is an almost simple primitive group and H H is a so-called non-standard subgroup, then b ⁡ ( G , Ω ) ⩽ 7 b(G,\Omega)\leqslant 7 , with equality if and only if G G is the Mathieu group M 24 {\rm M}_{24} in its natural action of degree 24 24 . This conjecture was settled in a series of papers by Burness et al. (2007-11). In this paper, we complete the proof of a natural generalisation of Cameron’s conjecture. Our main result states that if G G is an almost simple group and H 1 , … , H k H_{1},\ldots,H_{k} are any core-free non-standard maximal subgroups of G G with k ⩾ 7 k\geqslant 7 , then G G has a regular orbit on G / H 1 × ⋯ × G / H k G/H_{1}\times\cdots\times G/H_{k} , noting that Cameron’s original conjecture corresponds to the special case where the H i H_{i} are pairwise conjugate subgroups. In addition, we show that the same conclusion holds with k ⩾ 6 k\geqslant 6 , unless G = M 24 G={\rm M}_{24} and each H i H_{i} is isomorphic to M 23 {\rm M}_{23} . For example, this means that if G G is a simple exceptional group of Lie type and H 1 , … , H 6 H_{1},\ldots,H_{6} are proper subgroups of G G , then there exist elements g i ∈ G g_{i}\in G such that ⋂ i H i g i = 1 \bigcap_{i}H_{i}^{g_{i}}=1 . By applying recent work in a joint paper with Burness, we may assume G G is a group of Lie type and our proof uses probabilistic methods based on fixed point ratio estimates.



1.
Introduction

Let
G\leqslant\operatorname{Sym}(\Omega)
be a transitive permutation group on a finite set
\Omega
. Recall that a
base
for
G
is a subset of
\Omega
whose pointwise stabiliser is trivial. We write
b(G,\Omega)
for the
base size
of
G
, which is the minimal cardinality of a base for
G
. This classical invariant has been extensively studied since the early days of group theory in the nineteenth century, finding a wide range of applications to other areas of mathematics such as graph theory and model theory, for example. Bases are also fundamental in the computational study of finite groups and the implementation of efficient algorithms. There is an extensive literature on bases for permutation groups and we refer the reader to the survey articles
[
7
,
62
,
67
]
and
[
20
, Section 5]
for further details.

Determining the base size of a permutation group is a fundamental problem in group theory and it has attracted significant interest in recent years. In particular, there has been particular focus in the study of base sizes of primitive groups, motivated by some highly influential conjectures by Babai, Cameron, Kantor, and Pyber stated in the 1990s (Recall that a transitive permutation group
G\leqslant{\rm Sym}(\Omega)
is
primitive
if a point stabiliser is a maximal subgroup of
G
). All those conjectures have now been resolved.

Recall that a finite group
G
is
almost simple
if there exists a nonabelian simple group
G_{0}
such that
G_{0}\trianglelefteqslant G\leqslant{\rm Aut}(G_{0})
. We call
G_{0}
the
socle
of
G
. We say that a subgroup
H
of a finite group
G
is
core-free
if
\bigcap_{g\in G}H^{g}=1
. Note that if
G
is almost simple, then
H\leqslant G
is core-free if it does not contain
G_{0}
. Recall the following definition:

Definition
.

Let
G
be a finite almost simple group with socle
G_{0}
and let
H
be a core-free maximal subgroup of
G
. We say that
H
is
standard
if one of the following holds:

(i)

G_{0}=A_{n}
and the natural action of
H
on
\{1,\ldots,n\}
is not primitive;

(ii)

G
is a classical group and
H
is a subspace subgroup of
G
.

We say that
H
is
non-standard
otherwise.

We will define subspace subgroups of classical groups precisely in Section
3
(see Definition
3.4
), but roughly speaking, if
G
is a classical group, then
H
is standard if it is
reducible
. That is,
H\cap G_{0}
acts reducibly on the natural module. Note that if there exists an isomorphism of
G
to some other group that maps
H
to a standard subgroup, then we will also say that
H
is standard in this case. For instance, we note that
{\rm PSL}_{4}(2)\cong A_{8}
and the subgroup of type
{\rm GL}_{2}(4)
gets mapped to the intransitive subgroup
(S_{3}\times S_{5})\cap A_{8}
by an isomorphism. We will therefore say that the subgroup of type
{\rm GL}_{2}(4)
is standard in
{\rm PSL}_{4}(2)
. Those cases are handled in Table
3
.

If
G\leqslant{\rm Sym}(\Omega)
is a primitive group with point stabiliser
H
, then we will say that the action of
G
on
G/H
is non-standard if
H
is non-standard. It is natural to distinguish between standard and non-standard actions when studying bases. More specifically, we observe that there is a dichotomy between standard and non-standard actions in terms of base sizes. In particular, standard actions tend to admit ‘large bases’. For example, the base sizes of alternating and symmetric groups in standard actions have been determined due to work by several authors (see
[
21
,
35
,
45
,
46
,
68
,
69
]
) and we observe that in the majority of cases,
b(G,\Omega)
grows with
n
, and consequently
b(G,\Omega)
can get arbitrarily large as
n\rightarrow\infty
. As we now explain, almost simple groups in non-standard actions exhibit a completely different behaviour with respect to base sizes.

It was conjectured by Cameron and Kantor in
[
29
]
that if
G
is a primitive almost simple permutation group in a non-standard action, then
b(G,\Omega)
is bounded above by an absolute constant independent of
G
. This was proved by Liebeck and Shalev in
[
55
]
. In response, Cameron conjectured in
[
28
, p.122]
that the best possible value of this absolute constant is
7
. This conjecture was proved in a series of papers by Burness et al.
[
18
,
23
,
25
,
26
]
. In particular, we have the following result:

Theorem
.

Let
G\leqslant{\rm Sym}(\Omega)
be an almost simple primitive permutation group with non-standard point stabiliser
H
. Then
b(G,\Omega)\leqslant 7
with equality if and only if
(G,H)=({\rm M}_{24},{\rm M}_{23})
.

The aim of this paper is to generalise Theorem
Theorem
in the setting we describe below:

Let
G
be a finite group and let
H_{1},\ldots,H_{k}
be a collection of core-free subgroups of
G
, allowing repetitions. Consider the natural action of
G
on the Cartesian product

X=G/H_{1}\times\cdots\times G/H_{k}

and observe that
G
has a regular orbit on
X
if and only if

\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}H_{i}^{g_{i}}=1

for some elements
g_{i}\in G
. Following
[
2
]
, we say that a
k
-tuple
(H_{1},\ldots,H_{k})
of core-free subgroups of
G
is
regular
if
G
has a regular orbit on
X
, and
non-regular
otherwise. We will always be interested in the regularity of subgroup tuples all of whose components are core-free, and so, given a subgroup tuple, we will always assume that its components are core-free. We will also say that
\tau
is
conjugate
if the
H_{i}
are pairwise conjugate. Finally, we call the minimal
r
such that all
r
-tuples of core-free subgroups of
G
are regular the
regularity number
of
G
, which we denote by
R(G)
.

Recall that a transitive group
G\leqslant{\rm Sym}(\Omega)
has a base of size
k
if and only if
G
has a regular orbit on
\Omega^{k}
. Therefore, a conjugate tuple
(H_{1},\ldots,H_{k})
is regular if and only if
G
admits a base of size
k
in its natural action on
G/H_{1}
. This implies that
k\geqslant b(G,H_{1})
.

If
G
is almost simple and all the components of a tuple
\tau=(H_{1},\ldots,H_{k})
are non-standard, we will say that
\tau
is
non-standard
. Hence, in this language, Theorem
Theorem
asserts that every conjugate non-standard
7
-tuple of
G
is regular.

The regularity of subgroup tuples was introduced and studied in
[
2
]
for almost simple groups with alternating and sporadic socle, where Burness and the author stated generalisations of some of the most influential base size conjectures in the regularity setting, including Cameron’s conjecture. In particular, if
G
is a finite almost simple group and
R_{\rm ns}(G)
denotes the minimal integer
r
such that all non-standard tuples of
G
are regular, then they conjecture that
R_{\rm ns}(G)\leqslant 7
, with equality if and only if
G={\rm M}_{24}
. Note that this conjecture is a vast generalisation of Theorem
Theorem
. As noted above, Theorem
Theorem
implies that all conjugate non-standard
7
-tuples of a finite almost simple group
G
are regular and it is claimed that the conjugacy assumption can be removed.

The above conjecture was proved in
[
2
]
for almost simple groups with alternating and sporadic socle, which reduces the problem to the almost simple groups of Lie type. The main aim of this paper is to complete the proof of this conjecture. We now state our main theorem:

Theorem 1
.

Let
G
be a finite almost simple group. Then
R_{\rm ns}(G)\leqslant 7
with equality if and only if
G={\rm M}_{24}
.

It is shown in
[
2
]
, that up to conjugacy, the only non-regular non-standard
6
-tuple when
G={\rm M}_{24}
is
(H,H,H,H,H,H)
, where
H={\rm M}_{23}
. Moreover, note that if
G
is sporadic or exceptional, then all of its subgroups are non-standard, so Theorem
1
asserts that
R(G)\leqslant 7
in this case.

As we will see in Section
3
, we can prove a stronger statement than Theorem
1
for classical groups. In particular, if
G
is an almost simple classical group, then we will show that every non-standard
4
-tuple is regular, unless
G={\rm U}_{6}(2).2
, in which case
R_{\rm ns}(G)=5
and the only non-regular non-standard
4
-tuple up to conjugacy is
(H,H,H,H)
with
H={\rm U}_{4}(3).2^{2}
. Combining this with the results in
[
2
]
, we obtain a more refined version of Theorem
1
, which we state below:

Theorem 2
.

Let
G
be a finite almost simple group with socle
G_{0}
:

(i)

If
G_{0}=A_{n}
, then
R_{\rm ns}(G)\leqslant 6
with equality if and only if
G=A_{8}
. Moreover, if
n\geqslant 13
, then
R_{\rm ns}(G)=2
;

(ii)

If
G_{0}
is sporadic, then
R_{\rm ns}(G)=R(G)\leqslant 7
with equality if and only if
G={\rm M}_{24}
. Moreover, the exact value of
R(G)
is recorded in
[
2
, Table 3]
;

(iii)

If
G_{0}
is a classical group, then
R_{\rm ns}(G)\leqslant 5
with equality if and only if
G={\rm U}_{6}(2).2
;

(iv)

If
G_{0}
is an exceptional group, then
R_{\rm ns}(G)=R(G)\leqslant 6
.

We now briefly discuss the methods we use to prove Theorem
1
for groups of Lie type. These are similar to those used in the proof of Cameron’s conjecture. In particular, our main tool will be a generalised version of the powerful probabilistic method based on fixed point ratio estimates that was first introduced in
[
63
]
by Liebeck and Shalev for the study of bases.

Let
G
be a finite group and let
\tau=(H_{1},\ldots,H_{k})
be a tuple of core-free subgroups of
G
. We will write
X_{\tau}=G/H_{1}\times\cdots\times G/H_{k}
and
\mathbb{P}(G,\tau)
for the probability that a randomly chosen point in
X_{\tau}
is in a regular orbit. Our general approach will be to show that

Q(G,\tau)=1-\mathbb{P}(G,\tau)<1.

Note that generally, it is impossible to compute
Q(G,\tau)
for a finite group
G
. However, in most cases it is sufficient to work with the upper bound below:

Q(G,\tau)\leqslant\widehat{Q}(G,\tau):=\sum_{i=1}^{t}|x_{i}^{G}|\cdot\left(\prod_{j=1}^{k}{\rm fpr}(x_{i},G/H_{j})\right)

where
x_{1},\ldots,x_{t}
is a set of representatives of the prime order conjugacy classes in
G
and
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)
denotes the
fixed point ratio
of
x
in the natural action of
G
on the cosets of
H
. That is, the proportion of fixed points of
x
in this action. We refer the reader to Section
2.1
for more information.

If
G
is an almost simple classical group with socle
G_{0}
with natural module
V
and
\tau
is a non-standard tuple of
G
, then our main tool when estimating
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)
the following ‘zeta type function’

\eta_{G}(t)=\sum_{C\in\mathscr{C}}|C|^{-t}

where
\mathscr{C}
denotes the set of prime order
G_{0}
-conjugacy classes of
G
. More specifically, Burness obtains upper bounds on
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)
for a prime order element
x
and a non-standard subgroup
H
in terms of the size of the
G
-class of
x
in
[
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
]
. Using these allows us to bound
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)
by
\eta_{G}(t)
for some
t\in(0,1)
, and our problem reduces to showing that
\eta_{G}(t)<1
. In some cases, we will need stronger estimates on
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)
than the ones obtained
[
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
]
, and we obtain those in Section
4
. Finally, when
{\rm dim}V\leqslant 5
, using
\eta_{G}(t)
will no longer be possible, so we take a more direct approach to estimate
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)
.

If
G
is exceptional, then we obtain a character theoretic approach instead. In particular, if
H
is a maximal subgroup of
G
and
\Omega=G/H
, then we have

{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)=\frac{\chi(x)}{|\Omega|},

where
\chi(x)
is the corresponding permutation character. Noting this, we can employ powerful tools developed by Lawther, Liebeck and Seitz in
[
51
]
to estimate
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)
. In this way, for every prime order element
x\in G
, we are able to compute

f(x,q):=\max\{{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\,:\,H\in\mathcal{M}_{G}\}

where
\mathcal{M}_{G}
denotes the set of maximal subgroups of
G
. This allows us to obtain a uniform upper bound on
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)
that applies to any
6
-tuple
\tau
of maximal subgroups of
G
.

The main challenge when the conjugacy assumption on our subgroup tuples is removed is that we need to adopt a uniform approach that is applicable to any non-standard tuple. In particular, our arguments no longer depend on a particular action of our group
G
, which means that we are always bound to worst case fixed point ratio estimates over all maximal subgroups of
G
for all prime order elements of
G
.

Existing worst case bounds in the literature for a given element
x\in G
might be good enough for a particular action of
G
, but might not be sufficient in this general setting. This means that we will often need to obtain stronger fixed point ratio estimates for our purposes. We state those in Theorem
4.1
for the classical groups. For exceptional groups, we record worst case fixed point ratio estimates over non-parabolic actions in Propositions
8.1
,
8.2
,
8.3
, and
8.4
. Our main source for obtaining those is inspection of
[
25
]
and
[
19
]
, and in some instances we need to improve what currently is available. We give detailed information in Sections
2
,
4
and
8
.

Additionally, for classical groups there are certain instances where existing information on
\eta_{G}(t)
is no longer sufficient on our purposes and we will need stronger results. We obtain those in Section
5
.

The heavily probabilistic approach we adopt to prove Theorem
1
allows us to also obtain an asymptotic result. In particular, if
G
is an almost simple group of Lie type,
\mathcal{P}
denotes the set of non-standard subgroups of
G
and

\mathbb{P}(G,c)=\min\{\mathbb{P}(G,\tau)\,:\,\tau\in\mathcal{P}^{c}\},

then we have the following theorem:

Theorem 3
.

If
G
is a finite almost simple group of Lie type, then
\mathbb{P}(G,6)\rightarrow 1
as
|G|\rightarrow\infty
.

This paper is organised as follows: In Section
2
we record some preliminary results. In Section
3
, we proceed by recording some preliminaries that only apply to classical groups. We first discuss subgroup structure and conjugacy classes of classical groups, and then proceed to give a precise definition of a subspace subgroup of a classical group. The rest of the section is devoted to probabilistic and computational methods, which are the key tools that we will be using.

Section
4
is devoted to improving existing fixed point ratio estimates for low-dimensional classical groups, in Section
5
we prove some results relating to the ‘zeta-type’ function introduced in Definition
3.10
, and Section
6
, the final section on classical groups is devoted to the proof of Theorem
3.1
, a refinement of Theorem
1
for classical groups.

In Section
7
we record some preliminaries that only apply to exceptional groups. In particular, the section is divided into two subsections, where we briefly discuss subgroup structure and conjugacy classes respectively. In Section
8
we record fixed point ratio estimates for non-parabolic actions of exceptional groups, and then in Section
9
we proceed to discuss fixed point ratios for parabolic actions. Finally, Sections
10.1
,
10.2
, and
10.3
are devoted to the proof of Theorem
7.1
, a refinement of Theorem
1
for exceptional groups.

Notation.
Our notation is fairly standard. We write
{\rm Inndiag}(G_{0})
for the group of inner-diagonal automorphisms of a simple group of Lie type
G_{0}
. Moreover, we will also often write
\pi(n)
to denote the set of prime divisors of a positive integer
n
and
\pi_{a}(n)
to denote the set of prime divisors of
n
that are no less than
a
. In a similar fashion, we will denote the set of prime numbers at most
s
by
\pi_{s}
and the set of odd prime numbers at most
s
by
\pi^{\prime}_{s}
. For a group
G
, we will denote the number of elements of order
s
in
G
by
i_{s}(G)
. For classical groups, we write
{\rm PSL}^{+}_{n}(q)={\rm PSL}_{n}(q)={\rm L}_{n}(q)
and
{\rm PSL}^{-}_{n}(q)={\rm PSU}_{n}(q)={\rm U}_{n}(q)
, We use the same notation and terminology as
[
22
]
for field, graph, and graph-field automorphisms. Finally, we follow the definition of
[
47
]
for the
\mathscr{C}_{i}
and
\mathscr{S}
classes of maximal subgroups of classical groups. We will also be using the standard notation
E_{6}^{\epsilon}(q)
for the finite exceptional groups of type
E_{6}
, where
E_{6}^{+}(q)=E_{6}(q)
and
E_{6}^{-}(q)={}^{2}E_{6}(q)
. We will introduce more technical terminology, when and where appropriate in the paper.

GAP
-readable data on Foulkes functions of exceptional groups. Finally, she thanks the Heilbronn Institute for Mathematical Research for funding her PhD at the university of Bristol.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section, we record some preliminary lemmas that we will repeatedly use to prove Theorem
1
. We first discuss a small number theoretic lemma that we will repeatedly use to derive bounds on conjugacy class sizes, we then proceed to discuss probabilistic methods, and finally we briefly comment on computational methods.

Lemma 2.1
.

The following are true:

•

If
\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}\}
and
\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}\}
be two sets of integers, all at least
2
, then

\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{m}(q^{a_{i}}-1)}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}(q^{b_{i}}-1)}>2q^{\sum a_{i}-\sum b_{i}}

•

If
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
with
a<b
, then

\frac{(q^{a}+1)}{(q^{b}+1)}<q^{a-b}

This lemma is
[
51
, 1.2 (i) and (iii)]

2.1.
Probabilistic methods

Our main tool for proving our main result is the probabilistic method. Recall that if
G\leqslant{\rm Sym}(\Omega)
is a transitive permutation group with point stabiliser
H
, then the
fixed point ratio
of
x\in G
is defined as

{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)=\frac{|C_{\Omega}(x)|}{|\Omega|},

where
C_{\Omega}(x)=\{\omega\in\Omega\,:\,\omega^{x}=\omega\}
is the set of fixed points of
x
and
x^{G}
denotes the conjugacy class of
x
in
G
. We will use a generalisation of the powerful probabilistic approach based on fixed point ratio estimates that was first introduced in
[
63
]
by Liebeck and Shalev for the study of bases, which is applicable in the more general regularity setting. We now state and prove some preliminary lemmas about fixed point ratios for finite almost simple permutation groups that will help us confirm the regularity of tuples.

We first state two lemmas that appeared in
[
2
]
, which are natural generalisations of equivalent lemmas used for the study of base sizes of permutation groups, which can be found in
[
18
]
. Throughout the paper, if
n
is a natural number, we will often write
[n]
to denote the set
\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
G
be a finite group and let
\tau=(H_{1},\ldots,H_{k})
be a core-free tuple of subgroups of
G
. Then
\tau
is regular if

\widehat{Q}(G,\tau):=\sum_{i=1}^{t}|x_{i}^{G}|\cdot\left(\prod_{j=1}^{k}{\rm fpr}(x_{i},G/H_{j})\right)<1,

where
x_{1},\ldots,x_{t}
is a set of representatives of the conjugacy classes in
G
of elements of prime order.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
G
be a finite group and let
\tau=(H_{1},\ldots,H_{k})
be a core-free tuple of subgroups of
G
. Suppose
x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}
are elements in
G
such that
|x_{i}^{G}|\geqslant B
for all
i\in[m]
and
\sum_{i=1}^{m}|x_{i}^{G}\cap H_{j}|\leqslant A_{j}
for all
j\in[k]
. Then

\sum_{i=1}^{m}|x_{i}^{G}|\cdot\left(\prod_{j=1}^{k}{\rm fpr}(x_{i},G/H_{j})\right)\leqslant B^{1-k}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{k}A_{j}

Our aim will be to derive fixed point ratio bounds that apply to all almost simple groups with a fixed socle
G_{0}
. We now prove a small lemma that will allow us to deal with all such cases simultaneously.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
G
be an almost simple group with socle
G_{0}
, let
H
be a core-free maximal subgroup of
G
, and let
x\in G
be an element of prime order
r
. If
G_{1}=\langle G_{0},x\rangle
and
H_{1}=H\cap G_{1}
, then
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)={\rm fpr}(x,G_{1}/H_{1})
.

Proof.

First note that the maximality of
H
implies that
G=G_{0}H
. In particular,
G=G_{1}H
and therefore
G/H=G_{1}H/H
. This implies that
G_{1}
also acts transitively on the cosets of
H
.

We will now prove that
G/H=\Omega
and
G_{1}/H_{1}=\Omega_{1}
are isomorphic transitive
G_{1}
-sets. Define
\phi:\Omega\rightarrow\Omega_{1}
by
\phi(Hg)=H_{1}g
for
g\in G_{1}
. We claim that
\phi
is a bijection. Suppose that
H_{1}y=H_{1}z
for some
y,z\in G_{1}
. Then
yz^{-1}\in H_{1}
, and so
yz^{-1}\in H
, since
H_{1}\leqslant H
. Therefore,
\phi
is injective. Moreover,
|G:H|=|G_{1}:H_{1}|
, and so
\phi
is bijective.

Now note that if
y,z\in G_{1}
, then

\phi(Hyz)=H_{1}yz=\phi(Hy)z

so the actions of
G_{1}
on
G/H
and
G_{1}/H_{1}
are indeed equivalent. This implies that
|C_{\Omega}(x)|=|C_{\Omega_{1}}(x)|
, and therefore

\mbox{{\rm fpr}}(x,G/H)=\frac{|C_{\Omega}(x)|}{|\Omega|}=\frac{|C_{\Omega_{1}}(x)|}{|\Omega_{1}|}={\rm fpr}(x,G_{1}/H_{1}),

as required.
∎

Remark 2.5
.

Note that if
G_{0}\leqslant\langle G_{0},x\rangle\leqslant{\rm Inndiag}(G_{0})=G_{I}
, then Lemma
2.4
implies that

{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant\frac{|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|}{|x^{G_{0}}|}

We will repeatedly use this observation when computing bounds for
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)
.

2.2.
Computational methods

Here we briefly discuss how we use
GAP

[
43
]
and
Magma

[
10
]
in our proofs. Note that we only discuss some of the methods here. In particular, for exceptional groups, we heavily use the polynomial functionality in
GAP
and
Magma
to compute fixed point ratios, but we discuss this in detail later in Section
9
.

Let
G
be a finite almost simple group of Lie type over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
with socle
G_{0}
. There are two natural instances where we use
Magma
in our proofs. The first is in Section
4
where
G
is classical and we aim to improve the existing fixed point ratio bound given in Theorem
3.9
. There are some cases where we can only derive rather crude estimates, which prove the result when
q
is sufficiently large, but do not suffice when
q
is relatively small. In all of those cases, the dimension of the natural module for
G_{0}
is relatively low (in particular no more than
8
), so we construct
G
using the
Magma
function
AutomorphismGroupSimpleGroup()
and call
MaximalSubgroups(G)
to obtain a set of representatives of the
G
-classes of maximal subgroups of
G
. We then filter out the non-standard subgroups of
G
and one can verify the necessary fixed point ratio bounds for all of those using the following functions:

fpr := function( G, H, g )
cl:=Classes(H);
A:=[cl[i][2] : i in [1..#cl] | IsConjugate(G,cl[i][3],g)];
Append(~A,0);
return &+A*#Centraliser(G,g)/#G;
end function;

MaxFPR := function(G,H)
cl:=Classes(G);
a:=[i : i in [1..#cl] | IsPrime(cl[i][1])];
b:=[i : i in a | fpr(G,H,cl[i][3]) gt 0];
c,d:=Maximum([-Log(fpr(G,H,cl[i][3]))/Log(cl[i][2]): i in b]);
return c;
end function;

The function
fpr
takes as input a permutation group
G
, a subgroup
H
of
G
and an element
g\in G
and computes
{\rm fpr}(g,G/H)
. The function
MaxFpr
takes as input a group
G
a subgroup
H
of
G
and and returns the largest
t
so that
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<|x^{G}|^{-t}
for all
x\in G
of prime order. The case where the computation using the code above is the most time consuming is when
G_{0}={\rm PSp}_{8}(3)
, in which case we can get output in less than an hour.

The second instance where we will be using
Magma
is in some cases where we want to check confirm the regularity of a non-standard tuple
\tau
of
G
. As mentioned above, our standard method is to bound
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)
above by
1
, but again, there are instances where the bounds we obtain are not good enough for small values of
q
, so in these cases we use the function
RegTuplesPlus
presented in
[
1
, p.4]
instead, to verify the regularity all
R_{\rm ns}(G)
-tuples of
G
. The function takes as input a finite group
G
, an ordered tuple
M=[H_{1},\ldots,H_{t}]
of subgroups of
G
and two positive integers
l
and
N
with
l\geqslant 2
. The output is the minimal integer
k\geqslant l
such that all
k
-tuples of
G
are regular, together with all the non-regular
(k-1)
-tuples of
G
. We refer the reader to
[
1
]
for the code and more information.

To be able to use
RegTuplesPlus
, both
|G|
needs to be relatively small. In particular, we only make use of this function only if
G_{0}\in\{{}^{2}F_{4}(2)^{\prime},{}^{3}D_{4}(2)\}
for exceptional groups and when
G
is at most
8
-dimensional for classical groups. In the classical case, the maximal value of
q
we use
RegTuplesPlus
for, highly depends on the dimension of the natural module and the Lie type of the group we are considering, but it is always relatively small. For example, if
G_{0}={\rm PSp}_{8}(q)
, then we only use
Magma
for the case
q=2
, whilst if
G_{0}=L_{2}(q)
, then we use
Magma
for
q\geqslant 31
.

Finally, if
G_{0}=F_{4}(2)
or
E_{6}^{\epsilon}(2)
, then we will use the Character Table Library in
GAP

[
13
]
to compute fixed point ratios for certain non-parabolic maximal subgroups.

3.
Classical group preliminaries

Throughout this section, as well as Sections
4
,
5
and
6
, we will take
G
to be a finite almost simple classical group over a finite field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, where
q=p^{f}
for some prime
p
and some positive integer
f
. We will write
G_{0}
for the socle of
G
and
V
will denote the natural module for
G_{0}
. Moreover, let
G_{I}
denote the group
{\rm Inndiag}(G_{0})
of inner-diagonal automorphisms of
G_{0}
.

Our goal here is to prove Theorem
1
for classical groups. In particular, we will prove the following stronger statement:

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
G
be a finite almost simple classical group. Then either
R_{\rm ns}(G)\leqslant 5
with equality if and only if
G={\rm U}_{6}(2).2
. Moreover, one of the following holds:

(i)

\mathbb{P}(G,4)\rightarrow 1
as
|G|\rightarrow\infty
;

(ii)

G_{0}={\rm Sp}_{6}(q)
with
q
even or
G_{0}=\Omega_{7}(q)
with
q
odd and
\mathbb{P}(G,6)\rightarrow 1
as
|G|\rightarrow\infty
.

Remark 3.2
.

Let us record some comments on the statement of Theorem
3.1
.

(a)

We will prove that
(H,H,H,H)
is the only non-regular
4
-tuple of
{\rm U}_{6}(2).2
up to conjugacy, where
H={\rm U}_{4}(3).2^{2}
.

(b)

Theorem
3.1
is a direct regularity analogue of
[
18
, Theorem 1]
. In particular, Burness shows that
b(G,H)\leqslant 5
for every almost simple primitive classical group with non-standard point stabiliser
H
, with equality if and only if
(G,H)=({\rm U}_{6}(2).2,{\rm U}_{4}(3).2^{2})
.

(c)

Our methods will be similar to the ones used in
[
18
]
. However, recall from Section
1
that removing the conjugacy assumption on our tuples requires us to work with uniform worst case fixed point ratio bounds. This means that whilst we will heavily rely on the main theorem of
[
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
]
to obtain sufficient fixed point ratio estimates in the majority of the cases, there will be some cases, which we outline in Section
4
, where we will require stronger bounds. We obtain those bounds in Theorem
4.1
.

(d)

As we will explain in Section
3.3
,we will be heavily using a function
\eta_{G}(t)
, which is a variant of a ’zeta type function’ introduced by Liebeck and Shalev in
[
61
]
in the proof of Theorem
3.1
. We will heavily rely on results in
[
18
]
for this function, but as is the case with fixed point ratios, those results will not always be applicable in this more general setting, so we will need to refine those. We do this in Section
5
.

We now discuss some preliminary results around classical groups. In particular, we will briefly discuss Aschbacher’s theorem that describes the subgroup structure of classical groups and record some information on conjugacy classes of prime order elements. We will then proceed by giving a formal definition of a subspace subgroup, and we will finally provide some more information on probabilistic and computational methods that only apply to the classical groups.

3.1.
Subgroup structure

The subgroup structure of finite classical groups is described by Aschbacher in
[
4
]
, where he defines nine separate collections and proves that every maximal subgroup of a finite classical group is contained in one of those collections. Note that the situation is slightly different when
{\rm P\Omega}^{+}_{8}(q)
or
{\rm PSp}_{4}(2^{f})
and we discuss this below. The first eight collections, denoted by
\mathscr{C}_{1},\ldots,\mathscr{C}_{8}
are often referred to as the
geometric collections
, and the final collection, which we denote by
\mathscr{S}
is known as the
non-geometric collection
. The members of the geometric collections can be described in terms of the underlying geometry of
V
. For example, the members of
\mathscr{C}_{2}
are stabilisers of appropriate direct sum decompositions, and similarly, the members of
\mathscr{C}_{4}
and
\mathscr{C}_{7}
are stabilisers of appropriate tensor product decompositions. The
\mathscr{S}
-collection consists of subgroups that correspond to some absolutely irreducible representation on
V
of a covering group of
H\cap G_{0}
(see
[
47
, p.3]
for a precise definition). Note that the groups in
\mathcal{S}
are almost simple. Throughout this paper, we will adopt the definition of
[
47
]
for the collections of maximal subgroups, which differs slightly from the definition in the original paper by Aschbacher
[
4
]
. We give a brief description of each of the collections in Table
1
.

Let
H
be a maximal subgroup of
G
such that
H\not\in\mathscr{S}
and set
H_{0}=G_{0}\cap H
. If
H_{0}
is maximal, then
H_{0}\in\mathscr{C}
, where
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathscr{C}_{8}
. If
G_{0}={\rm PSp}_{4}(2^{f})
, or
{\rm P\Omega}_{8}^{+}(q)
, then the existence of exceptional automorphisms gives rise to an extra collection of so-called
novelty subgroups
. Following the notation in
[
22
]
, we will denote this class by
\mathscr{N}
. We give a description of all those subgroups in Table
2
, although the only cases of groups in
\mathcal{N}
that will be considered in this paper are the ones where
G_{0}={\rm PSp}_{4}(q)
for even
q
and
H
is of type
{\rm O}^{\epsilon}_{2}(q)\wr S_{2}
or
{\rm O}_{2}^{-}(q).2
. We note that Aschbacher’s original paper
[
4
]
only provides partial information for the case where
G_{0}={\rm P\Omega}^{+}_{8}(q)
and
G
contains triality graph automorphisms. In this case, the maximal subgroups were determined in
[
49
]
by Kleidman.

We will repeatedly refer to the
type
of a subgroup, following the terminology in
[
47
]
. If
H\in\mathscr{S}
, then the type of
H
will be the socle of the almost simple group
H
. On the other hand, if
H\in\mathscr{C}
, then the type of
H
will give an approximate group-theoretic description of
H
and will often indicate the underlying geometry associated to it. For example, if
G_{0}={\rm PSL}_{n}(q)
and
H
is of type
{\rm GL}_{k}(q)\wr S_{t}
, then
H
is a stabiliser of a direct sum decomposition
V=V_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus V_{t}
, where
{\rm dim}V_{i}=k
for all
i
.

Our standard reference for the existence, structure and maximality of subgroups in
\mathscr{C}
will be
[
47
]
, where Kleidman and Liebeck provide a complete description of the structure of geometric structures and determine maximality (up to conjugacy) for
n\geqslant 13
. For classical groups with dimension at most
12
our standard reference becomes
[
12
]
, where Bray, Holt and Roney-Dougal determine all the maximal subgroups (up to conjugacy) of the low-dimensional classical groups.

In view of the above discussion, we now provide a version of Aschbacher’s theorem on subgroup structure of classical groups:

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
G
be an almost simple classical group with socle
G_{0}
, and let
H
be a core-free maximal subgroup of
G
. Then
H\in\mathscr{C}\cup\mathscr{S}\cup\mathscr{N}
.

\begin{array}[]{ll}\hline\cr\mathscr{C}_{1}&\text{Stabilisers of subspaces or pairs of subspaces of }V\\
\mathscr{C}_{2}&\text{Stabilisers of direct sum decompositions }V=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{t}V_{i}\text{ with }{\rm dim}V_{i}=k\\
\mathscr{C}_{3}&\text{Stabilisers of prime degree extension fields of }\mathbb{F}_{q}\\
\mathscr{C}_{4}&\text{Stabilisers of tensor product decompositions }V=V_{1}\otimes V_{2}\\
\mathscr{C}_{5}&\text{Stabilisers of prime index subfields of }\mathbb{F}_{q}\\
\mathscr{C}_{6}&\text{Normalisers of symplectic type }r\text{-groups, where }r\neq p\\
\mathscr{C}_{7}&\text{Stabilisers of tensor product decompositions }V=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{t}V_{i}\text{ with }{\rm dim}V_{i}=k\\
\mathscr{C}_{8}&\text{Stabilisers of nondegenerate forms on }V\\
\mathscr{S}&\text{Almost simple absolutely irreducible subgroups}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 1.
The Aschbacher collections.

\begin{array}[]{lll}\hline\cr G_{0}&\text{Type of }H&\text{Conditions}\\
\hline\cr{\rm PSp}_{4}(q)&{\rm O}_{2}^{\epsilon}(q)\wr S_{2}&q\text{ even}\\
&{\rm O}_{2}^{-}(q^{2}).2&q\text{ even}\\
&[q^{4}].{\rm GL}_{1}(q)^{2}&q\text{ even}\\
{\rm P\Omega}^{+}_{8}(q)&{\rm GL}_{1}^{\epsilon}(q)\times{\rm GL}_{3}^{\epsilon}(q)\\
&{\rm O}^{-}_{2}(q^{2})\times{\rm O}^{-}_{2}(q^{2})\\
&G_{2}(q)\\
&[2^{9}].{\rm SL}_{3}(2)&q=p>2\\
&[q^{11}]{\rm GL}_{2}(q){\rm GL}_{1}(q)^{2}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 2.
The
\mathscr{N}
-collection.

In this paper we are interested in non-standard subgroup tuples of
G
. In the classical setting, a core-free subgroup of
G
is non-standard if and only if it is not a subspace subgroup. We begin by giving a precise definition of a subspace subgroup.

Definition 3.4
.

A core-free maximal subgroup
M
of
G
not containing
G_{0}
is a
subspace subgroup
if one of the following holds:

(i)

M\in\mathscr{C}_{1}
;

(ii)

(G_{0},p)=({\rm Sp}_{2m}(q)^{\prime},2)
and
M\in\mathscr{C}_{8}
;

(iii)

(G,M)
is displayed in Table
3
.

In Table
3
we list all the cases not covered by (i) and (ii) in Definition
3.4
. Those are all instances where there exists some isomorphism
\phi:G\rightarrow K
where
{\rm soc}(K)=T\cong G_{0}
and one of the following holds:

(a)

\phi(M)=L
is a subspace subgroup of Type (i) or (ii) from Definition
3.4
;

(b)

T=A_{n}
and
L
does not act primitively on
\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

In the first column of Table
3
we list
G_{0}
, in the second column the type of
M
, in the third column we list the pairs with entries
T
and the type of
L
, and finally, in the fourth column we mention the conditions under which such an isomorphism occurs, if any. We claim that Table
3
indeed covers all relevant cases. First suppose that
\phi
is an automorphism. Noting that inner, diagonal, and field automorphisms do not permute the Aschbacher families, we deduce that
\phi
must be a graph automorphism. Moreover, we must have
G_{0}\in\{{\rm P\Omega}_{8}^{+}(q),{\rm PSp}_{4}(q)^{\prime}\}
, as graph automorphisms of linear and unitary groups again preserve Aschbacher families. In those two cases, we verify that
\phi(M)
is as claimed in Table
3.4
by inspecting
[
12
, Tables 8.14, 8.50]
. If
\phi
is not an automorphism, then the possible isomorphisms of classical groups are listed in
[
47
, 2.9.1]
, and by inspecting the Tables in
[
12
, Chapter 8]
and comparing orders of maximal subgroups, we verify that Table
3
is complete.

\begin{array}[]{llll}\hline\cr G_{0}&\text{Type of }M&(T,\text{Type of }L)&\text{Conditions}\\
\hline\cr{\rm P\Omega}_{8}^{+}(q)&\Omega_{7}(q)&({\rm P\Omega}_{8}^{+}(q),{\rm O}_{7}(q)\perp{\rm O}_{1}(q))&M\,\text{irreducible},q\,\text{odd}\\
&{\rm Sp}_{6}(q)&({\rm P\Omega}_{8}^{+}(q),{\rm Sp}_{6}(q))&M\,\text{irreducible},q\,\text{even}\\
&{\rm GL}_{4}(q)&({\rm P\Omega}_{8}^{+}(q),{\rm O}_{2}^{+}(q)\perp{\rm O}_{6}^{+}(q))\\
&{\rm GU}_{4}(q)&({\rm P\Omega}_{8}^{+}(q),{\rm O}_{2}^{-}(q)\perp{\rm O}_{6}^{-}(q))\\
&{\rm Sp}_{2}(q)\otimes{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)&({\rm P\Omega}_{8}^{+}(q),{\rm O}_{3}(q)\perp{\rm O}_{5}(q))&q\,\text{odd}\\
{\rm PSL}^{\epsilon}_{4}(q)&{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)&({\rm P\Omega}_{6}^{\epsilon}(q),{\rm O}_{5}(q)\perp{\rm O}_{1}(q))&\\
&{\rm GL}_{2}^{\epsilon}(q)\wr S_{2}&({\rm P\Omega}_{6}^{\epsilon}(q),{\rm O}_{4}^{+}(q)\perp{\rm O}^{\epsilon}_{2}(q))&q\geqslant 3\text{ and }q\geqslant 4\text{ if }\epsilon=+\\
&{\rm GL}_{2}^{\epsilon}(q^{2})&({\rm P\Omega}_{6}^{\epsilon}(q),{\rm O}_{4}^{-}(q)\perp{\rm O}^{\epsilon}_{2}(q))&q\geqslant 4\text{ if }\epsilon=-\\
{\rm PSL}_{4}(q)&{\rm GL}_{2}(q^{2})&(A_{8},(S_{3}\times S_{5})\cap A_{8})&q=2\\
&A_{7}&(A_{8},(S_{7}\times S_{1})\cap A_{8})&q=2\\
{\rm PSp}_{4}(q)^{\prime}&{\rm Sp}_{2}(q)\wr S_{2}&({\rm Sp}_{4}(q),{\rm O}_{4}^{+}(q))&q\,\text{even}\\
&{\rm Sp}_{2}(q^{2})&({\rm Sp}_{4}(q),{\rm O}_{4}^{-}(q))&q\,\text{even}\\
&{\rm Sp}_{2}(q)\wr S_{2}&(\Omega_{5}(q),{\rm O}_{4}^{+}(q)\perp{\rm O}_{1}(q))&q\,\text{odd}\\
&{\rm Sp}_{2}(q^{2})&(\Omega_{5}(q),{\rm O}_{4}^{-}(q)\perp{\rm O}_{1}(q))&q\,\text{odd}\\
&{\rm GL}_{2}(q)&(\Omega_{5}(q),({\rm O}_{3}(q)\perp{\rm O}_{2}^{+}(q))&q\,\text{odd}\\
&{\rm GU}_{2}(q)&(\Omega_{5}(q),({\rm O}_{3}(q)\perp{\rm O}_{2}^{-}(q))&q\,\text{odd}\\
{\rm PSL}_{2}(q)&{\rm GL}_{2}(q_{0})&(\Omega^{-}_{4}(q_{0}),{\rm O}_{3}(q_{0})\perp{\rm O}_{1}(q_{0}))&q=q_{0}^{2},q_{0}\neq 2\\
&2^{2}.{\rm O}_{2}^{-}(2)&(A_{5},(S_{4}\times S_{1})\cap A_{6})&q=5\\
&A_{5}&(A_{6},(S_{5}\times S_{1})\cap A_{5})&q=9\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 3.
The standard subgroups of classical groups arising in part (iii) of Definition
3.4
.

3.2.
Conjugacy classes

In this section we will give a brief description of the conjugacy classes of prime order elements in classical groups, and we will establish the notation and terminology that we will be using throughout. We refer the reader to
[
22
, Chapter 3]
for a more detailed description of those classes and to
[
22
, Chapter 2]
for a more detailed analysis of the basic properties of the classical groups, as well as their diagonal, field, graph, and graph-field automorphisms. We note that due to a theorem of Steinberg
[
73
, Theorem 30]
, every element in an almost simple classical group is a product of an inner, a diagonal, a field, and a graph automorphism.

First let
x\in G_{I}
be an element of prime order
r
. We will say that
x
is
unipotent
if
r=p
and that
x
is
semisimple
if
r\neq p
. The description of
x
will differ depending on whether it is unipotent or semisimple, so we divide the analysis into two separate sections.

Semisimple elements

Here we will assume that
x
is semisimple. We start by stating a fundamental theorem (
[
44
, 4.2.2.(j)]
) that we will repeatedly use throughout this paper:

Proposition 3.5
.

If
T
is a finite simple group of Lie type and let
x\in T
be a semisimple element. Then
x^{T}=x^{{\rm Inndiag}(T)}
.

Note that this theorem applies to all finite simple groups, and we will in fact make use of it when dealing with exceptional groups.

The analysis slightly differs when
x
is an involution, so we will first discuss the structure of semisimple elements of odd order and then proceed to briefly comment on involutions. More information on all this can be found in
[
22
, Chapter 3]
.

First suppose that
x
has odd order
r
and let
i\geqslant 1
be minimal such that
r
divides
q^{i}-1
. As in
[
22
]
, we will write

c=\begin{cases}2i\text{ if }i\text{ is odd and }G_{0}\neq{\rm L}_{n}(q)\\
i/2\text{ if }i\equiv 2\allowbreak\mkern 4.0mu({\operator@font mod}\,\,4)\text{ and }G_{0}={\rm U}_{n}(q)\\
i\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

First assume that
c\geqslant 2
. Then
[
22
, 3.1.3]
implies that
x
lifts to a unique element
\hat{x}
of order
r
in the corresponding matrix group. That is,
x=\hat{x}Z
, where
Z
is the centre of this matrix group. We will now focus on the linear case, that is
G_{0}={\rm L}_{n}(q)
, to describe the structure of prime order classes, but one can get similar descriptions with minor modifications for the unitary, symplectic, and orthogonal groups.

Now note that
\hat{x}
is diagonalisable in
\mathbb{F}_{q^{i}}
, but not any proper subfield, so Maschke’s Theorem implies that
\hat{x}
fixes a direct sum decomposition

V=U_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus U_{s}\oplus C_{V}(\hat{x})

of the natural
{\rm GL}_{n}(q)
-module
V
, where

C_{V}(\hat{x})=\{v\in V\,:\,v\hat{x}=v\}

is the
1
-eigenspace of
\hat{x}
and
U_{j}
is an
j
-dimensional subspace of
V
, on which
\hat{x}
acts irreducibly.

Now let
\mathscr{S}_{r}
denote the set of non-trivial
r
-th roots of unity in
\mathbb{F}_{q^{i}}
, and observe that the standard Frobenius automorphism
\sigma:\mathbb{F}_{q^{i}}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}_{q^{i}}
defined by
\lambda\mapsto\lambda^{q}
induces a permutation on
\mathscr{S}_{r}
. In particular, all
\sigma
-orbits have length
i
, so there are precisely
t=(r-1)/i
distinct
\sigma
-orbits. We will write
\Lambda_{1},\ldots,\Lambda_{t}
to denote those
\sigma
-orbits, where

\Lambda_{j}=\{\lambda_{j},\lambda_{j}^{q},\ldots,\lambda_{j}^{q^{i-1}}\}

for some
\lambda_{j}\in\mathscr{S}_{r}
. We now note that the set of eigenvalues in
U_{j}\otimes\mathbb{F}_{q^{i}}
coincides with some
\sigma
-orbit, so we may abuse notation and write

\hat{x}=[\Lambda_{1}^{a_{1}},\Lambda_{2}^{a_{2}},\ldots,\Lambda_{t}^{a_{t}},I_{e}],

where
a_{j}
denotes the multiplicity of
\Lambda_{j}
on the multiset of eigenvalues of
\hat{x}
on
V\otimes\mathbb{F}_{q^{i}}
and
e={\rm dim}C_{V}(\hat{x})
. It now follows from
[
22
, 3.1.7]
that two semisimple elements of odd order
r
are
{\rm GL}_{n}(q)
-conjugate if and only if they have the same multiset of eigenvalues on
\mathbb{F}_{q^{i}}
, and in particular, since every
x\in{\rm PGL}_{n}(q)
of order
r
has a unique lift
\hat{x}
of order
r
in
{\rm GL}_{n}(q)
, we have a complete description of the prime order semisimple classes with
c\geqslant 2
in
{\rm PGL}_{n}(q)
. Moreover, we have a description of
C_{{\rm GL}_{n}(q)}(\hat{x})
. In particular,
[
22
, 3.1.9]
gives

C_{{\rm GL}_{n}(q)}(\hat{x})={\rm GL}_{e}(q)\times\prod_{j=1}^{t}{\rm GL}_{a_{j}}(q^{i})

With minor modifications we can get similar descriptions for odd order semisimple elements in all classical groups. For example if
G_{0}={\rm PSp}_{n}(q)
, then the main difference is that when
i
is odd, then the
\sigma
-orbits come in inverse pairs
(\Lambda,\Lambda^{-1})
, and so every element of order
r
is of the form
[(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{1}^{-1})^{a_{1}},\ldots,(\Lambda_{t},\Lambda_{t}^{-1})^{a_{t}},I_{e}]
. And again, two elements of order
r
are
{\rm Sp}_{n}(q)
-conjugate if and only if they have the same eigenvalues in
\mathbb{F}_{q^{i}}
, so we get a similar description for odd order semisimple classes. We also get a similar description of the centraliser structure of
\hat{x}
. In particular, if
\widehat{G}
denotes the lift of
G_{I}
in
{\rm GL}_{n}(q)
(or
{\rm GL}_{n}(q^{2})
if
G
is unitary), then
[
22
, Remark 3.3.4, Remark 3.4.4, Remark 3.5.6]
give

C_{\widehat{G}}(\hat{x})=\widehat{G}\cap C_{{\rm GL}_{n}(q^{\delta})}(\hat{x})

where
\delta=2
if
G
is unitary and
\delta=1
otherwise. This is all covered in detail in
[
22
, Chapter 3]
. Note that we will often refer to the
type
of
C_{G}(x)
, which will always mean
C_{\widehat{G}}(\hat{x})
.

Finally, if
c=1
, then we still write
x=\hat{x}Z
, but in this case the lift in
\hat{x}
is not unique, so additional conjugacy classes arise. A precise analysis of conjugacy in this case is given in
[
22
, 3.2.2, 3.3.3]
and the structure of
C_{G_{I}}(x)
is recorded in
[
22
, Tables B.3 and B.4]
.

We now briefly comment on the case where
G_{0}={\rm PSL}_{n}(q)
and
H
is a
\mathscr{C}_{3}
-subgroup of
G
, as we will often come across this situation in this paper. Set
L={\rm GL}_{n/k}(q^{k})
, let
W
be the natural
L
-module over
\mathbb{F}_{p^{k}}
and consider the natural embedding of
L
in
{\rm GL}_{n}(q)
with respect to a fixed
\mathbb{F}_{q^{k}}
-basis
\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{n/k}\}
for
W
. We refer the reader to
[
22
, Construction 2.1.4]
for more information. Now set
i_{0}
to be the smallest positive integer such that
r
divides
q^{ki_{0}}-1
, and note that

i_{0}=\begin{cases}i/k\text{ if }k\text{ divides }i\\
i\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

If
\Gamma_{1},\ldots,\Gamma_{s}
denote the distinct
\sigma_{0}
-orbits on
\mathscr{S}_{r}
, where
\sigma_{0}
is the permutation defined by
\lambda\mapsto\lambda^{q^{k}}
for all
\lambda\in\mathscr{S}_{r}
, then either each
\sigma
-orbit is a union of
k
distinct
\sigma_{0}
-orbits, or
i=i_{0}
and
\sigma
and
\sigma_{0}
-orbits coincide. In particular,
[
22
, 5.3.2]
gives the following description of the embedding of
\hat{x}\in L
inside
{\rm GL}_{n}(q)
:

Lemma 3.6
.

If
\hat{x}=[\Gamma_{1},\ldots\Gamma_{s},I_{e}]\in L
, then, up to conjugacy,

\hat{x}=[\Gamma_{1}^{a_{1}},(\Gamma_{1}^{q})^{a_{1}},\ldots,(\Gamma_{1}^{q^{k-1}})^{a_{1}},\ldots,\Gamma_{s}^{a_{s}},(\Gamma_{s}^{q})^{a_{s}},\ldots,(\Gamma_{s}^{q^{k-1}})^{a_{s}},I_{ke}]

on
V
. In particular, if
i=i_{0}
, then
\hat{x}
is conjugate to
[\Gamma_{1}^{ka_{1}},\ldots,\Gamma_{s}^{ka_{s}},I_{ke}]
.

We finish this section by very briefly commenting on the semisimple involutions in
{\rm PGL}_{n}(q)
and we refer the reader to
[
22
, Sections 3.2.2, 3.3.2, 3.4.2, 3.5.2]
for a detailed analysis of semisimple involutions in all classical almost simple groups of Lie type. So now let
G_{0}={\rm PGL}_{n}(q)
and take
x
to be a semisimple involution. For every
i
such that
1\leqslant i\leqslant n/2
we get a unique class of involutions with representative
t_{i}
, where
t_{i}=\hat{t}Z
and
\hat{t}
is an involution in
{\rm GL}_{n}(q)
whose
(-1)
-eigenspace has dimension
i
. If
x\in t_{i}^{G}
, then we will say that it is a
t_{i}
-
involution
. Finally, if
n
is even, then we get an additional class of involutions with representative
t^{\prime}_{n/2}
. This class of involutions arises from the quadratic field extension
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}/\mathbb{F}_{q}
and we direct the reader to
[
22
, 3.2.2.2]
for more details. The precise structure of
C_{G_{I}}(x)
is recorded in
[
22
, Table B.1]
.

Unipotent elements

We now shift our attention to unipotent elements in finite classical groups. Let
x\in G
be an element of order
p
. Then
[
22
, 3.1.3]
implies that
x
again lifts to a unique element
\hat{x}
of order
p
in the corresponding matrix group, so that
x=\hat{x}Z
, where
Z
again denotes the centre of this matrix group. We may now identify
\hat{x}
with its Jordan decomposition on
V
and write

\hat{x}=[J_{p}^{a_{p}},\ldots,J_{1}^{a_{1}}],

where each
J_{i}
is a unipotent Jordan block of size
i
and
a_{i}
denotes the multiplicity of
J_{i}
in
\hat{x}
. Note that if
G
is symplectic or orthogonal, then there are restrictions on the multiplicities of odd and even Jordan blocks respectively. In particular, combining
[
22
, 3.4.1, 3.5.1]
we get:

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\hat{x}\in{\rm Sp}_{n}(q)
(respectively
{\rm O}^{\epsilon}_{n}(q)
) be an element of odd order
p
with Jordan decomposition
[J_{p}^{a_{p}},\ldots,J_{1}^{a_{1}}]
on
V
. Then
a_{i}
is even for all odd (respectively even)
i
.

Now
[
22
, 3.1.14]
implies that two elements in
{\rm GL}_{n}(q)
are conjugate if and only if they have the same Jordan decomposition on
V
, and the uniqueness of the lift
\hat{x}
implies that we again have a complete description of unipotent classes in
{\rm PGL}_{n}(q)
. There are similar descriptions in the unitary, symplectic, and orthogonal cases and we direct the reader to
[
22
, Sections 3.2.3, 3.3.3, 3.4.3, 3.4.4, 3.5.3, 3.5.4]
for more information.

The main difference in the symplectic and orthogonal case worth highlighting, is when
p=2
, in which case all unipotent elements have Jordan decomposition
[J_{2}^{s},J_{1}^{n-2s}]
and there are at least two
G_{0}
-classes of elements with this decomposition when
s
is even.

Finally, we will often be interested in the number and size of
G_{0}
-classes of unipotent elements, so we will often appeal to lemmas in
[
22
]
that determine in how many
G_{0}
-classes a given
G_{I}
-class splits. For example, if
G_{0}={\rm PGL}_{n}^{\epsilon}(q)
, then
[
22
, 3.2.7, 3.3.10]
tell us that the
G_{I}
-class of an element
x\in G_{I}
of order
p
with Jordan decomposition
[J_{p}^{a_{p}},\ldots,J_{1}^{a_{1}}]
splits into
(\nu,q-\epsilon)
distinct
G_{0}
-classes, where
\nu=\gcd\{j\,:\,a_{j}>0\}
. Similar results are given in
[
22
, 3.4.12, 3.5.14]
for symplectic and orthogonal groups.

Finally, if
x
is a field, graph, or graph-field automorphism, then detailed information can be found in
[
22
, Chapter 3]
and the structure of
C_{G_{I}}(x)
is recorded in the relevant tables of
[
22
, Appendix B]
. We will again be particularly interested in the number of
G_{0}
-classes each
G_{0}
-class splits into and we will often refer to the relevant lemmas in
[
22
]
. For example, in the linear case, we will repeatedly refer to
[
22
, 3.2.9, 3.2.14, 3.2.15]
.

We record
[
16
, 3.50]
that will be useful to us when dealing with field, graph and graph-field automorphisms.

Lemma 3.8
.

Suppose that
G_{0}\neq{\rm P\Omega}^{+}_{8}(q)
, let
H
be a subgroup of
G
and suppose that
x\in H\setminus{\rm PGL}(V)
has prime order. Then
x^{G}\cap H\subseteq\widetilde{H}x
, where
\widetilde{H}=H\cap G_{I}
.

3.3.
Probabilistic methods

The proof of Theorem
3.1
will heavily rely on fixed point ratio bounds established in a series of papers by Burness
[
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
]
. We now state the main result of those papers, which is Theorem 1 in
[
14
]
.

Theorem 3.9
.

Let
G
be a finite almost simple classical group over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
and let
H
be a non-standard subgroup of
G
. Then

\mbox{{\rm fpr}}(x,G/H)<|x^{G}|^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{n}+\iota(G,H)}

for all
x\in G
of prime order, where
\iota(G,H)\geqslant 0
is a known constant.

Note that in most cases
\iota(G,H)=0
. The precise value of
\iota(G,H)
when
\iota(G,H)>0
is listed in
[
14
, Table 1]
. Note that in most cases the term
n
appearing in Theorem
3.9
is the dimension of the natural module, unless
G_{0}={\rm Sp}_{4}(2)^{\prime}
or
{\rm L}_{3}(2)
, in which case
n=2
(since
{\rm Sp}_{4}(2)^{\prime}\cong{\rm L}_{2}(2)
and
{\rm L}_{3}(2)\cong{\rm L}_{2}(7)
). We will refer to
n
as the
dimension
of
G
.

Following
[
61
]
, we now define an ‘zeta-type function’ associated to a finite group
G
, which will be a key tool in the proof of our main theorem for high-dimensional classical groups.

Definition 3.10
.

Let
G
be a finite almost simple group with socle
G_{0}
, and let
\mathscr{C}
be the set of
G_{0}
-conjugacy classes of prime order elements in
G
. For
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we define:

\eta_{G}(t)=\sum_{C\in\mathscr{C}}|C|^{-t}

Evidently, there exists a real number
T_{G}\in(0,1)
such that
\eta_{G}(T_{G})=1
.

Results on the asymptotic behaviour of
\eta_{G}(t)
can be extracted from
[
61
, 1.10]
. Note that the function considered in
[
61
]
is rather different to
\eta_{G}(t)
. In particular, Liebeck and Shalev sum over all
G
-conjugacy classes, whilst we are summing over all prime order
G_{0}
-classes. However, as we explain below, it is still possible to extract asymptotic results for our purposes:

Lemma 3.11
.

We have
\sum_{i=1}^{r}|x_{i}^{G}|^{-t}\leqslant\eta_{G}(t)
for any almost simple group
G
with socle
G_{0}
and
t\in(0,1)
, where
x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}
is a set of representatives of
G
-conjugacy classes of prime order elements in
G
.

Proof.

Let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\in G
be a set of representatives of the prime order
G_{0}
-classes, and let
c_{i}
be the number of
G_{0}
-classes in which
x_{i}^{G}
splits into. Then

\sum_{i=1}^{r}|x_{i}^{G}|^{-t}\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{r}c_{i}\left(\frac{|x_{i}^{G}|}{c_{i}}\right)^{-t}=\sum_{j=1}^{s}|x_{j}^{G_{0}}|^{-t}=\eta_{G}(t)

as required.
∎

Remark 3.12
.

Note that the proof of Lemma
3.11
does not rely at all on the assumption that the elements we are summing over have prime order. In fact, by carefully inspecting
[
61
, 1.10]
, we note that Liebeck and Shalev use the exact same observation in their proof and work in terms of
G_{0}
-classes. This allows us to extract the following asymptotic result:

Proposition 3.13
.

Let
G=G(q)
be an almost simple group with socle
G_{0}
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Then the following hold:

(i)

\eta_{G}(t)\rightarrow 0
as
q\rightarrow\infty
for any
t>T_{G}
.

(ii)

For a fixed real number
t>0
, there exists some integer
r(t)
, such that for all almost simple groups
G=G(q)
with rank
r\geqslant r(t)
we have

\eta_{G}(t)\rightarrow 0\text{ as }|G|\rightarrow\infty.

Proof.

We will prove (i). The argument for (ii) is identical. If
\mathscr{S}
denotes the set of
G_{0}
-conjugacy classes in
G
, then inspecting
[
61
, 1.10]
gives

\zeta_{G}(t):=\sum_{C\in\mathscr{S}}|C|^{-t}\rightarrow 1\,\,\text{ as }\,\,\rightarrow\infty

and noting that
\eta_{G}(t)<\zeta_{G}(t)-1
, the result follows.
∎

We now prove a variant of
[
18
, 2.2]
, which will allow us to use
\eta_{G}
to establish bounds on
R_{\rm ns}(G)
for a finite classical almost simple group
G
.

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
G
be a finite almost simple classical group and let
\tau=(H_{1},\ldots,H_{k})
be a core-free tuple. Suppose that the following hold:

(i)

{\rm fpr}(x,G/H_{i})<|x^{G_{0}}|^{-m}
for some some
m>0
for all
x\in G
of prime order;

(ii)

T_{G}<km-1
.

Then
\tau
is regular.

Proof.

In view of Lemma
2.2
, it suffices to show that
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)<1
. If
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
are representatives of the
G_{0}
-conjugacy classes of prime order elements in
G
, then Lemma
2.2
implies that

\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{k}|x_{i}^{G_{0}}|^{-km+1}=\eta_{G}(km-1).

Finally, noting that
\eta_{G}(t)<1
for all
t>T_{G}
, the claim follows.
∎

We now record some bounds on
T_{G}
established in
[
18
, Proposition 2.2, Remark 2.3]
that will be key to proving the main result for almost simple classical groups of large dimension.

Proposition 3.15
.

Let
G
be a finite almost simple classical group of rank
n
with socle
G_{0}
. If
n\geqslant 6
, then
T_{G}<1/3
. Moreover, if
G_{0}={\rm PSL}_{10}^{\epsilon}(q)
, then
T_{G}<1/5
and if
G_{0}={\rm P\Omega}_{n}^{\epsilon}(q)
and
n\geqslant 12
, then
T_{G}<4/15
.

Remark 3.16
.

Burness again works with a slightly different function than
\eta_{G}(t)
. In particular, he sums over
G
-classes of prime order elements instead of
G_{0}
-classes. However, by closely inspecting his proofs, we note that he also makes use of the observation in Lemma
3.11
, and so the bounds in
[
18
, Proposition 2.2, Remark 2.3]
still apply in our setting.

We now record a result on fixed point ratios of almost simple classical groups, which is an immediate consequence of
[
55
, Theorem 1]
. Note that the original result concerns all the almost simple classical groups, but we will only need the result for groups with socle
{\rm PSL}_{2}(q)
.

Proposition 3.17
.

Let
G
be an almost simple group with socle
{\rm PSL}_{2}(q)
, where
q=p^{f}
,
H
be a core-free subgroup of
G
, and
x\in G
an element of prime order
r
. Then

{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<\frac{4}{3q}

or
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<\alpha
and
(H,x,\alpha)
is recorded in Table
4
.

\begin{array}[]{llll}\hline\cr\text{Type of }H&x&\alpha&\text{Conditions}\\
\hline\cr{\rm GL}_{1}(q)\wr S_{2}&\phi&2/(q+1)&r=2\\
{\rm GL}_{2}(q^{1/2})&u&2/(q+1)&p\text{ odd}\\
&s&2/(q+1)&r=2,p\text{ odd},q\text{ a square}\\
&\phi&(2+q^{1/2}(q^{1/2}+1))/(q^{1/2}(q+1))&r=2,p\text{ odd}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 4.
The exceptional cases in Proposition
3.17
.

In view of Lemma
2.4
, Lemma
3.11
and Remark
2.5
, we will often need to refer to results in the literature on lower bounds on
|x^{G}_{0}|
. In particular, if
G_{0}
is in the following collection:

\mathcal{A}=\{{\rm PSL}^{\epsilon}_{3}(q),{\rm PSL}_{4}^{\epsilon}(q),{\rm PSL}_{5}^{\epsilon}(q),{\rm PSp}_{4}(q)\}

then we will repeatedly appeal to results in
[
15
]
on conjugacy class bounds. In the following lemma we record the bounds that are of interest to us.

Lemma 3.18
.

Suppose that
G_{0}\in\mathcal{A}
and
x\in G
is an element of prime order
r
. Then
|x^{G_{0}}|>\alpha
, where
\alpha
is recorded in Table
5
and
\beta=\frac{q}{q+1}
.

Proof.

Sufficient upper bounds on
|x^{G_{I}}|
(or
|x^{G_{0}}|
if
x
is a field, graph, or graph-field automorphism) are established in
[
16
, Sections 3.3-3.5]
. Combining those with
[
22
, 3.2.8, 3.2.9, 3.2.14, 3.2.15, 3.3.10, 3.3.12, 3.3.17, 3.4.12, 3.4.15, 3.4.16, 3.5.20]
, which describe when prime order
G_{I}
-classes split into
G_{0}
-classes, we verify the entries in Table
5
. The argument is similar in most cases, so we only treat a few cases that are slightly more involved here.

First assume that
G_{0}={\rm PSL}_{3}^{\epsilon}(q)
and
x\in G_{I}
. Note that
[
22
, 3.2.8, 3.3.10]
imply that
|x^{G_{0}}|=|x^{G_{I}}|
, unless
x
is unipotent with Jordan decomposition
[J_{3}]
, in which case
|x^{G_{0}}|\geqslant\frac{1}{3}|x^{G_{I}}|
. Therefore, if
x
is unipotent, then it follows from
[
16
, 3.22]
that
|x^{G_{0}}|>\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{4}
. Moreover, if
s
denotes the codimension of the largest eigenspace of the lift
\hat{x}
of
x
in
{\rm GL}(V)
, then we note that
s=2
unless
x
has Jordan form
[J_{2},J_{1}]
, and so with this exception we obtain the better bound

|x^{G_{0}}|>\frac{1}{6}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{6}.

Next suppose that
x
is semisimple. If
C_{\bar{G_{I}}}(x)
is connected, where
\bar{G_{I}}
denotes the ambient algebraic group of
G
, then the bounds in Table
5
are verified via
[
16
, 3.36]
. On the other hand, if
C_{\bar{G_{I}}}(x)
is non-connected, then
[
15
, 3.35]
implies that
r
divides
3
, and so
r=3
, and moreover

|x^{G_{0}}|>\frac{1}{6}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)^{2}q^{6},

as claimed.

Now assume that
x
is a field or graph-field automorphism. If
r=2
, then

|x^{G_{0}}|\geqslant\frac{|{\rm PSL}_{3}^{\epsilon}(q)|}{|{\rm PGU}_{3}(q^{1/2})|}>\frac{1}{2(3,q-\epsilon)}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{(3^{2}-1)/2}>\frac{1}{6}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{4}.

Similarly, if
r\geqslant 3
, then we obtain
|x^{G_{0}}|>\frac{1}{6}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{16/3}
.

Now assume that
G_{0}={\rm PSp}_{4}(q)
and
x\in G_{I}
. Appealing to
[
16
, 3.21]
we find that
|x^{G_{0}}|>\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{4}
if
x
is unipotent, and in particular
|x^{G_{0}}|>\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{6}
if
x
does not have Jordan decomposition
[J_{2},J_{1}^{2}]
on
V
.

Similarly, if
x
is semisimple and
s
denotes the codimension of the largest eigenspace of the lift
\hat{x}
of
x
on
{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)
, then we note that
s>1
, and so
[
16
, 3.36-3.37]
immediately gives us

|x^{G_{0}}|=|x^{G_{I}}|>\frac{1}{4}q^{4}

and unless
x
is a
t_{1}
or
t_{1}^{\prime}
involution, then we obtain the better bound
|x^{G_{0}}|>\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{6}
via
[
16
, 3.30, 3.37]
.

We obtain all other bounds in Table
5
in a similar fashion.
∎

\begin{array}[]{llll}\hline\cr G_{0}&x&\alpha&\text{Conditions}\\
\hline\cr{\rm PSL}_{3}(q)&s&\frac{1}{2}\beta q^{4}&C_{G}(x)\text{ of type }{\rm GL}_{2}^{\epsilon}(q)\times{\rm GL}_{1}^{\epsilon}(q)\\
&&\frac{1}{6}\beta^{2}q^{6}&C_{G}(x)\text{ not of type }{\rm GL}_{2}^{\epsilon}(q)\times{\rm GL}_{1}^{\epsilon}(q)\\
&u&\frac{1}{2}\beta q^{4}&x=[J_{2},J_{1}]\\
&&\frac{1}{6}\beta q^{6}&x=[J_{2},J_{1}]\\
&\phi&\frac{1}{6}\beta q^{16/3}&r\geqslant 3\\
&\phi,\tau,\phi\tau&\frac{1}{6}\beta q^{4}&r=2\\
{\rm PSL}_{4}(q)&s&\frac{1}{2}\beta q^{6}&C_{G}(x)\text{ of type }{\rm GL}_{3}^{\epsilon}(q)\times{\rm GL}_{1}^{\epsilon}(q)\\
&&\frac{1}{4}\beta q^{8}&C_{G}(x)\text{ of type }{\rm GL}_{2}^{\epsilon}(q)^{2}\text{ or }{\rm GL}_{2}^{\epsilon}(q^{2})\\
&&\frac{1}{2}\beta^{2}q^{10}&C_{G}(x)\text{ not of type }{\rm GL}_{2}^{\epsilon}(q^{2})\text{ or }{\rm GL}^{\epsilon}_{s}(q)\times{\rm GL}_{4-s}^{\epsilon}(q)\text{ for }s\in\{2,3\}\\
&u&\frac{1}{2}\beta q^{6}&x=[J_{2},J_{1}^{2}]\\
&&\frac{1}{4}\beta q^{8}&x=[J_{2}^{2}]\\
&&\frac{1}{2}\beta q^{10}&x\neq[J_{2},J_{1}^{2}],[J_{2}^{2}]\\
&\phi,\phi\tau&\frac{1}{8}\beta q^{15/2}&\\
&\tau&\frac{1}{4}\beta q^{4}\\
{\rm PSL}_{5}^{\epsilon}(q)&s&\frac{1}{2}\beta q^{8}&C_{G}(x)\text{ of type }{\rm GL}_{4}^{\epsilon}(q)\times{\rm GL}_{1}^{\epsilon}(q)\\
&&\frac{1}{2}\beta q^{12}&C_{G}(x)\text{ of type }{\rm GL}_{3}^{\epsilon}(q)\times{\rm GL}_{2}^{\epsilon}(q)\\
&&\frac{1}{2}\beta^{2}q^{14}&C_{G}(x)\text{ not of type }{\rm GL}_{s}^{\epsilon}(q)\times{\rm GL}_{5-s}^{\epsilon}(q)\text{ for }s\in\{3,4\}\\
&u&\frac{1}{2}\beta q^{8}&x=[J_{2},J_{1}^{3}]\\
&&\frac{1}{2}\beta q^{12}&x=[J_{2}^{2},J_{1}]\\
&&\frac{1}{2}\beta q^{14}&x\neq[J_{2},J_{1}^{3}],[J_{2}^{2},J_{1}]\\
&\phi,\phi\tau&\frac{1}{10}\beta q^{12}&\\
&\tau&\frac{1}{10}\beta q^{13}\\
{\rm PSp}_{4}(q)&s&\frac{1}{4}q^{4}&x\text{ is a }t_{1}\text{ or }t^{\prime}_{1}\text{ involution}\\
&&\frac{1}{2}\beta q^{6}&x\text{ is not a }t_{1}\text{ or }t^{\prime}_{1}\text{involution}\\
&u&\frac{1}{4}\beta q^{4}&x=[J_{2},J_{1}^{2}]\\
&&\frac{1}{4}\beta q^{6}&x=[J_{2},J_{1}^{2}]\\
&\phi&\frac{1}{4}\beta q^{20/3}&r\geqslant 3\\
&\phi,\phi\tau&\frac{1}{4}q^{5}&r=2\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 5.
The bounds from Lemma
3.18
.

4.
Fixed point ratios for classical groups

In this section we improve the fixed point ratio bounds obtained in
[
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
]
for some low-dimensional classical groups. The goal here is to obtain sharper bounds that will allow us to use the
\eta
function machinery discussed in Definition
3.10
and Propositions
3.13
and
3.14
in some low-dimensional cases, where a more direct approach was taken in
[
18
]
to derive results on base sizes.

Recall that
G
is a finite almost simple classical group with socle
G_{0}
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, where
q=p^{f}
for some prime
p
. Moreover,
V
denotes the natural module of
G_{0}
and we will write
H
for a non-standard subgroup of
G
. We will improve the fixed point ratio bounds in Theorem
3.9
only for certain families of classical groups. The following list contains the possible socle types of the groups we consider and we will treat each possibility in turn:

\mathcal{B}=\{{\rm PSp}_{8}(q),{\rm PSL}^{\epsilon}_{8}(q),{\rm P\Omega}_{10}^{\epsilon}(q),{\rm PSL}_{6}^{\epsilon}(q),{\rm PSp}_{6}(q),{\rm\Omega}_{7}(q),{\rm PSL}_{5}^{\epsilon}(q),{\rm PSL}_{4}^{\epsilon}(q),{\rm PSL}_{3}^{\epsilon}(q)\}

We will prove the following theorem:

Theorem 4.1
.

If
G_{0}\in\mathcal{B}
, then
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<|x^{G_{0}}|^{-\beta}
, where
\beta
is recorded in Table
6
.

Remark 4.2
.

We note that our bounds have a slightly weaker form than the ones given by Burness in
[
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
]
. In particular, he bounds
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)
in terms of the
G
-conjugacy class of
x
, whilst we obtain an estimate in terms of the
G_{0}
-class of
x
. However, note that our different definition of
\eta_{G}
allows us to work with this more relaxed bound.

We will divide the proof of Theorem
4.1
in a series of propositions, according to the socle type of
G
, where we will consider the non-standard subgroups of
G
, for which the claim does not follow immediately from Theorem
3.9
. We briefly outline our general approach:

(i)

In the majority of cases we first verify that the claim holds using
Magma
for small values of
q
, where our general approach is not applicable, using the methods described in Section
2.2
.

(ii)

We next consider elements
x\in G_{I}
. To derive an upper bound on
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)
we appeal to Lemma
2.4
and Remark
2.5
, which allow us to work with the estimate

{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant\frac{|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|}{|x^{G_{0}}|}

so our first step is to derive an upper bound on
|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|
and a lower bound on
|x^{G_{0}}|
, which we either do directly, or we inspect the proofs of
[
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
]
, and we use the bounds derived there when those are sufficient.

(ii)

We then note that
\frac{|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|}{|x^{G_{0}}|}<|x^{G_{0}}|^{-\beta}
if and only if
\log|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|/\log|x^{G_{0}}|<1-\beta
, so we work with this bound and verify that the claim in Theorem
4.1
holds.

(iii)

We then consider graph, field and graph-field automorphisms. In this case, the estimates in the proofs of
[
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
]
are sufficient in most cases, and when they are not, then we work in a similar fashion as Burness to derive tighter bounds.

\begin{array}[]{lll}\hline\cr G_{0}&\beta&\text{Conditions}\\
\hline\cr{\rm PSp}_{8}(q)&1/3\\
{\rm PSL}_{8}(q)&1/3\\
{\rm P\Omega}_{10}^{\epsilon}(q)&1/3\\
{\rm PSL}_{6}(q)&1/3&C_{G}(x)\text{ not of type }{\rm GL}_{3}(q)^{2}\\
&0.32&C_{G}(x)\text{ of type }{\rm GL}_{3}(q)^{2}\\
{\rm PSU}_{6}(q)&1/3&C_{G}(x)\text{ not of type }{\rm GL}_{3}(q^{2})\\
&0.32&C_{G}(x)\text{ of type }{\rm GL}_{3}(q^{2})\\
{\rm PSp}_{6}(q)&0.4&H\text{ not of type }G_{2}(q),p=2\\
{\rm PSp}_{6}(q)&0.249&H\text{ of type }G_{2}(q),p=2\\
{\rm\Omega}_{7}(q)&0.4&H\text{ not of type }G_{2}(q),p\neq 2\\
{\rm\Omega}_{7}(q)&0.225&H\text{ of type }G_{2}(q),p\neq 2\\
{\rm PSL}_{5}^{\epsilon}(q)&9/20&x\in{\rm Inndiag}(G_{0})\\
&3/10&x=\phi,\tau,\text{ or }\phi\tau\\
{\rm PSL}_{4}^{\epsilon}(q)&9/20&x\in{\rm Inndiag}(G_{0})\\
&3/10&x=\phi,\tau,\text{ or }\phi\tau\\
{\rm PSL}_{3}^{\epsilon}(q)&9/20&x\in{\rm Inndiag}(G_{0})\\
&3/10&x=\phi,\tau,\text{ or }\phi\tau\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 6.
The value of
\beta
in Theorem
4.1
.

Proposition 4.3
.

Suppose that
G_{0}={\rm PSp}_{8}(q)
,
q\geqslant 3
and
x\in G
is an element of prime order
r
. Then
\mbox{{\rm fpr}}(x,G/H)<|x^{G_{0}}|^{-1/3}
.

Proof.

If
H
is not of type
{\rm Sp}_{4}(q^{2})
or
{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)\wr S_{2}
, then the claim follows by Theorem
3.9
, so it remains to consider those two cases. The claim can easily be checked using
Magma
for
q=3
using the function
CheckFpr()
provided in Section
2.2
, so for the remainder of the proof we may assume that
q\geqslant 4
.

We first consider the case where
x
is semisimple. First suppose that
H
is of type
{\rm Sp}_{4}(q^{2})
. Let
i
be minimal such that
r
divides
q^{i}-1
and
i_{0}
be minimal such that
r
divides
q^{2i_{0}}-1
, and note that
i_{0}=i/2
if
i
is even and
i_{0}=i
otherwise. Note that we may assume that
x\in H
, since otherwise
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)=0
. In this case
r
must divide
|H|
, and so we have
i\in\{1,2,4,8\}
. We need to consider each possibility of
i
in turn. The calculations for each value of
i
are straightforward and similar, and so we will only consider the cases where
i=4
and
i=1
here and leave the rest for the reader to check.

First suppose that
i=4
. In this case
i_{0}=2
and
C_{G}(x)
is of type
{\rm GU}_{2}(q^{2})
,
{\rm GU}_{1}(q^{2})^{2}
, or
{\rm GU}_{1}(q^{2})\times{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)
. If
C_{G}(x)
is of type
{\rm GU}_{2}(q^{2})
, then we compute

|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|=2\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{4}(q^{2})|}{|{\rm GU}_{2}(q^{2})|}+\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{4}(q^{2})|}{|{\rm GU}_{1}(q^{2})|}=2q^{6}(q^{4}+1)(q^{2}-1)+q^{8}(q^{2}-1)^{2}(q^{4}+1)

where
G_{I}={\rm Inndiag}(G_{0})
and

|x^{G_{0}}|=\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{8}(q)|}{|{\rm GU}_{2}(q^{2})|}=q^{14}(q^{2}-1)^{2}(q^{4}+1)(q^{6}-1),

and so the claim follows.

If
C_{G}(x)
is of type
{\rm GU}_{1}(q^{2})^{2}
, then we have

|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|\leqslant 4\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{4}(q^{2})|}{|{\rm GU}_{1}(q^{2})|^{2}}=4q^{8}(q^{2}-1)^{2}(q^{4}+1)

and

|x^{G_{0}}|=\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{8}(q)|}{|{\rm GU}_{1}(q^{2})|^{2}}=q^{16}(q^{2}-1)^{3}(q^{4}+1)(q^{6}-1),

and so
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<|x^{G}|^{-1/3}
here as well.

Finally, if
C_{G}(x)
is of type
{\rm GU}_{1}(q^{2})\times{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)
, then we compute

|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|\leqslant 2\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{4}(q^{2})|}{|{\rm GU}_{1}(q^{2})||{\rm Sp}_{2}(q^{2})|}=2q^{6}(q^{4}+1)(q^{2}-1)

and

|x^{G_{0}}|=\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{8}(q)|}{|{\rm GU_{1}}(q^{2})||{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)|}=q^{12}(q^{2}-1)(q^{6}-1)(q^{4}+1)

and we observe that the desired bound is satisfied.

We finally consider the case
i=i_{0}=1
. Note that we have assumed that
x\in H
and under this assumption we claim that
C_{G}(x)
must be of type
{\rm GL}_{4}(q)
,
{\rm GL}_{2}(q)\times{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)
, or
{\rm GL}_{2}(q)^{2}
. Let
\hat{x}
denote the lift of
x
in
{\rm Sp}_{4}(q^{2})
. Recall from Section
3.2
that two elements of order
r
are
{\rm Sp}_{4}(q^{2})
-conjugate if and only if they have the same eigenvalues in
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2i_{0}}}=\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}
. Moreover, we have seen again in Section
3.2
that since
i_{0}=1
the eigenvalues of
\hat{x}
come in inverse pairs
(\lambda,\lambda^{-1})
. Thus, if
\hat{x}
denotes the lift of
x
in
{\rm Sp}_{4}(q^{2})
, then we may write
\hat{x}=[\lambda,\lambda^{-1},\mu,\mu^{-1}]
with
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}
not necessarily distinct.

As explained in Section
3.2
, the equality
i=i_{0}
implies that
\sigma
and
\sigma_{0}
-orbits coincide, and in particular, Lemma
3.6
implies that
\hat{x}
embeds as
[(\lambda,\lambda^{-1})^{2},(\mu,\mu^{-1})^{2}]
in
G
, so all eigenvalues occur with even multiplicity. It follows that
C_{G}(x)
cannot be of type
{\rm GL}_{1}(q)^{4}
or
{\rm GL}_{3}(q)\times{\rm Sp}_{2}(q)
, and the claim follows.

We continue by considering each possible centraliser type for
x
in turn. All cases are entirely similar so we only consider the case where
C_{G}(x)
is of type
{\rm GL}_{2}(q)\times{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)
and leave the remaining cases to the reader.

In this case we compute

|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|=\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{4}(q^{2})|}{|{\rm GL}_{1}(q^{2})||{\rm Sp}_{2}(q^{2})|}=q^{6}(q^{2}+1)^{2}(q+1)(q^{4}+1)

and

|x^{G_{0}}|=\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{8}(q)|}{|{\rm GL}_{2}(q)||{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)|}=q^{11}(q^{4}+q^{2}+1)(q^{4}+1)(q^{2}+1)(q+1),

and so the claim holds.

Now suppose that
H
is of type
{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)\wr S_{2}
and again let
i
be minimal such that
r|q^{i}-1
. We may again assume that
x\in H
, and in particular that
r
divides
|H|
, since otherwise we have
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)=0
. It follows that
i\in\{1,2,4\}
. The computations are straightforward here as well, and so, to avoid unnecessary repetition, we only consider the case
i=2
and leave the remaining cases to the reader.

If
i=2
, then there are
11
possibilities for
C_{G}(x)
up to isomorphism. More precisely,
C_{G}(x)
is one of the following types:

\displaystyle{\rm GU}_{4}(q),{\rm GU}_{3}(q)\times{\rm GU}_{1}(q),{\rm GU}_{3}(q)\times{\rm Sp}_{2}(q),{\rm GU}_{2}(q)^{2},{\rm GU_{2}}(q)\times{\rm GU}_{1}(q)^{2},

\displaystyle{\rm GU}_{2}(q)\times{\rm GU}_{1}(q)\times{\rm Sp}_{2}(q),{\rm GU}_{2}(q)\times{\rm Sp}_{4}(q),{\rm GU}_{1}(q)^{4},{\rm GU}_{1}(q)^{3}\times{\rm Sp}_{2}(q),

\displaystyle{\rm GU}_{1}(q)^{2}\times{\rm Sp}_{4}(q),{\rm GU}_{1}(q)\times{\rm Sp}_{6}(q).

All cases can be handled in a similar way. For example, let us consider the case where
C_{G}(x)
is of type
{\rm GU}_{2}(q)^{2}
. As described in Section
3.2
, if
\hat{x}
denotes the lift of
x
in
{\rm Sp}_{8}(q)
, then
\hat{x}
is
{\rm Sp}_{8}(q)
-conjugate to an element of the form
[\Lambda_{1}^{2},\Lambda_{2}^{2}]
for two distinct
\sigma
-orbits
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}
.

Let
\widehat{H}={\rm Sp}_{4}(q)\wr S_{2}
denote the lift of
H
in
{\rm GSp}_{8}(q)
and
V_{1}\oplus V_{2}
denote the decomposition of
V
into
4
-dimensional
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-spaces
V_{1},V_{2}
preserved by
\hat{H}
. Note that since
r
is odd, we must have
\hat{x}\in\widehat{B}={\rm Sp}_{4}(q)^{2}
. Now there are two
\widehat{H}
-classes of elements that fuse to the
{\rm GSp}_{8}(q)
-class of
\hat{x}
, namely one with representative
[\hat{y},\hat{y}]
, where
\hat{y}\in{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)
is
{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)
-conjugate to
[\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}]
and the one with representative
[\hat{z},\hat{w}]
, where
\hat{z}\in{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)
is
{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)
-conjugate to
[\Lambda_{1}^{2}]
and
\hat{w}\in{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)
is
{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)
-conjugate to
[\Lambda_{2}^{2}]
. It now remains to compute the sizes of the
\widehat{H}
-classes of
[\hat{y},\hat{y}]
and
[\hat{z},\hat{w}]
to obtain a bound on
|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|
. It is easy to see that the
\widehat{H}
-class of
[\hat{y},\hat{y}]
consists of all elements of the form
[\hat{a},\hat{b}]
, with
\hat{a},\hat{b}

\widehat{H}
-conjugate to
\hat{y}
, and so

|[\hat{y},\hat{y}]^{\widehat{H}}|=\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)|^{2}}{|{\rm GU}_{1}(q)|^{4}}.

On the other hand, if
[\hat{a},\hat{b}]
is
\widehat{H}
-conjugate to
[\hat{z},\hat{w}]
, then since
\widehat{H}
can permute
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
either
\hat{a}\in\hat{z}^{\widehat{H}}
and
\hat{b}\in\hat{w}^{\widehat{H}}
, or
\hat{a}\in\hat{w}^{\widehat{H}}
and
\hat{b}\in\hat{z}^{\widehat{H}}
, and so

|[\hat{z},\hat{w}]^{\widehat{H}}|=2\left(\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)|}{|{\rm GU}_{2}(q)|}\right)^{2}.

It follows that

\displaystyle|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|=2\left(\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)|}{|{\rm GU}_{2}(q)|}\right)^{2}+\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)|^{2}}{|{\rm GU}_{1}(q)|^{4}}=2q^{6}(q-1)^{2}(q^{2}+1)^{2}+q^{8}(q-1)^{4}(q^{2}+1)^{2}

and moreover,

|x^{G_{0}}|=\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{8}(q)|}{|{\rm GU}_{2}(q)|^{2}}=q^{14}(q-1)^{2}(q^{2}+1)^{2}(q^{4}+q^{2}+1)(q^{4}+1),

so
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<|x^{G_{0}}|^{-1/3}
.

Now suppose that
x
is a unipotent element of odd prime order. If
H
of type
{\rm Sp}_{4}(q^{2})
, then the possible Jordan forms on
V
for an element
x\in H
are
[J_{4}^{2}],[J_{2}^{4}]
, and
[J_{2}^{2},J_{1}^{4}]
. We can easily compute
|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|
and
|x^{G_{0}}|
using the information in
[
22
, Table B.7]
in every case and deduce that the desired bound holds. For example, if
x
has Jordan form
[J_{2}^{2},J_{1}^{4}]
, then we have

|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|\leqslant\frac{2|{\rm Sp}_{4}(q^{2})|}{q^{6}|{\rm Sp}_{2}(q^{2})|}=2(q^{8}-1)

and

|x^{G_{0}}|\geqslant\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{8}(q)|}{q^{11}|{\rm O}^{-}_{2}(q)||{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)|}=\frac{1}{2}q(q-1)(q^{2}+1)(q^{4}+1)(q^{6}-1)

and those bounds are sufficient.

If
H
is of type
{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)\wr S_{2}
, then the possibilities and bounds for
|x^{G}\cap H|
and
|x^{G}|
are listed in the proof of
[
18
, 3.2]
, and one can check that those bounds are sufficient, so we omit the details here.

We now consider the case where
x
is an involution. If
H
is of type
{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)\wr S_{2}
, then one can easily check that the desired bound holds by inspecting the proof of
[
16
, 2.8, 2.9]
, so we now assume that
H
is of type
{\rm Sp}_{4}(q^{2})
.

If
x
is not semisimple with
C_{G}(x)
of type
{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)^{2}
, then the bounds found in the proof of
[
16
, 3.2]
are sufficient, so we may now assume that
q
is odd and
C_{G}(x)
is of type
{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)^{2}
. By
[
22
, Section 3.4.2.2]
we have
x\in G_{0}
. Now
[
47
, 4.3.10]
tells us that

H\cap G_{0}={\rm PSp}_{4}(q^{2}).\langle\phi\rangle=B.\langle\phi\rangle,

where
\phi
is a field automorphism of order 2, and so we compute

|x^{G_{I}}\cap B|\leqslant\frac{|{\rm PSp}_{4}(q^{2})|}{|{\rm Sp}_{2}(q^{2})|^{2}}\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,|x^{G_{0}}|\geqslant\frac{|{\rm PSp}_{8}(q)|}{2|{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)|^{2}}.

Moreover, (68) in
[
16
]
gives us

|x^{G_{I}}\cap(H\setminus B)|\leqslant\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{4}(q^{2})|}{|{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)|}

and so the claim holds.

Finally, if
x
is a field automorphism of order
r
, then the bounds presented at the start of
[
16
, Section 3.2]
and
[
16
, 2.3]
are sufficient.
∎

Proposition 4.4
.

Suppose that
G_{0}={\rm PSL}^{\epsilon}_{8}(q)
and
x\in G
is an element of prime order
r
. Then
\mbox{{\rm fpr}}(x,G/H)<|x^{G_{0}}|^{-1/3}
.

Proof.

We only need to consider the case where
H
is of type
{\rm Sp}_{8}(q)
, since otherwise the claim follows straight from Theorem
3.9
. In this remaining case, the claim can be verified using
Magma
for
q\leqslant 2
, and for
q\geqslant 3
one can argue in a similar fashion to the proof of Proposition
4.3
, so we leave the details for the reader to check.

If
x
is a field, graph, or graph-field automorphism, then the bounds in
[
15
, 8.1]
suffice, so it is left to check the claim for semisimple and unipotent elements. In those cases we follow the same arguments as in
[
15
, 8.1]
. We start by noting that
H\cap{\rm PGL}(V)\leqslant{\rm PGSp}_{8}(q)
, and so
|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|\leqslant|x^{{\rm PGSp}_{8}(q)}|
. Now the conjugacy classes in
{\rm PGSp}_{8}(q)
and
{\rm PGL}_{8}^{\epsilon}(q)
are discussed in Section
3.2
briefly and in
[
22
, Chapter 7]
in more detail, and the centraliser structure for each class can be read off from
[
22
, Tables B.3 and B.7]
, so one can check that the claim holds.
∎

Proposition 4.5
.

Suppose that
G_{0}={\rm P\Omega}^{\epsilon}_{10}(q)
and
x\in G
is an element of prime order
r
. Then
\mbox{{\rm fpr}}(x,G/H)<|x^{G_{0}}|^{-1/3}
.

Proof.

In this case we may assume that
H
is of type
{\rm GL}_{5}^{\epsilon}(q)
, as the claim follows by Theorem
3.9
in all other cases. The claim can be verified using
Magma
for
q\in\{2,3\}
, and for
q\geqslant 4
the bounds in the proof of
[
16
, 3.3]
are sufficient.
∎

Proposition 4.6
.

Suppose that
G_{0}={\rm PSL}^{\epsilon}_{6}(q)
and
x\in G
is an element of prime order
r
. Then either
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<|x^{G_{0}}|^{-1/3}
, or one of the following holds:

(i)

\epsilon=+
,
x
is semisimple with
C_{G}(x)
of type
{\rm GL}_{3}(q)^{2}
, and
\mbox{{\rm fpr}}(x,G/H)<|x^{G}|^{-0.32}

(ii)

\epsilon=-
,
x
is semisimple with
C_{G}(x)
of type
{\rm GL}_{3}(q^{2})
, and
\mbox{{\rm fpr}}(x,G/H)<|x^{G}|^{-0.32}
.

Proof.

Here we may assume that
H
is of type
{\rm Sp}_{6}(q)
since the claim follows directly by Theorem
3.9
in all other cases. First assume
x
is semisimple of odd order and let
i
be minimal such that
r|q^{i}-1
. Again, we may assume that
x\in H
and in particular that
r
divides
|H|
, since otherwise we have
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)=0
. Thus, we have
i\in\{1,2,3,4,6\}
. The embedding here is straightforward so we will only give details for the cases where
i\in\{1,4\}
and leave the remaining cases for the reader.

First suppose
i=4
. In this case
C_{G}(x)
is of type
{\rm GL}_{1}(q^{4})\times{\rm GL}^{\epsilon}_{2}(q)
and we have

|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|\leqslant\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{6}(q)|}{|{\rm GU}_{1}(q^{2})||{\rm Sp}_{2}(q)|}=q^{8}(q^{2}-1)(q^{6}-1)

and

\displaystyle|x^{G_{0}}|\geqslant\frac{|{\rm GL}_{6}(q)|}{|{\rm GL}_{1}(q^{4})||{\rm GU}_{2}(q)|}=q^{14}(q-1)^{2}(q^{3}-1)(q^{5}-1)(q^{4}+q^{2}+1).

The claim follows.

Now assume
i=1
. If
\epsilon=+
, then there are six possibilities for
C_{G}(x)
for an element
x\in H
. More precisely,
C_{G}(x)
can be of type
{\rm GL}_{3}(q)^{2}
,
{\rm GL}_{2}(q)^{2}\times{\rm GL}_{1}(q)^{2}
,
{\rm GL}_{2}(q)^{3}
,
{\rm GL}_{2}(q)\times{\rm GL}_{1}(q)^{4}
,
{\rm GL}_{1}(q)^{6}
, or
{\rm GL}_{4}(q)\times{\rm GL}_{1}(q)^{2}
. All cases can be handled in a similar way. For example, if
C_{G}(x)
is of type
{\rm GL}_{3}(q)^{2}
, then we find

|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|\leqslant\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{6}(q)|}{|{\rm GL}_{3}(q)|}=q^{6}(q+1)(q^{2}+1)(q^{3}+1)

and

|x^{G_{0}}|\geqslant\frac{|{\rm GL}_{6}(q)|}{|{\rm GL}_{3}(q)|^{2}}=q^{9}(q^{4}+q^{3}+q^{2}+q+1)(q^{2}+1)(q^{3}+1)

and one can check that
\log|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|/\log|x^{G_{0}}|<0.68
.

Similarly, if
C_{G}(x)
is of type
{\rm GL}_{2}(q)^{2}\times{\rm GL}_{1}(q)^{2}
, then we have

|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|\leqslant\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{6}(q)|}{|{\rm GL}_{2}(q)||{\rm GL}_{1}(q)|}=q^{8}(q+1)^{2}(q^{2}-1)(q^{4}+q^{2}+1)

and

|x^{G_{0}}|\geqslant\frac{|{\rm GL}_{6}(q)|}{|{\rm GL}_{2}(q)|^{2}|{\rm GL}_{1}(q)|}=q^{13}(q+1)(q^{2}+q+1)(q^{2}+1)(q^{4}+q^{3}+q^{2}+q+1)(q^{4}+q^{2}+1)

and so the claim is true in this case as well. We leave the remaining cases for the reader to check.

Now suppose that
\epsilon=-
. In this case the possible centraliser types for a prime order element
x\in H
are
{\rm GL}_{3}(q^{2})
,
{\rm GL}_{2}(q^{2})\times{\rm GL}_{1}(q^{2})
,
{\rm GL}_{2}(q^{2})\times{\rm GU}_{1}(q)
