,
{\rm GL}_{1}(q^{2})^{2}\times{\rm GU}_{2}(q)
,
{\rm GL}_{1}(q^{2})^{3}
, and
{\rm GL}_{1}(q^{2})\times{\rm GU}_{4}(q)
, and again we can verify that the desired bound holds by treating each possibility in turn. For example, if
C_{G}(x)
is of type
{\rm GL}_{3}(q^{2})
, then

|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|\leqslant\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{6}(q)|}{|{\rm GL}_{3}(q)|}=q^{6}(q+1)(q^{2}+1)(q^{3}+1)

and

|x^{G_{0}}|\geqslant\frac{|{\rm GU}_{6}(q)|}{|{\rm GL}_{3}(q^{2})|}=q^{9}(q+1)(q^{3}+1)(q^{5}+1)

and one can check that
\log|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|/\log|x^{G_{0}}|<0.68
.

Now suppose that
x
is unipotent. By appealing to Section
3.2
, we find that the Jordan decompositions corresponding to an element
x\in H
are

[J_{6}],[J_{4},J_{2}],[J_{4},J_{1}^{2}],[J_{3}^{2}],[J_{2}^{3}],[J_{2}^{2},J_{1}^{2}],[J_{2},J_{1}^{4}],

so we check each possibility in turn to confirm that the required bound holds. For example, if
x
is of type
[J_{2},J_{1}^{4}]
, then we get

|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|\leqslant\frac{2|{\rm Sp}_{6}(q)|}{q^{5}|{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)|}\leqslant 2(q^{6}-1)

and

|x^{G_{0}}|\geqslant\frac{|{\rm GU}_{6}(q)|}{q^{9}|{\rm GU}_{4}(q)||{\rm GU}_{1}(q)|}=(q^{4}-q^{3}+q^{2}-q+1)(q^{6}-1)

and hence
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<|x^{G_{0}}|^{-1/3}
. The other cases can be dealt with in a similar fashion by inspecting
[
22
, Tables B.3 and B.7]
, so we omit the details here.

Finally, if
x
is a graph field or graph field automorphism or a semisimple involution, then the bounds in the proof of
[
15
, 8.1]
suffice.
∎

Proposition 4.7
.

Suppose that
G_{0}={\rm PSp}_{6}(q)

q
is even and
x\in G
is an element of prime order
r
. Then either
\mbox{{\rm fpr}}(x,G/H)<|x^{G_{0}}|^{-0.4}
, or
H
is of type
G_{2}(q)
.

Proof.

The claim can be easily verified using
Magma
for
q\in\{2,4\}
, and so we may assume that
q\geqslant 8
for the remainder of the proof. By inspecting
[
12
, Tables 8.28 and 8.29]
, we find that if
H
is not of type
G_{2}(q)
, then
H_{0}=H\cap G_{0}\in\{{\rm Sp}_{2}(q)\wr S_{3},{\rm Sp}_{2}(q^{3}){:}3,{\rm Sp}_{6}(q_{0})\}
, where
k
is a prime and
q=q_{0}^{k}
.

First suppose that
H_{0}={\rm Sp}_{2}(q)\wr S_{3}=B.S_{3}
. If
x
is a field automorphism of order
r
, then the bounds in
[
16
, Section 2.3]
give

|x^{G}\cap H|<48q^{9(1-1/r)}\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,|x^{G}|>\frac{1}{4}q^{21(1-1/r)}

and so the claim holds.

Now suppose that
x\in{\rm PGL}(V)
. There are two cases to consider depending whether or not
x^{G}\cap(H\setminus B)\neq\emptyset
. If
x^{G}\cap(H\setminus B)\neq\emptyset
, then either
r=2
or
r=3
. If
r=2
, then by inspecting the proof of
[
16
, 2.8]
, we deduce that
\log|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|/\log|x^{G_{0}}|<0.543
, so we may now assume that
r=3
. In this case we may assume
x=b\pi=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\pi
, where
b\in B
and
\pi\in S_{3}
is a
3
-cycle and it follows from the proof of
[
63
, 4.5]
that
x
is
B
-conjugate to
\pi
. Now note that if
y=(A,B,C)\in C_{B}(x)
, then since
\pi
permutes
A,B
, and
C
, we must have
A=B=C
, and so

|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|=\frac{6|{\rm Sp}_{2}(q)|^{3}}{3|{\rm Sp}_{2}(q)|}=2q^{2}(q^{2}-1)^{2}.

We now note that with respect to a symplectic basis
\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\}
,
(I_{2},I_{2},I_{2})\pi
embeds in
{\rm Sp}_{6}(q)
as the block-diagonal matrix
[A,A]
, where

A=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&1\\
1&0&0\\
0&1&0\end{pmatrix}

and since
r=3
and
q
is even,
x
is semisimple. Moreover, since
q
is even,
3
must either divide
q-1
or
q+1
, and so if
i
is minimal such that
3
divides
q^{i}-1
, we must have
i=1
or
2
. Now note that
x
has eigenvalues
1^{2},(\omega)^{2},(\omega^{2})^{2}
, where
\omega\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{i}}
is a primitive cube root of unity, and so
C_{G}(x)
is of type
{\rm GL}_{2}^{\epsilon}(q)\times{\rm Sp}_{2}(q)
, where
\epsilon=(-1)^{i+1}
. Therefore,

|x^{G_{0}}|\geqslant\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{6}(q)|}{|{\rm Sp}_{2}(q)||{\rm GU}_{2}(q)|}=q^{7}(q-1)(q^{2}+1)(q^{4}+q^{2}+1)

and the claim holds.

The case
x^{G}\cap H\subseteq B
is straightforward. First suppose that
x
is semisimple and
i
is minimal such that
r
divides
q^{i}-1
. We may again assume that
x\in H
, in which case
r
divides
|H|
, and we must have
i\in\{1,2\}
. If
i=2
, then
C_{G}(x)
must be of type

{\rm GU}_{3}(q),{\rm GU}_{2}(q)\times{\rm Sp}_{2}(q),{\rm GU}_{2}(q)\times{\rm GU}_{1}(q),{\rm GU}_{1}(q)\times{\rm Sp}_{4}(q),{\rm GU}_{1}(q)^{2}\times{\rm Sp}_{2}(q),\text{ or }{\rm GU}_{1}(q)^{3}.

We can now compute bounds for
|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|
and
|x^{G_{0}}|
using
[
22
, Table B.7]
. For example, if
C_{G}(x)
is of type
{\rm GU}_{2}(q)\times{\rm GU}_{1}(q)
, then as discussed in Section
3.2
, we may assume that the lift
\hat{x}
of
x
in
{\rm Sp}_{6}(q)
is
[(\Lambda_{1})^{2},\Lambda_{2}]
for some
\sigma
-orbits
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}
. Now we find that
\hat{y}=[\hat{y_{1}},\hat{y_{2}},\hat{y_{3}}]\in\hat{x}^{{\rm Sp}_{2}(q)^{3}}
if and only if
\hat{y}_{i}
is
{\rm Sp}_{2}(q)
-conjugate to
[\Lambda_{2}]
for some
i
and
\hat{y}_{j}
is
{\rm Sp}_{2}(q)
-conjugate to
\Lambda_{1}
for
j\in\{1,2,3\}\setminus\{i\}
. Therefore,

|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|\leqslant\frac{3|{\rm Sp}_{2}(q)^{3}|}{|{\rm GU}_{1}(q)|^{3}}=3q^{3}(q-1)^{3}

and

|x^{G_{0}}|\geqslant\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{6}(q)|}{|{\rm GU}_{2}(q)||{\rm GU}_{1}(q)|}=q^{8}(q-1)^{2}(q^{2}+1)(q^{4}+q^{2}+1)

and the claim follows. We can compute in the same way in all other cases. If
i=1
, then the argument is almost identical, so we omit the details to avoid unnecessary repetition.

Now suppose that
x\in H
is unipotent. Since
x^{G}\cap H\subseteq B
, we deduce that
x
must be a
b_{1},c_{2}
or
b_{3}
involution by consulting
[
22
, 3.4.14]
. We consider each possibility in turn by inspecting
[
22
, Table B.7]
and we obtain the bounds in the following Table
7
, which allow us to verify that the desired claim holds.

\begin{array}[]{lll}\hline\cr x&|x^{G}\cap H|\leqslant&|x^{G}|\geqslant\\
\hline\cr b_{1}&3(q^{2}-1)&q^{6}-1\\
c_{2}&2(q^{2}-1)^{2}&(q^{4}-1)(q^{6}-1)\\
b_{3}&(q^{2}-1)^{3}&q^{2}(q^{4}-1)(q^{6}-1)\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 7.
Fixed point ratio bounds for unipotent involutions arising in Proposition
4.7
.

Now suppose that
H_{0}={\rm Sp}_{2}(q^{3}).3=B.3
. If
x
is a field automorphism of order
r
, then the proof of
[
16
, 3.2]
gives

|x^{G}\cap H|\leqslant 2q^{9(1-1/r)}\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,|x^{G}|>\frac{1}{4}q^{21(1-1/r)}

and the claim follows.

Now suppose that
x\in{\rm PGL}(V)
. If
x^{G}\cap(H\setminus B)\neq\emptyset
, then
x
acts on
{\rm soc}(H_{0})={\rm Sp}_{2}(q^{3})
as a field automorphism of order
3
, and so we get

|x^{G}\cap H|\leqslant 2\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{2}(q^{3})|}{|{\rm Sp}_{2}(q)|}=2q^{2}(q^{4}+q^{2}+1).

Moreover, with respect to the symplectic basis
\{e_{1},e_{1}^{q},e_{1}^{q^{2}},f_{1},f_{1}^{q},f_{1}^{q^{2}}\}
,
x
embeds in
{\rm Sp}_{6}(q)
as
[A,A]
, where
A
is the matrix defined above. By arguing as in the case
H_{0}={\rm Sp}_{2}(q)\wr S_{3}
, we get

|x^{G}|\geqslant\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{6}(q)|}{|{\rm Sp}_{2}(q)||{\rm GU}_{2}(q)|}=q^{7}(q-1)(q^{2}+1)(q^{4}+q^{2}+1).

Therefore, the required bound is satisfied and it now remains to deal with the case where
x^{G}\cap H\subseteq B
.

First suppose that
x
is semisimple (so
r
is odd), and let
i
be minimal such that
r|q^{i}-1
and
i_{0}
be minimal such that
r|q^{3i_{0}}-1
. Note that
i_{0}=i/3
if
3
divides
i
, and
i=i_{0}
otherwise. We may again assume that
x\in H
, so
r
divides
|H|
, and in particular
i\in\{1,2,3,6\}
. All cases are similar, so we only consider the cases where
i=6
and
i=2
here.

First suppose that
i=6
, so
i_{0}=2
. In this case,
x
is
G_{I}
-conjugate to
[\Lambda]Z
for some
\sigma
-orbit
\Lambda
, which as discussed in
[
22
, Section 5.3.1]
is a union of
3
distinct
\sigma_{0}
-orbits, where
\sigma_{0}:\alpha\mapsto\alpha^{q^{3}}
. Therefore,
C_{G}(x)
is of type
{\rm GU}_{1}(q^{3})
we compute

|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|\leqslant\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{2}(q^{3})|}{|{\rm GU}_{1}(q^{3})|}=q^{3}(q^{3}-1)

and

|x^{G_{0}}|\geqslant\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{6}(q)|}{|{\rm GU}_{1}(q^{3})|}=q^{9}(q^{2}-1)(q^{3}-1)(q^{4}-1)

and so the claim holds.

Similarly, if
i=i_{0}=2
, then
C_{G}(x)
is of type
{\rm GU}_{1}(q)^{3}
, and so

|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|\leqslant\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{2}(q^{3})|}{|{\rm GU}_{1}(q^{3})|}=q^{3}(q^{3}-1)

and

|x^{G_{0}}|\geqslant\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{6}(q)|}{|{\rm GU}_{1}(q)|^{3}}=q^{9}(q-1)^{3}(q^{2}+1)(q^{4}+q^{2}+1)

and we deduce that
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<|x^{G}|^{-0.4}
as desired.

Finally, if
x\in H
is unipotent, then it embeds in
G
as a
b_{3}
involution and we compute

|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|=q^{6}-1

and

|x^{G_{0}}|\geqslant q^{2}(q^{4}-1)(q^{6}-1)

Therefore, the claim is true for all elements
x\in H
.

We are now left with the case where
H
is of type
{\rm Sp}_{6}(q_{0})
, and
q=q_{0}^{k}
for some prime
k
. If
x\in{\rm PGL}(V)
, then we obtain the required bound by inspecting
[
22
, Table B.7]
in a similar fashion to the previous two cases, so we leave the details to the reader.

Now assume that
x
is a field automorphism of order
r
. If
r\neq k
, then
[
44
, 4.9.1]
implies that

|x^{G}\cap H|\leqslant|x^{{\rm Sp}_{6}(q_{0})}|<2q_{0}^{21(1-1/r)}

and
[
15
, 3.48]
gives

|x^{G}|>\frac{1}{4}q_{0}^{21(1-1/r)}

and it can be checked that those bounds are sufficient.

Suppose now that
r=k
. If
r>2
, then one can check that the trivial bound

|x^{G}\cap H|\leqslant|{\rm Sp}_{6}(q_{0})|

and

(1)

|x^{G}|>\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{6}(q)|}{|{\rm Sp}_{6}(q_{0})|}

suffice. Otherwise, if
r=k=2
, then
[
42
, 4.3]
gives us

\displaystyle|x^{G}\cap H|\leqslant i_{2}({\rm Sp}_{6}(q_{0}))+1

\displaystyle\leqslant 1+\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{6}(q_{0})|}{q_{0}^{7}|{\rm Sp}_{2}(q_{0})|}+\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{6}(q_{0})|}{q_{0}^{8}|{\rm Sp}_{2}(q_{0})|}+\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{6}(q_{0})|}{q_{0}^{5}|{\rm Sp}_{4}(q_{0})|}+\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{6}(q_{0})|}{q_{0}^{6}|{\rm Sp}_{2}(q_{0})|}

\displaystyle=(q_{0}^{2}+q_{0}+1)(q_{0}^{4}-1)(q_{0}^{6}-1)+q_{0}^{6},

where
i_{2}({\rm Sp}_{6}(q_{0}))
denotes the number of involutions in
{\rm Sp}_{6}(q_{0})
. The result follows via (
1
), so the proof is complete.
∎

Proposition 4.8
.

Suppose that
G_{0}={\Omega}_{7}(q)
,
q\geqslant 5
and
x\in G
is an element of prime order
r
. Then either
\mbox{{\rm fpr}}(x,G/H)<|x^{G_{0}}|^{-0.4}
, or
G
is of type
G_{2}(q)
.

Proof.

The claim can be verified using
Magma
for
q=5
, so we may assume that
q\geqslant 7
for the remainder of the proof. By inspecting
[
12
, Tables 8.39-8.40]
we see that if
H
is not of type
G_{2}(q)
, then
H
must be of type
{\rm O}_{1}(q)\wr S_{7},{\rm O}_{7}(q_{0})
with
q=q_{0}^{k}
for some prime
k
, or
{\rm Sp}_{6}(2)
. We treat each possibility in turn.

If
H
is of type
{\rm Sp}_{6}(2)
, then it follows directly from
[
17
, 2.15]
that
{\rm fpr}(x)<|x^{G}|^{-1/2}
, and so the claim holds.

If
H
is of type
{\rm O}_{1}(q)\wr S_{7}
, then the bounds in
[
16
, 2.10]
suffice.

Finally, if
H
is of type
{\rm O}_{7}(q)
, then we obtain the result by inspecting the proof of
[
15
, 5.3]
and
[
22
, Table B.12]
, in a similar fashion to the proof of Proposition
4.7
. We omit the details.
∎

Proposition 4.9
.

Suppose that
G_{0}={\rm PSL}^{\epsilon}_{5}(q)
and
x\in G_{I}
is an element of prime order
r
. Then
\mbox{{\rm fpr}}(x,G/H)<|x^{G_{0}}|^{-9/20}
.

Proof.

The claim can easily be verified using
Magma
when
q\leqslant 5
, so for the remainder we will assume that
q\geqslant 7
. By inspecting
[
12
, Tables 8.18-8.21]
, we deduce that the possible types of
H
are the ones listed in Table
8
. Moreover, it follows from Lemma
3.18
that
|x^{G_{0}}|>\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{8}
.

\begin{array}[]{lll}\hline\cr G_{0}&\text{type of }H&\text{Conditions}\\
\hline\cr{\rm L}_{5}^{\epsilon}(q)&{\rm GL}_{1}^{\epsilon}(q)\wr S_{5}&\\
&{\rm GL}_{1}^{\epsilon}(q^{5})&\\
&5^{2}.{\rm Sp}_{2}(5)&p\equiv q\equiv\epsilon\allowbreak\mkern 4.0mu({\operator@font mod}\,\,5)\text{ or }\epsilon=-,q=p^{2}\text{ and }p\equiv 2,3\allowbreak\mkern 4.0mu({\operator@font mod}\,\,5)\\
&{\rm O}_{5}(q)&q\,\text{odd}\\
&{\rm GU}_{5}(q_{0})&q=q_{0}^{k}\text{ with }k=2\text{ if }\epsilon=+\text{ and }k\text{ odd prime if }\epsilon=-\\
&{\rm L}_{2}(11)&q=p\equiv\epsilon,3\epsilon,4\epsilon,5\epsilon,9\epsilon\allowbreak\mkern 4.0mu({\operator@font mod}\,\,11)\\
&{\rm U}_{4}(q)&q=p\equiv\epsilon\allowbreak\mkern 4.0mu({\operator@font mod}\,\,6)\\
{\rm L}_{5}(q)&{\rm GL}_{5}(q_{0})&q=q_{0}^{k},k\,\text{prime}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 8.
Non-standard subgroups of almost simple groups with socle
{\rm L}_{5}^{\epsilon}(q)\,(q\geqslant 7)
.

First note that if
H
is of type
5^{2}.{\rm Sp}_{2}(5)
, then since
q\geqslant 7
and
q\equiv 1\allowbreak\mkern 4.0mu({\operator@font mod}\,\,5)
, the required bound holds, since
|x^{G}\cap H|\leqslant|H|
.

Similarly, if
H
is of type
{\rm L}_{2}(11)
or
{\rm U}_{4}(2)
, then we can construct
{\rm Aut}(H)
using the
Magma
command
AutomorphismGroupSimpleGroup()
, and we observe that

|x^{G}\cap H|\leqslant\max{\{i_{s}({\rm Aut}(H))\,:\,s\in\pi(|{\rm Aut}(H)|)\}}\leqslant 5184,

where we recall that
i_{s}({\rm Aut}(H))
denotes the number of elements of prime order
s
in
{\rm Aut}(H)
. This is sufficient unless
q=7
and
\epsilon=+
and
H
is of type
U_{4}(2)
. In this case, we observe that if
r\neq 5
, then
i_{r}({\rm Aut}(H))\leqslant 891
, which is sufficient.

On the other hand, if
r=5
, then
x
is semisimple and the minimal
i
such that
r|q^{i}-1
is
4
. This implies that
C_{G}(x)
is not of type
{\rm GL}_{4}^{\epsilon}(q)\times{\rm GL}_{1}^{\epsilon}(q)
, so
|x^{G_{0}}|>\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{12}
, and the claim is again satisfied.

Now assume that
H
is of type
{\rm GL}_{1}^{\epsilon}(q)\wr S_{5}
. Then
H
preserves a decomposition
V_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus V_{5}
of the natural module
V
. Recall from Lemma
3.18
that
|x^{G_{0}}|>\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)^{2}q^{14}
, unless
x
is semisimple and
C_{G}(x)
is of type
{\rm GL}_{s}(q)\times{\rm GL}_{n-s}(q)
for
s\in\{3,4\}
, or
x
is unipotent with Jordan form
[J_{2}^{s},J_{1}^{5-2s}]
for
s\in\{1,2\}
, and so if
x
is not in those classes of prime order elements, the trivial bound

(2)

|x^{G}\cap H|\leqslant|H|<\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)^{2}q^{14}\right)^{11/20}<|x^{G_{0}}|^{11/20}

holds and we are done. We may now assume that
|x^{G_{0}}|\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)^{2}q^{14}
. Recall that
[
47
, 4.2.9]
implies that
{\rm PGL}(V)\cap H\leqslant(q-\epsilon)^{4}.S_{5}=B.S_{5}
.

First assume that
x
is unipotent. Then
x^{G_{I}}\cap B=\emptyset
, since
r
does not divide
|B|
, so we may write
x=b\pi
for some
b\in B
and
\pi=(r^{h},1^{5-hr})\in S_{5}
. Note that
[
15
, 3.11]
implies that
x
lifts to a unique element
\hat{x}=(\hat{x}_{1},\ldots,\hat{x}_{5})\pi\in\hat{B}\pi
of order
r=p
, where
\widehat{B}={\rm GL}^{\epsilon}_{1}(q)^{5}
. Moreover, it is shown in the proof of
[
63
, 4.5]
that
\hat{x}
is
\widehat{B}
-conjugate to
(1,\ldots,1,\hat{x}_{hr+1},\ldots,\hat{x}_{5})\pi
, and as demonstrated in the proof of
[
16
, 2.6]
,
\hat{x}
has Jordan form
[J_{p}^{h+b_{p}},J_{p-1}^{b_{p-1}},\ldots,J_{1}^{b_{1}}]
, where
[J_{p}^{b_{p}},\ldots,J_{1}^{b_{1}}]
is the Jordan form of the restriction of
x
on
V_{hr+1}\oplus\cdots\oplus V_{5}
.

From this we deduce that if
\hat{x}
has Jordan form
[J_{2},J_{1}^{3}]
, then
\pi
must be a
2
-cycle, and so we compute

|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|=\binom{5}{2}(q-\epsilon)

and since
|x^{G_{0}}|>\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{8}
, we clearly have
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<|x^{G_{0}}|^{-9/20}
, as claimed.

Similarly, if
\hat{x}
has Jordan form
[J_{2}^{2},J_{1}]
, then
\pi
must be a product of two transpositions, and so

|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|=\frac{1}{2}\binom{5}{2}\binom{3}{2}(q-1)^{3}

which is again sufficient.

Now assume that
x
is semisimple. Recall that under our assumption
C_{G}(x)
is of type
{\rm GL}_{4}(q)\times{\rm GL}_{1}(q)
or
{\rm GL}^{\epsilon}_{3}(q)\times{\rm GL}^{\epsilon}_{2}(q)
. First assume that
C_{G}(x)
is of type
{\rm GL}_{4}(q)\times{\rm GL}_{1}(q)
, so
\hat{x}
has eigenvalues
(\lambda)^{4},\mu\in\mathbb{F}_{q}
. If
x^{G_{I}}\cap H\subseteq B
, then
|x^{G}\cap H|
is bounded above by the number of ways of arranging the eigenvalues of
\hat{x}
on the main diagonal, so we get
|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|\leqslant 5
, and the bound quickly follows, so we may now assume that
x^{G_{I}}\cap(H\setminus B)\neq\emptyset
. We may gain write
x=b\pi
for some
b\in B
and
\pi=(r^{h},1^{5-hr})\in S_{5}
. Note that
\pi
embeds in
{\rm GL}(V)
as a permutation matrix, and so we quickly deduce that if
\hat{x}=[\lambda I_{4},\mu]
, then
\pi
must be a transposition, and consequently,
x
must embed in
G
as a
t_{1}
involution. Therefore, we get

|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|\leqslant|x^{G_{I}}\cap B|+|x^{G_{I}}\cap(H\setminus B)|=5+10(q-\epsilon)

and so the claim is true.

If
C_{G}(x)
is of type
{\rm GL}^{\epsilon}_{3}(q)\times{\rm GL}^{\epsilon}_{2}(q)
, then using the same arguments we find that

|x^{G_{I}}\cap B|\leqslant\binom{5}{2}\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,|x^{G_{I}}\cap(H\setminus B)|=\frac{1}{2}\binom{5}{2}\binom{3}{2}(q-1)^{3}

and since
|x^{G_{0}}|>\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{8}
the result follows.

If
H
is of type
{\rm GL}_{1}^{\epsilon}(q^{5}).5
, then it follows directly from
[
22
, 5.3.2]
that there are no elements in
H
with
|x^{G_{0}}|\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{12}
, and so we have

|x^{G}\cap H|\leqslant|H|<\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{12}\right)^{11/20}<|x^{G_{0}}|^{11/20}

for all
x\in H
, as required.

Finally, if
H
is of type
{\rm O}_{5}(q)
,
{\rm GL}_{5}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}(q_{0})
, then it is straightforward to verify the required bound using the relevant tables in
[
22
, Appendix B]
, as in the proof of Proposition
4.7
.
∎

Proposition 4.10
.

Suppose that
G_{0}={\rm PSL}^{\epsilon}_{4}(q)
and
x\in G_{I}
is an element of prime order
r
. Then
\mbox{{\rm fpr}}(x,G/H)<|x^{G_{0}}|^{-9/20}
.

Proof.

If
q\leqslant 11
, then we verify that the bound holds using
Magma
, so we may assume for the remainder of the proof that
q\geqslant 13
. By inspecting
[
12
, Tables 8.8-8.11]
we deduce that the possible types of
H
are the ones listed in Table
9
. Moreover, Lemma
3.18
implies that
|x^{G_{0}}|>\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{6}
for any prime order element
x\in G_{I}
.

\begin{array}[]{lll}\hline\cr G_{0}&\text{type of }H&\text{Conditions}\\
\hline\cr{\rm L}_{4}^{\epsilon}(q)&{\rm GL}_{1}^{\epsilon}(q)\wr S_{4}&\\
&{\rm GU}_{4}(q_{0})&q=q_{0}^{k}\text{ with }k=2\text{ if }\epsilon=+\text{ and }k\text{ odd prime if }\epsilon=-\\
&2^{4}.{\rm Sp}_{4}(2)&p=q\equiv\epsilon,5\epsilon\allowbreak\mkern 4.0mu({\operator@font mod}\,\,8)\\
&{\rm O}^{+}_{4}(q)&q\,\text{odd}\\
&{\rm O}^{-}_{4}(q)&q\,\text{odd}\\
&{\rm U}_{4}(2)&q=p\equiv\epsilon\allowbreak\mkern 4.0mu({\operator@font mod}\,\,6)\\
&{\rm L}_{2}(7)&q=p\equiv\epsilon,2\epsilon,4\epsilon\allowbreak\mkern 4.0mu({\operator@font mod}\,\,7)\\
&A_{7}&q=p\equiv\epsilon,2\epsilon,4\epsilon\allowbreak\mkern 4.0mu({\operator@font mod}\,\,7)\\
{\rm L}_{4}(q)&{\rm GL}_{4}(q_{0})&q=q_{0}^{k},k\text{ prime}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 9.
Non-standard subgroups of almost simple groups with socle
{\rm L}_{4}^{\epsilon}(q)\,(q\geqslant 8)
.

If
H\in\mathscr{S}
, then using the
Magma
command
AutomorphismGroupSimpleGroup()
, we can construct
{\rm Aut}(H)
and obtain bounds for
m=\max\{i_{s}({\rm Aut}(H))\,:\,s\in\pi(|{\rm Aut}(H)|)\}
. For
q>13
we find

(3)

|x^{G}\cap H|\leqslant m\leqslant 5184<|x^{G_{0}}|^{11/20}

and this bound is sufficient, so we may now assume that
q=13
. If
H
is not of type
{\rm U}_{4}(2)
, or
r\neq 5
, then again by computing directly using
Magma
, we find that
m\leqslant 891
, and so the claim holds.

Now let us assume that
q=13
,
r=5
and
H
is of type
{\rm U}_{4}(2)
. Then
\epsilon=+
and we note that
r
does not divide
q-1
, so Lemma
3.18
implies that that
|x^{G_{0}}|>\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{8}
. The claim is then verified via (
3
).

Now assume
H
is of type
{\rm GL}_{1}^{\epsilon}(q)\wr S_{4}
. If
|x^{G_{0}}|>\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)^{2}q^{10}
, then the trivial bound
|x^{G}\cap H|\leqslant|H|

is sufficient. We may now assume that
|x^{G_{0}}|\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)^{2}q^{10}
. Lemma
3.18
implies that if this holds, then either
x
is unipotent with Jordan form
[J_{2}^{s},J_{1}^{4-2s}]
for
s\in\{1,2\}
, or
x
is semisimple and
C_{G}(x)
is of type
{\rm GL}^{\epsilon}_{s}(q)\times{\rm GL}^{\epsilon}_{4-s}(q)
for
s\in\{2,3\}
or
{\rm GL}_{2}(q^{2})
. In those cases we can argue in the same way as in the proof of Proposition
4.9
and obtain the required bound, so we leave the details to the reader.

If
H
is of type
2^{4}.{\rm Sp}_{4}(2)
, then the claim follows directly from the bounds established in the proof of
[
15
, 6.5]
.

Finally, if
H
is of type
{\rm GL}_{4}(q_{0})
,
{\rm GU}_{4}(q_{0})
, or
{\rm O}^{\epsilon}_{4}(q)
then we can compute directly using the relevant tables in
[
22
, Appendix B]
.
∎

Proposition 4.11
.

Suppose that
G_{0}={\rm PSL}^{\epsilon}_{4}(q)
and
x\in G
is a field, graph, or graph-field automorphism of prime order
r
. Then
\mbox{{\rm fpr}}(x,G/H)<|x^{G_{0}}|^{-3/10}
.

Proof.

If
q\leqslant 11
, then we can verify the claim using
Magma
, so we may assume that
q\geqslant 13
for the remainder of the proof. First suppose that
r
is odd. Note that we must have
q\neq p
, and so
G
is of type
{\rm GL}_{1}^{\epsilon}(q)\wr S_{4}
,
{\rm GL}_{4}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}(q_{0})
or
{\rm O}^{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}_{4}(q)
. In those cases the bounds in the proofs of the bounds in the proofs of
[
16
, 2.7]
,
[
15
, 8.2]
and
[
15
, 5.1]
respectively are sufficient.

Now suppose that
r=2
. If
H\in\mathscr{S}
, then again
q=p
, and so
x
must be an involutory graph automorphism. The same argument we used in Proposition
4.10
shows that
|x^{G}\cap H|\leqslant 891
. Moreover, Lemma
3.18
implies that
|x^{G_{0}}|>\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{4}
and those bounds are sufficient unless
q=13
. Now suppose that
q=13
. If
\epsilon=+
, then we find

|x^{G_{0}}|=\frac{1}{2}\frac{|{\rm PGL}_{4}(13)|}{|{\rm PGSp}_{4}(13)|}=371124

and one can check that the claim holds. On the other hand, if
\epsilon=-
, then
H
is not of type
{\rm U}_{4}(2)
, so we get

|x^{G}\cap H|\leqslant i_{2}({\rm P\Gamma L}_{2}(7))\leqslant 241

and this bound is sufficient.

If
H
is of type
{\rm GL}_{1}^{\epsilon}(q)\wr S_{4}
, then the bounds in
[
16
, 2.7]
are sufficient if
x
is a field or graph automorphism. If
x
is a graph-field automorphism then Lemma
3.18
gives
|x^{G_{0}}|>\frac{1}{8}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{15/2}
and this together with the bound
|x^{G}\cap H|<|H|
is sufficient.

If
H
is of type
2^{4}.{\rm Sp}_{4}(2)
, then
q=p
, and so again,
x
must be an involutory graph automorphism. As demonstrated in the proof of
[
15
, 6.6]
, we have
|x^{G}\cap H|\leqslant 340
, and one can check that this bound suffices.

Finally, if
H
is of type
{\rm O}_{4}^{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}(q)
or
{\rm GL}_{4}(q_{0})
, then the claim can be verified via the bounds in the proofs of
[
15
, 8.2]
and
[
15
, 5.1]
respectively.
∎

Proposition 4.12
.

Suppose that
G_{0}={\rm PSL}^{\epsilon}_{3}(q)
and
x\in G_{I}
is an element of prime order
r
. Then
\mbox{{\rm fpr}}(x,G/H)<|x^{G_{0}}|^{-9/20}
.

Proof.

The claim can easily be verified using
Magma
if
q\leqslant 19
, and so we may assume for the remainder of the proof that
q\geqslant 23
. By inspecting
[
12
, Tables 8.3-8.6]
we deduce that the possible types of
H
are the ones listed in Table
10
.

\begin{array}[]{l l l}\hline\cr G_{0}&\text{type of }H&\text{Conditions}\\
\hline\cr{\rm L}_{3}^{\epsilon}(q)&{\rm GL}_{1}(q)\wr S_{3}&\\
&{\rm GU}_{3}(q_{0})&q=q_{0}^{k}\text{ with }k=2\text{ if }\epsilon=+\text{ and }k\text{ odd prime if }\epsilon=-\\
&3^{2}.{\rm Sp}_{2}(3)&p=q\equiv\epsilon\allowbreak\mkern 4.0mu({\operator@font mod}\,\,3)\\
&{\rm O}_{3}(q)&q\,\text{odd}\\
&{\rm L}_{2}(7)&q=p\equiv\epsilon,2\epsilon,4\epsilon\allowbreak\mkern 4.0mu({\operator@font mod}\,\,7)\\
&A_{6}&q=p\equiv\epsilon,4\epsilon\allowbreak\mkern 4.0mu({\operator@font mod}\,\,15)\text{ or }\epsilon=+,q=p^{2}\text{ and }p\equiv 2,3\allowbreak\mkern 4.0mu({\operator@font mod}\,\,5)\\
{\rm L}_{3}^{\epsilon}(q)&{\rm GL}_{3}(q_{0})&q=q_{0}^{k},k\,\text{prime}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 10.
Non-standard subgroups of almost simple groups with socle
{\rm L}_{3}^{\epsilon}(q)\,(q\geqslant 16)
.

If
H\in\mathscr{S}
, then as in the proofs of Propositions
4.9
, and
4.10
, we use the
Magma
command
AutomorphismGroupSimpleGroup()
to construct
{\rm Aut}(H)
and compute
m=\max\{i_{s}(H)\,:\,s\,\text{prime}\}
. If
H
is of type
{\rm L}_{2}(7)
, then

|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|\leqslant m=56

and this bound is sufficient for all
x\in H
, as
|x^{G_{0}}|>\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{4}
by Lemma
3.18
for all
x\in G_{I}
. On the other hand, if
x\in H
is of type
A_{6}
, then
m=144
, and since
q\geqslant 23
, we deduce that

|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|\leqslant m=144<\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{4}\right)^{11/20}<|x^{G_{0}}|^{11/20}.

Now suppose that
H
is of type
3^{2}.{\rm Sp}_{2}(3)
. Since
q\geqslant 23
, the trivial bound

|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|\leqslant|H|<\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{4}\right)^{11/20}<|x^{G_{0}}|^{11/20}

is sufficient.

Next let us assume that
H
is of type
{\rm GL}_{1}^{\epsilon}(q)\wr S_{3}
. If
|x^{G_{0}}|>\frac{1}{6}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)^{2}q^{6}
, then we obtain the result via the trivial bound
|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|\leqslant|H\cap G_{I}|
, so we may assume that
|x^{G_{0}}|\leqslant\frac{1}{6}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)^{2}q^{6}
. In this case, Lemma
3.18
implies that if
x
is unipotent, then it has Jordan form
[J_{2},J_{1}]
and if
x
is semisimple, then
C_{G}(x)
is of type
{\rm GL}^{\epsilon}_{2}(q)\times{\rm GL}^{\epsilon}_{1}(q)
. If
x
is unipotent, then we argue in exactly the same way as in the proof of Proposition
4.9
and we find
|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|\leqslant 3(q+1)
. Moreover, we note that
|x^{G_{0}}|>\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{4}
, so the claim holds. If
x
is semisimple, then the argument is also identical with the one in the proof of Proposition
4.9
, and we get
|x^{G}\cap H|\leqslant 3(q+1)+3
, which is again sufficient.

If
H
is of type
{\rm GL}_{1}^{\epsilon}(q^{3})
, then it follows immediately from
[
22
, 5.3.2]
that
|x^{G_{0}}|>\frac{1}{6}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)^{2}q^{6}
for every
x\in H
, and so the trivial bound
|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|\leqslant|H\cap G_{I}|
is sufficient.

Finally, if
H
is of type
{\rm GL}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}_{3}(q_{0})
or
{\rm O}_{3}(q)
, then we obtain the required result by computing exact fixed point ratios using the relevant tables in
[
22
, Appendix B]
. We will only consider the case where
\epsilon=+
and
H
is of type
{\rm GU}_{3}(q^{1/2})
here to avoid unnecessary repetition, as all cases are very similar.

First suppose that
x
is unipotent. Then
x
either has Jordan form
[J_{3}]
or
[J_{2},J_{1}]
on
V
. If
x
has Jordan form
[J_{3}]
, then we find

|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|\leqslant\frac{|{\rm GU}_{3}(q_{0})|}{q_{0}^{2}|{\rm GU}_{1}(q_{0})|}=q_{0}(q_{0}^{2}-1)(q_{0}^{3}+1)

and

|x^{G_{0}}|\geqslant\frac{|{\rm GL}_{3}(q)|}{q^{2}|{\rm GL}_{1}(q)|}=q(q^{2}-1)(q^{3}-1)

and one can verify that the claim holds. Similarly, if
x
has Jordan form
[J_{2},J_{1}]
, then we get

|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|\leqslant\frac{|{\rm GU}_{3}(q_{0})|}{q_{0}^{3}|{\rm GU}_{1}(q_{0})|^{2}}=(q_{0}-1)(q_{0}^{3}+1)

and

|x^{G_{0}}|\geqslant\frac{|{\rm GL}_{3}(q)|}{q^{3}|{\rm GL}_{1}(q)|^{2}}=(q+1)(q^{3}-1)

and the required bound holds in this case as well.

Now suppose that
x
is semisimple, and let
i
be minimal such that
r|q^{i}-1
and
i_{0}
be minimal such that
r|q_{0}^{2i_{0}}-1
. Note that
i=i_{0}/2
if
i_{0}
is even and
i=i_{0}
otherwise. There are two possible centraliser types for
x
, namely
{\rm GL}_{2}(q)\times{\rm GL}_{1}(q)
and
{\rm GL}_{1}(q)^{3}
. If
C_{G}(x)
is of type
{\rm GL}_{2}(q)\times{\rm GL}_{1}(q)
, then we compute

|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|\leqslant q_{0}^{2}(q_{0}^{2}-q_{0}+1)\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,|x^{G_{0}}|\geqslant q^{2}(q^{2}+q+1)

and if
C_{G}(x)
is of type
{\rm GL}_{1}(q)^{3}
, then

|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|\leqslant q_{0}^{3}(q_{0}-1)(q_{0}^{2}-q_{0}+1)+q_{0}^{3}(q_{0}^{3}+1)\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,|x^{G_{0}}|\geqslant q^{3}(q+1)(q^{2}+q+1)

and one can check that those bounds are sufficient.

∎

Proposition 4.13
.

Suppose that
G_{0}={\rm PSL}^{\epsilon}_{3}(q)
and
x\in G
is a field, graph, or graph-field automorphism of prime order
r
. Then
\mbox{{\rm fpr}}(x,G/H)<|x^{G_{0}}|^{-3/10}
.

Proof.

The cases where
q\leqslant 19
can be checked using
Magma
, so we may assume that
q\geqslant 23
. Lemma
3.18
implies that
|x^{G_{0}}|>\frac{1}{6}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{4}
, and so unless
H
is of type
{\rm GL}_{3}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}(q_{0})
,
{\rm O}_{3}(q)
or
{\rm GL}_{1}^{\epsilon}(q)\wr S_{3}
, then the bound
|x^{G}\cap H|<|H|
is sufficient.

If
H
is of type
{\rm GL}_{3}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}(q_{0})
or
{\rm O}_{3}(q)
and
x
is not an involutory graph-field automorphism then the bounds in the proofs of
[
15
, 5.1]
and
[
15
, 8.2]
are sufficient, so now assume that
x
is an involutory graph-field automorphism. In both cases, Lemma
3.8
implies that
x^{G}\cap H\subseteq\widetilde{H}x
, where
\widetilde{H}=H\cap G_{I}
and using
[
51
, 1.3]
we find

|x^{G}\cap H|\leqslant i_{2}(\widetilde{H})+1<2(1+1q^{-1/2})q^{5/2}

which is sufficient.

Finally assume that
H
is of type
{\rm GL}_{1}^{\epsilon}(q)\wr S_{3}
. If
r>2
, then the bound
|x^{G}\cap H|<|H|
is sufficient, so now assume that
r=2
. In this case, again appealing to Lemma
3.8
gives

|x^{G}\cap H|\subseteq\widetilde{H}x\leqslant i_{2}(\widetilde{H})+1\leqslant 16,

and recall that
|x^{G_{0}}|>\frac{1}{6}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{4}
, so we are done.
∎

Proposition 4.14
.

Suppose that
G_{0}={\rm PSp}_{4}(q)
for
q\geqslant 4
, and
x\in G_{I}
is an element of prime order
r
. Then
\mbox{{\rm fpr}}(x,G/H)<|x^{G_{0}}|^{-9/20}
.

Proof.

Using
[
12
, Tables 8.12-8.14]
, as well as Table
3
, we can determine the possibilities of
H
. For most of those cases we use entirely similar methods as the ones we use to prove Propositions
4.9
,
4.10
, and
4.12
, so we only discuss the cases where we use different methods here, to avoid unnecessary repetition. In particular, we discuss the cases where
H
is of type
{}^{2}B_{2}(q)
,
O_{2}^{\epsilon}(q)\wr S_{2}
, or
O^{-}_{2}(q^{2}).2
for
q
even,
{\rm L}_{2}(q)
for
q
odd.

If
H
is of type
{}^{2}B_{2}(q)
, then we obtain the required bounds by inspecting the proof of
[
18
, 4.4]
, so it remains to discuss the other four cases.

Suppose that
H
is of type
O_{2}^{\epsilon}(q)\wr S_{2}
. Note that if
|x^{G_{0}}|>\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{6}
, then the trivial bound
|x^{G}\cap H|<|H|
is sufficient. Now suppose that
|x^{G_{0}}|\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{6}
. Lemma
3.18
implies that this only occurs when
x
is a
b_{1}
involution. In this case we know that
x\in G_{0}
, and so
x^{G}\cap H=x^{G}\cap H_{0}
, where
H_{0}=H\cap G_{0}
. We can infer from
[
12
, Table 8.14]
that
H_{0}<K<G_{0}
, where
K={\rm Sp}_{2}(q)\wr S_{2}
, and so
|x^{G}\cap H|=|x^{G}\cap H_{0}|\leqslant|x^{G}\cap K|
. Now
[
16
, 2.9]
gives
|x^{G}\cap K|\leqslant 2q^{2}
and we get
|x^{G_{0}}|=q^{4}-1
, and one can check that those bounds are sufficient for
q>32
.

If
q\leqslant 32
, then we can construct
H_{0}
using
Magma
as the normaliser of an appropriate Sylow subgroup. We only give the details for the case where
H
is of type
\Omega^{-}_{2}(q)\wr S_{2}
when
q=32
, as the other cases are similar. In this case we can obtain
H_{0}
as the normaliser of a Sylow
3
-subgroup. Note that
3
divides
q+1
, and in particular, the
3
-factor of
|G_{0}|
is the same as the
3
-factor of
(q+1)^{2}
, so a Sylow
3
-subgroup
P
of
G_{0}
is contained in some maximal torus
T\cong C_{q+1}\times C_{q+1}
. In particular,
P\cong C_{3}\times C_{3}
is a characteristic subgroup of
T
, so
N_{G}(T)\leqslant N_{G}(P)
. One then checks using
Magma
that
|N_{G}(P)|=|H_{0}|
, so
N_{G}(P)=H_{0}
, and we can then check that the bound
|x^{G}\cap H|<i_{2}(H_{0})
is sufficient.

The case where
H
is of type
O^{-}_{2}(q^{2}).2
is very similar. If
|x^{G_{0}}|>\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{6}
, then the trivial bound
|x^{G}\cap H|<|H|
is sufficient, so it remains to prove the claim for
b_{1}
-type involutions. Again we note that
x\in G_{0}
, and so
x^{G}\cap H=x^{G}\cap H_{0}
, and this time
H_{0}=H\cap G_{0}
embeds inside a maximal subgroup
K
of type
{\rm Sp}_{2}(q^{2})
, so
|x^{G}\cap H|\leqslant|x^{G}\cap K|
. However,
K
does not contain any
b_{1}
-type involutions, and therefore
|x^{G}\cap H|=|x^{G}\cap K|=0
, and the claim trivially holds.

Finally, suppose that
H\in\mathscr{S}
is of type
{\rm L}_{2}(q)
. Let
W=\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle
be the natural module for
L={\rm SL}_{2}(q)
. Then the embedding of
L
in
{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)
is the image of the representation
\rho:L\rightarrow{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)
corresponding to the
L
-module
S^{3}(W)
. Let
\hat{x}
be the lift of
x
in
{\rm GSp}_{4}(q)
and first suppose that
x
is unipotent. In this case
x
has associated Jordan decomposition
[J_{2}]
one
W
and we note that

\rho\left(\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\right)=\begin{pmatrix}1&1&1&1\\
0&1&2&3\\
0&0&1&3\\
0&0&0&1\end{pmatrix}=M

with respect to the basis
\{v_{1}^{3},v_{1}^{2}v_{2},v_{1}v_{2}^{2},v_{2}^{3}\}
.
Now, since
M-I_{4}
has rank
3
, we deduce that
M
is not a transvection, and therefore
|x^{G_{0}}|>\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{6}
. Moreover, we know that
|x^{G}\cap H|\leqslant q^{2}-1
, and so the claim holds.

Next suppose that
x\in H
is semisimple and
r
is odd, and let
i
be minimal such that
r|q^{i}-1
. First suppose that
i=1
. Here
\hat{x}
is
L
-conjugate to
[\lambda,\lambda^{-1}]
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}
and we have

\rho([\lambda,\lambda^{-1}])=[\lambda^{3},\lambda,\lambda^{-1},\lambda^{-3}]

If
x
has order
3
, then
C_{G}(x)
is of type
{\rm GL}_{1}(q)\times{\rm Sp}_{2}(q)
. In this case we have

|x^{G_{0}}|\geqslant\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)|}{|{\rm GL}_{1}(q)||{\rm Sp}_{2}(q)|}=q^{3}(q+1)(q^{2}+1)

and

|x^{G_{I}}\cap H|\leqslant\frac{|{\rm GL}_{2}(q)|}{|{\rm GL}_{1}(q)|^{2}}=q(q+1)

and so the bound holds.

If
r\geqslant 5
, then
x
is a regular semisimple element, and we have

|x^{G_{0}}|\geqslant\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)|}{|{\rm GL}_{1}(q)|^{2}}=q^{4}(q+1)^{2}(q^{2}+1).

The case where
i=2
is similar, so we leave the details to the reader.

We finally consider involutions. Let
K
denote the algebraic closure of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
and let
\bar{G}={\rm PSp}_{4}(K)
and
\bar{H}={\rm PSL}_{2}(K)
. Now let
\bar{W}
be the natural module for
{\rm SL}_{2}(K)
and
\bar{\rho}:{\rm SL}_{2}(K)\rightarrow{\rm Sp}_{4}(K)
denote the representation afforded by the
K{\rm SL}_{2}(q)
-module
S^{3}(\bar{W})
. Now note that
\bar{H}
has a unique class of involutions,
y^{\bar{H}}
, and
y
lifts in
{\rm SL}_{2}(K)
to a diagonal matrix
\hat{y}=[\lambda,\lambda^{-1}]
for some
\lambda\in K^{\times}
of order
4
. Then

\bar{\rho}([\lambda,\lambda^{-1}])=[\lambda^{-1},\lambda,\lambda^{-1},\lambda]

with respect to the basis
\{w_{1}^{3},w_{1}^{2}w_{2},w_{1}w_{2}^{2},w_{2}^{3}\}
. Now there are two classes of involutions in
\bar{G}
, one of type
A_{1}A_{1}
and one of type
A_{1}T_{1}
, and the former lift to involutions in
{\rm Sp}_{4}(K)
, whilst the latter lift to elements of order
4
. We therefore deduce that
y
embeds in
\bar{G}
as an
A_{1}T_{1}
involution.

Since both
\bar{H}
and
\bar{G}
are stable under the standard Frobenius morphism
\sigma
, we deduce that if
x\in\bar{H}_{\sigma}
is an involution, then it embeds in
\bar{G}_{\sigma}={\rm Inndiag}(G_{0})
as an involution with centraliser type
{\rm GL}_{2}^{\epsilon}(q)
. We therefore have

|x^{\bar{G}_{\sigma}}\cap H|\leqslant i_{2}({\rm PSL}_{2}(q))=q^{2}-1

and

|x^{G_{0}}|=|x^{\bar{G}_{\sigma}}|\geqslant\frac{|{\rm Sp}_{4}(q)|}{2|{\rm GU}_{2}(q)|}=\frac{1}{2}q^{3}(q-1)(q^{2}+1)

so the claim holds and the proof is complete.
∎

Proposition 4.15
.

Suppose that
G_{0}={\rm PSp}_{4}(q)
for
q\geqslant 4
, and
x\in G
is a field or graph-field automorphism of prime order
r
. Then
\mbox{{\rm fpr}}(x,G/H)<|x^{G_{0}}|^{-3/10}
.

Proof.

The methods we use here are similar to the ones in the proofs of Propositions
4.11
and
4.13
, so we leave the details for the reader.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

This follows by combining Propositions
4.3
,
4.4
,
4.5
,
4.6
,
4.7
,
4.8
,
4.9
,
4.10
,
4.11
,
4.12
,
4.13
,
4.14
, and
4.15
.
∎

5.
Eta function bounds

We will use Proposition
3.14
and variations as heavily as possible in the proof of Theorem
3.1
. In most cases, the bounds in Proposition
3.15
suffice, but in some cases we will need stronger bounds on
T_{G}
, where
T_{G}
is as defined in Definition
3.10
, or even stronger bounds on
\eta_{G}(t)
for some
t\geqslant T_{G}
. We establish those bounds in this section.

Proposition 5.1
.

If
G_{0}={\rm PSp}_{6}(q)
and
q
is even, then
\eta_{G}(1/5)<0.9
.

Proof.

If
q\leqslant 16
, then we check the claim using
Magma
. In particular, we first call
ClassicalClasses("Sp", 6, q)
, and we compute
\alpha=\sum_{i=1}^{k}|x_{i}^{G_{0}}|^{-1/5}
directly, where
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
denote representatives of the prime order classes of
G_{0}
. It now suffices to manually add the contribution
\beta
from outer automorphisms to
\alpha
to obtain an upper bound for
\eta_{G}(1/5)
. We note that

\beta=\sum_{r\in\pi(f)}(r-1)\cdot\left(\frac{|{\rm PSp}_{6}(q)|}{|{\rm PSp}_{6}(q^{1/r})|}\right)^{-1/5}

where recall that
f=\log_{p}(q)
and we confirm that
\alpha+\beta<0.9
for
q\leqslant 16
.

If
q\geqslant 32
, then we use the bound obtained by Burness in the proof of
[
18
, 2.3]
. In particular, we have

(4)

\displaystyle\eta_{G}(t)

\displaystyle<2\left(\frac{1}{2}(q^{6}-1)\right)^{-t}+\left(2+\frac{q}{2}\log(q^{2}-1)\right)\cdot\left(\frac{1}{4}(q+1)^{-1}q^{11}\right)^{-t}

\displaystyle+((q^{2}+1)(q^{6}-1))^{-t}+\left(6+\frac{q}{2}\log(q^{2}-1)\right)\cdot\left(\frac{1}{4}(q+1)^{-1}q^{13}\right)^{-t}

\displaystyle+q^{2}\log(q^{2}+1)\left(\frac{1}{8}q^{14}\right)^{-t}+\frac{1}{3}q^{3}\log(q^{6}-1)\cdot\left(\frac{1}{4}q^{18}\right)^{-t}

\displaystyle+2(\log_{2}q-1)\cdot\log(\log_{2}q+2)\cdot\left(\frac{1}{4}q^{21/2}\right)^{-t}

and one can check that this bound is sufficient if
q\geqslant 32
.

Finally, for
16<q<32
we use a more precise version of (
4
). In particular,
\log(q^{2}-1),\log(q^{2}+1)
and
\log(q^{6}-1)
appear in (
4
) as approximations of the number of prime divisors of
q^{2}-1,q^{2}+1
, and
q^{6}-1
respectively, and replacing those approximations with the exact number of prime divisors of those numbers for
16<q<32
gives the desired bound.
∎

Proposition 5.2
.

If
G_{0}={\rm\Omega}_{7}(q)
and
q
is odd, then
\eta_{G}(1/5)<0.9
.

Proof.

If
q\leqslant 13
, then again we compute directly using
Magma
exactly as in the proof of Proposition
5.1
, so we may assume that
q\geqslant 17
for the remainder of the proof.

Our aim here is to derive a similar bound to the one used in Proposition
5.1
. To do this, we must bound the number of
G_{0}
-conjugacy classes of prime order elements of each type in
G
, as well as the size of each
G_{0}
-conjugacy class, and then group them accordingly. We record the relevant bounds for each type of prime order element in Table
11
, but for the sake of brevity, we only discuss a few cases in detail. The cases not discussed are very similar.

Table
11
is organised as follows: The first row records the type of each prime order element in
G_{0}
. For unipotent elements we record the Jordan form on
V
, for odd order semisimple elements we record the centraliser type, and if
x
is a semisimple involution or a field automorphism, then we use the standard notation from
[
22
, Sections 3.5.2 and 3.5.5]
. The second row records an upper bound
f(x,q)
on the number of
G_{0}
-classes of each type and the third row records a lower bound
g(x,q)
on the
G_{0}
-conjugacy class size of each element.

We first consider unipotent classes. Note that the possible Jordan forms on
V
are as follows:

[J_{7}],[J_{5},J_{1}^{2}],[J_{3},J_{2}^{2}],[J_{3},J_{1}^{4}],[J_{2}^{2},J_{1}^{3}]

We can estimate how many classes correspond to each Jordan decomposition by counting the number of labelled partitions corresponding to that form and consulting
[
22
, 3.5.14]
to determine which
G_{I}
-classes split in
G_{0}
. The
G_{0}
-class bounds in Table
11
are obtained via
[
15
, 3.21]
.

We now look at semisimple classes of odd order. Let
i
be minimal such that
r|q^{i}-1
. Note that since
r
divides
|G|
we must have
i\in\{1,2,3,4,6\}
. The
G_{0}
-class bounds are determined via
[
15
, Tables 3.6 and 3.8]
, so it remains to determine
f(x,q)
. We must treat each possibility in turn. We indicatively discuss two cases in detail here.

Suppose first that
i=6
. In this case
C_{G}(x)
is of type
{\rm O}_{1}(q)\times{\rm GU}_{1}(q^{3})
, and
x
is of the form
[\Lambda,1]
, for some
\sigma
-orbit
\Lambda
. We now know that if
i=6
, then
r|q^{3}+1
, and moreover that there are exactly
\frac{r-1}{6}\leqslant\frac{q^{3}}{6}

\sigma
-orbits, so the number of
G_{0}
-classes of elements of this type in
G
is at most
\frac{q^{3}}{6}\pi(q^{3}+1)\leqslant\frac{q^{3}}{6}\log(q^{3}+1)
.

Now suppose that
i=1
and
C_{G}(x)
is of type
{\rm GL}_{1}(q)^{3}\times{\rm O}_{1}(q)
. In this case
r|q-1
, and since there are exactly
\frac{q-1}{2}
distinct pairs
(\lambda,\lambda^{-1})\in(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times})^{2}
, we conclude that there are at most
\binom{(q-1)/2}{3}\pi(q-1)\leqslant\frac{q^{3}}{48}\log(q-1)

G_{0}
-classes of this type.

We next consider semisimple involutions. By inspecting
[
22
, Table B.8]
we find that there are at most six
G_{0}
-classes of semisimple involutions, namely
t_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
and
t^{\prime}_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
.

Finally, we can infer from
[
22
, 3.5.20]
that for a fixed prime divisor
r
of
\log_{p}q
, there are at most
2(r-1)\leqslant 2\log_{p}q

G_{0}
-classes of field automorphisms of order
r
, and
\log_{p}q
has at most
\log(\log_{p}q+2)
distinct prime divisors, so there are at most
2\log_{p}q\cdot\log(\log_{p}q+2)

G_{0}
-classes of field automorphisms.

Now if
x_{1},\ldots,x_{t}
is a set of representatives for the
G_{0}
-classes of prime order elements in
G
, then we have

\eta_{G}(1/5)\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{t}f(x_{i},q)\cdot g(x_{i},q)^{-1/5}

where
f(x_{i},q)
and
g(x_{i},q)
are recorded in Table
11
for every
i
and one can check that this bound is sufficient for
q\geqslant 17
.

\begin{array}[]{llll}\hline\cr\text{Type of }x&f(x,q)&g(x,q)&\text{Notes}\\
\hline\cr(J_{7})&4&\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{18}&\\
(J_{5},J_{1}^{2})&4&\frac{1}{8}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)^{2}q^{16}&\\
(J_{3},J_{2}^{2})&4&\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{12}&\\
(J_{3}^{2},J_{1})&4&\frac{1}{8}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)^{2}q^{10}&\\
(J_{3},J_{1}^{4})&4&\frac{1}{8}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)^{2}q^{10}&\\
(J_{2}^{2},J_{1}^{3})&2&\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{8}&\\
{\rm GU}_{1}(q^{3})\times{\rm O}_{1}(q)&\frac{1}{6}q^{3}\log(q^{3}+1)&\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{18}&i=6\\
{\rm GU}_{1}(q^{2})\times{\rm O}_{3}(q)&\frac{1}{4}q^{2}\log(q^{2}+1)&\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{14}&i=4\\
{\rm GL}_{1}(q^{3})\times{\rm O}_{1}(q)&\frac{1}{6}(q^{2}+q)\log(q^{2}+q+1)&\frac{1}{2}q^{18}&i=3\\
{\rm GU}_{3}(q)\times{\rm O}_{1}(q)&\frac{1}{2}q\log(q+1)&\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{12}&i=2\\
{\rm GU}_{2}(q)\times{\rm GU}_{1}(q)\times{\rm O}_{1}(q)&\frac{1}{4}q^{2}\log(q+1)&\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)^{2}q^{16}&i=2\\
{\rm GU}_{2}(q)\times{\rm O}_{3}(q)&\frac{1}{2}q\log(q+1)&\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{14}&i=2\\
{\rm GU}_{1}(q)^{2}\times{\rm O}_{3}(q)&\frac{1}{8}q^{2}\log(q+1)&\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)^{2}q^{16}&i=2\\
{\rm GU}_{1}(q)^{3}\times{\rm O}_{1}(q)&\frac{1}{48}q^{3}\log(q+1)&\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)^{3}q^{18}&i=2\\
{\rm GL}_{3}(q)\times{\rm O}_{1}(q)&\frac{1}{2}q\log(q-1)&\frac{1}{2}q^{12}&i=1\\
{\rm GL}_{2}(q)\times{\rm GL}_{1}(q)\times{\rm O}_{1}(q)&\frac{1}{4}q^{2}\log(q-1)&\frac{1}{2}q^{16}&i=1\\
{\rm GL}_{2}(q)\times{\rm O}_{3}(q)&\frac{1}{2}q\log(q-1)&\frac{1}{2}q^{14}&i=1\\
{\rm GL}_{1}(q)^{2}\times{\rm O}_{3}(q)&\frac{1}{8}q^{2}\log(q-1)&\frac{1}{2}q^{16}&i=1\\
{\rm GL}_{1}(q)^{3}\times{\rm O}_{1}(q)&\frac{1}{48}q^{3}\log(q-1)&\frac{1}{2}q^{18}&i=1\\
t_{1}&1&\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{10}&\\
t_{2}&1&\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{12}&\\
t_{3}&1&\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{6}&\\
t^{\prime}_{1}&1&\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{10}&\\
t^{\prime}_{2}&1&\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{12}&\\
t^{\prime}_{3}&1&\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{6}&\\
\phi^{fj/r}&2\log_{p}q\cdot\log(\log_{p}q+2)&\frac{1}{2}q^{21/2}&q=p^{f},\,\,1\leqslant j<r\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 11.
Bounds on conjugacy classes and number of prime order elements in
\Omega_{7}(q)
.

∎

Proposition 5.3
.

If
G_{0}={\rm PSL}^{\epsilon}_{6}(q)
and
q\geqslant 3
, then
\eta_{G}(1/3)<0.979
.

Proof.

We adopt essentially the same strategy as the one used in the proof of
[
18
, 2.3]
. For an element
x\in{\rm PGL}^{\epsilon}_{6}(q)
, we write
\nu(x)
for the codimension of the largest eigenspace of
x
on
V
, and for
1\leqslant s\leqslant 5
we denote the number of distinct
G_{0}
-classes of unipotent (respectively semisimple) prime order elements
x\in G
with
\nu(x)=s
by
k_{s,u}
(respectively
k_{s,s}
).
Moreover, we will write
c_{u}(s)
(respectively
c_{s}(s)
) for the size of the smallest unipotent (respectively semisimple)
G_{0}
-class
y^{G}
with
\nu(y)=s
,
\pi
for the distinct prime divisors of
\log_{p}q
and for
r\in\pi
we will write
\mathcal{F}_{r}
for the set of
G
-classes of field automorphisms of order
r
. Finally, we will denote the set of
G
-classes of involutory graph-field automorphisms by
\mathcal{F}_{gf}
, and we will write
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}
for the
G_{I}
-classes of a graph automorphism of type
{\rm PGSp}_{6}(q),{\rm PGO}^{+}_{6}(q)
, and
{\rm PGO}^{-}_{6}(q)
respectively. Noting that
[
22
, 3.2.14]
tells us that
C_{i}
splits into at most
3

G_{0}
-classes for all
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, we have

\eta_{G}(t)\leqslant\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{F}_{gf}}}|C|^{-t}+\sum_{r\in\pi}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{F}_{r}}|C|^{-t}+\sum_{i=1}^{3}3\left(\frac{|C_{i}|}{3}\right)^{-t}+\sum_{s=1}^{5}(k_{s,u}+k_{s,s})\cdot c(s)^{-t}

and therefore it suffices to find appropriate bounds for
|\mathcal{F}_{r}|
,
|\mathcal{F}_{gf}|
,
|C|
,
|C_{i}|
,
|\pi|
,
k_{s,u}
,
k_{s,s}
,
c_{u}(s)
, and
c_{s}(s)
for
s\in\{1,\ldots,5\}
.

Firstly, we know that every
N\in\mathbb{N}
has at most
\log(N+2)
distinct prime divisors, and so
|\pi|\leqslant\log(\log_{p}q+2)
if
q\neq p
and
|\pi|=0
if
p=q
.
Moreover,
[
44
, 4.9.1]
gives
|\mathcal{F}_{r}|\leqslant(6,q-\epsilon)(r-1)\leqslant 6(\log_{p}q+1)
. Similarly, it follows straight from
[
22
, 3.2.15]
that the
G_{I}
-class of graph-field automorphisms splits into
(6,q^{1/2}-1)

G_{0}
-classes, and so
|\mathcal{F}_{gf}|\leqslant 6
.

We now consider unipotent elements. Note that
(8)
in
[
15
]
tells us that there are at most
2^{s}
unipotent
{\rm Inndiag}(G_{0})
-classes with
\nu(x)=s
for
s\in\{1,\ldots,,5\}
and each class can split to at most
6
classes in
G_{0}
. Therefore,
k_{s,u}<6\cdot 2^{s}
. Moreover, we can infer from
[
15
, 3.22]
and
[
22
, 3.2.7]
that

c_{u}(s)>\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{2s(6-s)}\,\text{if}\,s\in\{1,2\}\\
\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{6s}\,\text{if}\,s=3\\
\frac{1}{12}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{6s}\,\text{if}\,s\in\{4,5\}\\
\end{cases}

Similarly, for semisimple elements
[
15
, 3.40]
tells us that there are at most
q^{s+1}
prime order elements of nu-value
s
of order
r
, so now it suffices to bound the possibilities for
r
to bound
k_{s,s}
. Note that
r
must divide
\prod_{i=1}^{3}(q^{i}-\epsilon)(q^{2i}-1)<q^{22}
, and since every integer
N>6
has at most
\log N
divisors, we conclude that
k_{s,s}<22\log q\cdot q^{s+1}
.

Finally,
[
15
, 3.36-3.37]
gives

c_{s}(s)>\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{2s(6-s)}\,\text{if}\,s\in\{1,2\}\\
\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{6s}\,\text{if}\,s=3\\
\frac{1}{6}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)^{\frac{s}{6-s}}q^{6s}\,\text{if}\,s\in\{4,5\}\\
\end{cases}

and one can check that those bounds are sufficient for
q\geqslant 11
. If
3\leqslant q\leqslant 9
, then we can compute
k_{s,s}
, and
k_{s,u}
precisely and obtain the result this way. For example, let us consider the case
(\epsilon,q,s)=(+,8,4)
. If
\nu(x)=4
for a unipotent element
x\in G
, then the Jordan decomposition of
x
on
V
must comprise of two blocks. But one cannot have an involution in
G
whose Jordan decomposition only has two blocks, so
k_{4,u}=0
.

Now suppose that
x
is semisimple and let
i
be minimal such that
r|q^{i}-1
, so
i\in\{1,\ldots,6\}
. Now it is easy to see that if
i\geqslant 4
, then the lift
\hat{x}
of
x
in
{\rm GL}(V)
is of the form
[\Lambda,I_{6-i}]
for some
\sigma
-orbit
\Lambda
, so
\nu(x)=5
, a contradiction, and so
i\geqslant 3
. If
i=3
, then since
\nu(x)=4
we must have
\hat{x}=[\Lambda^{2}]
for some
\sigma
-orbit
\lambda
. Moreover, by inspecting the prime divisors of
|G_{I}|
, we find that
r=73
, and so, as explained in
[
22
, Section 3.2.1]
, there are exactly
\frac{73-1}{3}=24
distinct
\sigma
-orbits for
i=3
, so we get precisely
24

G_{0}
-classes with
\nu(x)=4
.

Now assume that
i=2
. Note that for all the eigenvalues of
\hat{x}
to occur with multiplicity at least
2
,
\hat{x}
must be of the form
[\Lambda^{2},I_{2}]
. Moreover, the assumption
i=2
implies that
r=3
, and there is a unique
\sigma
-orbit when
r=3
.

Finally assume that
i=1
, so
r=7
. Since
7\not|6
, we find that
\hat{x}=[\lambda I_{2},\lambda^{\alpha}I_{2},\lambda^{\beta}I_{2}]
for some non-trivial
7
-th root of unity
\lambda
and
1<\alpha,\beta\leqslant 6
, so there are
\binom{7}{3}=35
possibilities for
x
. Therefore
k_{4,s}=24+1+35=90\ll 22\log 8\cdot 8^{5}
. The argument in all remaining cases is similar, so we omit the details.
∎

6.
Proof of Theorem
3.1

Let
\mathcal{C}
denote the set of simple classical groups. We will divide the possibilities for
G_{0}\in\mathcal{C}
into the following six collections, according to the method we use to prove Theorem
3.1
, and we will handle each one in turn:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{1}

\displaystyle=\{{\rm PSp}_{8}(q),{\rm PSL}_{8}^{\epsilon}(q),{\rm P\Omega}^{\epsilon}_{10}(q)\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{2}

\displaystyle=\{{\rm PSL}^{\epsilon}_{6}(q)\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{3}

\displaystyle=\{{\rm PSp}_{6}(q)\,:\,q\text{ even}\}\cup\{{\rm\Omega}_{7}(q)\,:\,q\text{ odd}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{4}

\displaystyle=\{{\rm PSL}_{3}^{\epsilon}(q),{\rm PSL}_{4}^{\epsilon}(q),{\rm PSL}_{5}^{\epsilon}(q),{\rm PSp}_{4}(q)\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{5}

\displaystyle=\{{\rm PSL}_{2}(q)\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{6}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{C}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2}\cup\mathcal{A}_{3}\cup\mathcal{A}_{4}\cup\mathcal{A}_{5})

Proposition 6.1
.

The conclusion to Theorem
3.1
holds for the groups with
G_{0}\in\mathcal{A}_{6}
.

Proof.

In this case, it follows immediately from Theorem
3.9
and Propositions
3.14
and
3.15
that any non-standard
4
-tuple
\tau
of
G
is regular, and moreover Proposition
3.13
implies that
\mathbb{P}(G,4)\rightarrow 1
as
|G|\rightarrow\infty
.
∎

Proposition 6.2
.

The conclusion to Theorem
3.1
holds for the groups with
G_{0}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

Proof.

If
G_{0}={\rm PSp}_{8}(q)
and
q=2
, then the claim can be verified using
Magma
, using the function
RegTuplesPlus()
, so we may assume for the remainder of the proof that
q\geqslant 3
if
G_{0}={\rm PSp}_{8}(q)
.

Propositions
4.3
,
4.4
, and
4.5
, together with Theorem
3.9
, tell us that
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<|x^{G_{0}}|^{-1/3}
for every core-free subgroup
H
of
G
and all
x\in G
of prime order. Moreover, Proposition
3.15
gives
T_{G}<1/3
, and so if
\tau=(H_{1},\ldots,H_{4})
is any non-standard tuple of
G
, then Proposition
3.14
implies that
\tau
is regular. Finally, Proposition
3.13
implies that
\mathbb{P}(G,4)\rightarrow 1
as
|G|\rightarrow\infty
.
∎

Proposition 6.3
.

The conclusion to Theorem
3.1
holds for the groups with
G_{0}\in\mathcal{A}_{2}
.

Proof.

If
q=2
, then as described in Section
2.2
, we can check that all non-standard
4
-tuples (or
5
-tuples if
G=U_{6}(2).2
) are regular using the
Magma
function
RegTuplesPlus()
. If
G=U_{6}(2).2
, then we can also verify that the only non-regular
4
tuple is
(H,H,H,H)
for
H={\rm U}_{4}(3).2^{2}
as the same function outputs all the non-regular
4
-tuples. We may therefore assume that
q\geqslant 3
for the remainder. Now note that Proposition
4.6
, together with Theorem
3.9
implies that

\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)\leqslant\eta_{G}(1/3)+\alpha,

where

\alpha=\frac{q-1}{2}\log(q-1)\frac{(q^{6}(q+1)(q^{2}+1)(q^{3}+1))^{4}}{(q^{9}(q-\epsilon)(q^{3}-\epsilon)(q^{5}-\epsilon))^{3}}

for any non-standard
4
-tuple
\tau
of
G
. In view of Proposition
5.3
, it suffices to show that
\alpha<0.021

for all
q\geqslant 3
to prove that
\tau
is regular, and one can easily check that this is true. Moreover, we see that
\alpha\rightarrow 0
as
q\rightarrow\infty
, and this combined with Proposition
3.13
implies that
\mathbb{P}(G,4)\rightarrow 1
as
|G|\rightarrow\infty
.
∎

Proposition 6.4
.

The conclusion to Theorem
3.1
holds for the groups with
G_{0}\in\mathcal{A}_{3}
.

Proof.

We can check that all non-standard tuples of
G
are regular if
q\leqslant 5
using
Magma
, so we may assume that
q\geqslant 7
for the remainder of the proof. Let
\tau=(H_{1},\ldots,H_{4})
be a non-standard tuple of
G
. Note that if
H_{i}
is of type
G_{2}(q)
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,4\}
, then it follows straight from
[
18
, Theorem 1]
that
\tau
is regular, so we may assume that at least one of the
H_{i}
’s is not of type
G_{2}(q)
, say
H_{1}
without loss of generality.
Moreover,
[
17
, 2.13]
implies that if
H
is of type
G_{2}(q)
and
x\in H
has prime order, then unless
x
is unipotent with Jordan form
[J_{2}^{2},J_{1}^{3}]
or
[J_{3}^{2},J_{1}]
, we have
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<|x^{G_{0}}|^{-0.268}
.

Now let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{t}
be a set of representatives of the prime order conjugacy classes of
G
, and without loss of generality assume that
x_{t-1}
and
x_{t}
are the representatives of the unipotent
G
-classes with Jordan form
[J_{2}^{2},J_{1}^{3}]
and
[J_{3}^{2},J_{1}]
respectively. Propositions
4.7
and
4.8
tell us that
{\rm fpr}(x_{j},G/H_{1})<|x_{j}^{G}|^{-0.4}
for all
j\in[t]
, whilst
{\rm fpr}(x_{j},G/H_{k})<|x_{j}|^{-0.268}
for
k\neq 1
and
j\in[t-2]
. Finally, for
j\in\{t-1,t-2\}

{\rm fpr}(x_{j},G/H_{k})
can be recovered from
[
17
, Tables 2.4 and 2.5]
. Combining all of this we get

\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{t}|x_{i}^{G_{0}}|^{-3\cdot 0.268-0.4+1}+\alpha+\beta

where

\alpha=|x_{t-1}^{G}|^{0.6}\prod_{i=2}^{4}\frac{|{x_{t-1}}^{G}\cap H_{i}|}{|{x_{t-1}}^{G}|}=\frac{(q^{6}-1)^{3}}{((q^{2}-1)(q^{6}-1))^{12/5}}

and

\beta=|x_{t}^{G}|^{0.6}\prod_{i=2}^{4}\frac{|{x_{t}}^{G}\cap H_{i}|}{|{x_{t}}^{G}|}=\frac{2^{12/5}(q^{2}(q^{2}-1)(q^{6}-1))^{3}}{(q^{6}(q-1)(q^{4}-1)(q^{6}-1))^{12/5}}.

Therefore,

\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)\leqslant\eta_{G}(1/5)+\alpha+\beta

and in view of Propositions
5.1
and
5.2
, it suffices to show that

\alpha+\beta<0.1,

to show that
\tau
is regular. We can easily check that this is true.

Moreover, noting that
\alpha,\beta\rightarrow 0
as
q\rightarrow\infty
and combining this with Proposition
3.13
, we find that
\mathbb{P}(G,\tau)\rightarrow 1
as
|G|\rightarrow\infty
. Finally, if
\sigma
is a
6
-tuple of
G
all of whose entries are of type
G_{2}(q)
, then
[
25
, Theorem 3]
implies that
\mathbb{P}(G,\sigma)\rightarrow 1
as
|G|\rightarrow\infty
, and so
\mathbb{P}(G,6)\rightarrow 1
as
|G|\rightarrow\infty
, as required.

∎

Proposition 6.5
.

The conclusion to Theorem
3.1
holds for the groups with
G_{0}\in\mathcal{A}_{4}
.

Proof.

Let
\tau=(H_{1},\ldots,H_{4})
be a non-standard tuple. If
G_{0}={\rm PSL}^{\epsilon}_{5}(q)
or
{\rm PSL}^{\epsilon}_{4}(q)
, then in view of Propositions
4.9
,
4.10
, and
4.11
, we may write

(5)

\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)\leqslant\alpha_{1}\cdot\beta_{1}^{-4/5}+\alpha_{2}\cdot\beta_{2}^{-1/5}+\alpha_{3}\cdot\beta_{3}^{-1/5}

where
\alpha_{1}
denotes the number of prime order
G_{0}
-classes of unipotent and semisimple elements,
\alpha_{2}
denotes the number of
G_{0}
-classes of field and graph-field automorphisms and
\alpha_{3}
denotes the number of
G_{0}
-classes of graph automorphisms respectively, and

\displaystyle\beta_{1}

\displaystyle=\min\{|x^{G_{0}}|\,:\,x\in{\rm Inndiag}(G_{0}),|x|\in\pi(|G|)\}

\displaystyle\beta_{2}

\displaystyle=\min\{|x^{G_{0}}|\,:\,x\in\mathcal{F}\}

\displaystyle\beta_{3}

\displaystyle=\min\{|x^{G_{0}}|\,:\,x\in\mathcal{G}\}

where
\mathcal{F}
denotes the set of prime order field and graph-field automorphisms in
G
and
\mathcal{G}
the set of involutory graph automorphisms in
G
. We will derive bounds for
\alpha_{i}
and
\beta_{i}
for
i\in[3]
for each possibility for
G_{0}
separately.

First suppose that
G_{0}={\rm PSL}^{\epsilon}_{5}(q)
. If
q\leqslant 4
, then the regularity of
\tau
can be verified using
Magma
, so we may assume that
q\geqslant 5
. In this case, we can infer from
[
41
, Table 3]
that the total number of conjugacy classes in
{\rm PGL}_{5}^{\epsilon}(q)
is bounded above by
q^{4}+8q^{3}
. We now note that the only
G
-class of unipotent elements that potentially splits in
G_{0}
is the one corresponding to the Jordan form
[J_{5}]
, and
[
22
, 3.2.7, 3.3.10]
imply that it splits into at most
5

G_{0}
-classes. Therefore, we may take
\alpha_{1}\leqslant q^{4}+8q^{3}+4
. Moreover,
[
44
, 4.9.1]
implies that there are at most

\left(5,\frac{q-\epsilon}{q^{1/r}-\epsilon}\right)(r-1)|\pi(\log_{p}(q))|\leqslant 5\log_{p}q\cdot\log(\log_{p}q+2)

G_{0}
-classes of field automorphisms and similarly at most
5
distinct
G_{0}
-classes of involutory graph-field automorphisms by
[
22
, 3.2.15]
. We also get at most
5

G_{0}
-classes of involutory graph automorphisms by
[
22
, 3.2.14, 3.3.17]
, so we have

\alpha_{2}\leqslant 5+5\log_{p}q\cdot\log(\log_{p}q+2)\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\alpha_{3}\leqslant 5.

Finally, it follows straight from Lemma
3.18
that
\beta_{1}>\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{8}
,
\beta_{2}>\frac{1}{10}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{12}
, and
\beta_{3}>\frac{1}{10}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{14}
, and by substituting the above bounds in (
5
) we find that
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)<1
for all
q\geqslant 5
, as required.

Next assume that
G_{0}={\rm PSL}^{\epsilon}_{4}(q)
. If
q\leqslant 9
, then the regularity of
\tau
can again be verified using
Magma
, so we may assume that
q\geqslant 11
for the remainder. Again, from
[
41
, Table 3]
we see that there are at most
\alpha_{1}\leqslant q^{3}+8q^{2}

G_{I}
-classes of unipotent and semisimple elements, and the only classes that possibly split in
G_{0}
are the class of unipotent elements corresponding to the Jordan decompositions
[J_{4}]
and
[J_{2}^{2}]
, so
\alpha_{1}\leqslant q^{3}+8q^{2}+4
. Moreover,
[
44
, 4.9.1]
again tells us that the contribution to
\alpha_{2}
from field automorphisms is at most
4\log_{p}q\cdot\log(\log_{p}q+2)
, and
[
22
, 3.2.15]
tells us that there are at most
4

G_{0}
-classes of involutory graph-field automorphisms. Finally, we can infer from
[
22
, 3.2.14, 3.3.17]
that each
G_{I}
-class of graph automorphisms splits into at most
2

G_{0}
-classes, and so

\alpha_{2}\leqslant 4\log_{p}q\cdot\log(\log_{p}q+2)+4\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\alpha_{3}\leqslant 6

Now Lemma
3.18
gives
\beta_{1}>\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{6}
,
\beta_{2}>\frac{1}{8}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{15/2}
, and
\beta_{3}>\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{4}
. Substituting into (
5
) we get
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)<1
for
q\geqslant 32
. If
q\leqslant 32
, then we replace the upper bound on
\alpha_{1}
, by the tighter bound
(4,q-1)\log_{p}q\cdot|\pi(\log_{p}q)|
and we compute the exact contribution from involutory graph automorphisms, and we again verify that
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)<1
.

Our strategy is similar for the cases where
G_{0}={\rm PSL}_{3}(q)
or
{\rm PSp}_{4}(q)
. Let
\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\alpha_{3},\beta_{3}
be defined as above, and this time let
\alpha_{2}
be the number of
G_{0}
-classes of field automorphisms of odd order and

\beta_{2}\min\{|x^{G_{0}}|\,:\,x\in\mathcal{F},|x|\neq 2\}.

Finally, let
\alpha_{4}
denote the number of
G_{0}
-classes of involutory field and graph-field automorphisms and

\beta_{4}=\min\{|x^{G_{0}}|\,:\,x\in\mathcal{F},|x|=2\}.

Then Propositions
4.12
and
4.13
give us

(6)

\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)\leqslant\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}^{-4/5}+\alpha_{2}\beta_{2}^{-1/5}+\alpha_{3}\beta_{3}^{-1/5}+\alpha_{4}\beta_{4}^{-1/5}

so it suffices to find sufficient bounds for
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}
for
i\in[4]
. We can again infer from
[
41
, Table 3]
that there are at most
q^{2}+8q
distinct prime order classes in
G_{I}
, none of which split in
G_{0}
, so
\alpha_{1}\leqslant q^{2}+8q
. Moreover, the same arguments we used when
G_{0}={\rm PSL}_{5}(q)
gives
\alpha_{2}\leqslant 3\log_{p}q\cdot\log(\log_{p}q+2)
,
\alpha_{3}\leqslant 3
and
\alpha_{4}\leqslant 6
. Next, as usual we obtain

\displaystyle\beta_{1}

\displaystyle>\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{4}

\displaystyle\beta_{2}

\displaystyle>\frac{1}{6}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{16/3}

\displaystyle\beta_{3}

\displaystyle>\frac{1}{6}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{4}

\displaystyle\beta_{4}

\displaystyle>\frac{1}{6}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{4}

via Lemma
3.18
and one can check that these bounds are sufficient for
q\geqslant 67
. If
q\leqslant 25
, then we verify the claim using
Magma
, so it is left to verify that
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)<1
for
27\leqslant q\leqslant 64
. In this case, the tighter bounds
\alpha_{2}\leqslant(3,q-\epsilon)\log_{p}q|\pi(\log_{p}q)|
,
\alpha_{3}\leqslant(3,q-\epsilon)
and
\alpha_{4}\leqslant 2(3,q-\epsilon)
are sufficient.

Finally, suppose that
G_{0}={\rm PSp}_{4}(q)
. We verify the claim using
Magma
if
q\leqslant 11
so we may assume that
q\geqslant 13
for the remainder. Let
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}
be defined as in the case
G_{0}={\rm PSL}_{3}^{\epsilon}(q)
for
i\in[4]
. Then Propositions
4.14
and
4.15
imply that (
6
) still applies, so we will again derive sufficient bounds for
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}
for
i\in[4]
.

By inspecting
[
41
, Table 3]
we deduce that there are at most
q^{2}+12q
distinct
G_{I}
-classes of unipotent and semisimple elements and each unipotent class can split into at most
4

G_{0}
-classes, so we get
\alpha_{1}\leqslant q^{2}+12q+9
. Moreover, Lemma
3.18
implies that
\beta_{1}>\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{4}
. Next, using
[
22
, 3.4.15]
we find

\alpha_{2}\leqslant(2,q-1)(r-1)\pi(\log_{p}q)\leqslant 2\log_{p}q\cdot\log(\log_{p}q+2).

and once again via Lemma
3.18
, we get
\beta_{2}>\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{q}{q+1}\right)q^{20/3}
. Similarly, we deduce that there are at most
2

G_{0}
-classes of involutory field automorphisms by inspecting
[
22
, 3.4.15]
, and
[
22
, 3.4.16]
tells us that there is an additional
G_{0}
class of involutory graph-field automorphisms when
p=2
, so
\alpha_{4}\leqslant 3
. Finally, we get
\beta_{4}>\frac{1}{4}q^{5}
from Lemma
3.18
, and trivially,
\alpha_{3}=\beta_{3}=0
, since
G
contains no graph automorphisms. One can check that those bounds are sufficient for
q\geqslant 13
.

Finally, we observe that
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)\rightarrow 0
as
q\rightarrow\infty
in all cases, so
\mathbb{P}(G,4)\rightarrow 1
as
|G|\rightarrow\infty
.
∎

Proposition 6.6
.

The conclusion to Theorem
3.1
holds for the groups with
G_{0}\in\mathcal{A}_{5}
.

Proof.

First we note that the claim can easily be verified using
Magma
for
q\leqslant 31
, so we may assume for the remainder of the proof that
q\geqslant 32
. If
\tau=(H_{1},\ldots,H_{4})
is a non-standard tuple of
G
, then Proposition
3.17
yields

\hat{Q}(G,\tau)\leqslant|G|\cdot\left(\frac{4}{3q}\right)^{4}+\alpha\cdot\left(\frac{2+q^{1/2}(q^{1/2}+1)}{q^{1/2}(q+1)}\right)^{4}

where
\alpha
denotes the number of involutory field automorphisms. We now note that

\alpha\leqslant\frac{|{\rm PGL}_{2}(q)|}{|{\rm PGL}_{2}(q^{1/2})|}

and we can now check that the above bound gives
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)<1
for all
q\geqslant 32
. Moreover, we can easily see that
\widehat{G}(G,\tau)\rightarrow 0
as
q\rightarrow\infty
, and so
\mathbb{P}(G,4)\rightarrow 1
as
|G|\rightarrow\infty
, as required.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

This follows by combining Propositions
6.1
,
6.2
,
6.3
,
6.4
,
6.5
,
6.6
.
∎

7.
Preliminaries on exceptional groups

For the remainder of the paper, we may assume that
G
is an almost simple exceptional group of Lie type with socle
G_{0}
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, where
q=p^{f}
for some prime
p
and
\tau
is a
6
-tuple of core-free subgroups of
G
. As in the previous section, our main aim is to prove Theorem
1
. In particular, we will show that the stronger statement below holds, which in combination with Theorem
3.1
for classical groups, completes the proof of Theorem
1
.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
G
be a finite almost simple exceptional group of Lie type and let
\tau
be a
6
-tuple of core-free subgroups of
G
. Then
\tau
is regular, and moreover,
Q(G,\tau)\rightarrow 0
as
q\rightarrow\infty
.

We start by fixing some initial notation. As before, we will write
\bar{G}
for the ambient algebraic group (of the same type as
G
) over the algebraic closure of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, so
G_{0}=(G_{\sigma})^{\prime}
for some appropriate Steinberg endomorphism
\sigma
. Also, for a closed subgroup
\bar{H}
of
\bar{G}
, we will write
\bar{H}^{0}
to denote the connected component of
\bar{H}
containing the identity. We will also be using the standard notation
E_{6}^{\epsilon}(q)
for the finite exceptional groups of type
E_{6}
, where
E_{6}^{+}(q)=E_{6}(q)
and
E_{6}^{-}(q)={}^{2}E_{6}(q)
.

We will denote the number of elements of order
r
in some group
H
by
i_{r}(H)
. We will often denote the set of prime numbers at most
r
by
\pi_{r}
and the number of odd primes at most
r
by
\pi^{\prime}_{r}
.

For the remainder of the paper we will write
\mathcal{M}_{G}
for the set of core-free maximal subgroups of
G
and
\mathcal{P}_{G}
for the set of parabolic maximal subgroups of
G
, and finally, following the notation in
[
51
]
, we will often write
x=s,u,\phi,\tau,\phi\tau
to mean that
x
is semisimple, unipotent, a field, graph, or graph-field automorphism respectively.

7.1.
Subgroup structure

We will repeatedly refer to standard sources in the literature for information regarding maximal subgroups of finite exceptional groups. We recall some important results in this section. We write
\mathcal{M}_{G}
as a union
\mathcal{C}\cup\mathcal{S}
of two collections, where
\mathcal{C}
is the collection of maximal subgroups
H
of
G
of the following types:

(a)

H=N_{G}(\bar{H}_{\sigma})
for some maximal
\sigma
-stable positive dimensional closed subgroup
\bar{H}
of
\bar{G}
;

(b)

H
is of the same type as
G
. That is,
H
is either a subfield subgroup of a twisted version of
G
;

(c)

H
is the normaliser of some exotic
r
-local subgroup for some prime
r\neq p
;

(d)

G_{0}=E_{8}(q)
,
p>5
, and
{\rm soc}(H)=A_{5}\times A_{6}
.

A description of the subgroups in
(a)
and
(b)
can be found in
[
60
]
and the groups in
(c)
have been classified (up to conjugacy) in
[
31
]
. Finally, the group in case
(d)
was first described by Borovik in
[
9
]
and is known as the
Borovik subgroup
. Note that the work of Borovik in
[
9
]
in combination with the work of Liebeck and Seitz in
[
58
]
implies that all maximal subgroups in
\mathcal{S}
are almost simple.

At the time of writing, all maximal subgroups of
G
have been determined up to conjugacy unless
G_{0}=E_{7}(q)
or
E_{8}(q)
. We now give a few details for each case and the original sources where the information can be found:

•

\mathbf{G_{0}={}^{2}B_{2}(q):}
In this case, Suzuki determines the maximal subgroups of
G
up to conjugacy in
[
74
]
and Bray, Hold and Roney-Dougal record them in
[
12
, Table 8.16]
.

•

\mathbf{G_{0}={}^{2}G_{2}(q)^{\prime}\textbf{ or }{}^{3}D_{4}(q):}
Here the subgroups in
\mathcal{M}_{G}
up to conjugacy are determined by Kleidman in
[
48
]
and
[
50
]
respectively, and unless
q=3
, they are listed in
[
12
, Tables 8.43, 8.51]
.

•

\mathbf{G_{0}={}^{2}F_{4}(q)^{\prime}:}
In the case
q\geqslant 8
the maximal subgroups of
G
are determined by Malle in
[
66
]
apart from the omission of three classes of maximal subgroups isomorphic to
{\rm PGL}_{2}(13)
when
q=8
. This is straightened out in
[
33
, Remark 4.11]
.

If
q=2
, then the subgroups in
\mathcal{M}_{G}
are determined up to conjugacy by Wilson in
[
76
]
with the exception of the omission of a unique class of maximal subgroups isomorphic to
{\rm U}_{3}(2).2
.

•

\mathbf{G_{0}=G_{2}(q)^{\prime}:}
Here the members of
\mathcal{M}_{G}
are determined up to conjugacy in
[
32
]
for even
q
and
[
48
]
for odd
q
. For
q=2^{f}
and
f>1
one can find them listed
[
12
, Table 8.30]
and for odd
q
they are listed in
[
12
, Tables 8.41, 8.42]
.

•

\mathbf{G_{0}=F_{4}(q):}\textbf{ or }\mathbf{{E}_{6}^{\epsilon}(q):}
The definitive source for those groups is
[
33
]
, where Craven determines their maximal subgroups up to conjugacy. The members of
\mathcal{C}
are listed in
[
33
, Table 7, 8]
and the members of
\mathcal{S}
are listed in
[
33
, Tables 1-3]
.

•

\mathbf{G_{0}=E_{7}(q):}
The members of
\mathcal{M}_{G}
are discussed in
[
34
]
in this case. In particular, the members of
\mathcal{C}
are listed in
[
34
, Table 4.1]
up to conjugacy, and as stated in
[
34
, Theorem ]
, the collection
\mathcal{S}
consists of the groups listed in
[
34
, Table 1.1]
up to conjugacy possibly together with some additional subgroups with socle
{\rm L}_{2}(q^{\prime})
for
q\in\{7,8,9,13\}
.

•

\mathbf{G_{0}=E_{8}(q):}
The subgroups in
\mathcal{C}
have been completely determined up to conjugacy. In particular, the parabolic and maximal rank subgroups of the form
N_{G}(\bar{H}_{\sigma})
are listed in
[
56
, Tables 5.1 and 5.2]
, the subfield subgroups and exotic local subgroups are listed in
[
57
, Table 1]
, and finally for the non-maximal rank subgroups of the form
N_{G}(\bar{H}_{\sigma})
, one can find the possibilities for
{\rm soc}(\bar{H}_{\sigma})
listed in
[
31
, Table 3]
. If
p>5
then
\mathcal{C}
also contains the Borovik subgroup.

It is currently an open problem to determine the members of
\mathcal{S}
even up to isomorphism. However, significant progress has been made towards a classification. The current state of the art regarding the groups in class
\mathcal{S}
will not be relevant to us, but we refer the reader to
[
24
, p.19]
for more information.

7.2.
Conjugacy classes

We will repeatedly be using results from the literature about conjugacy classes of elements in finite exceptional groups. In this section we point the reader to the sources where the information is recorded.

•

\mathbf{G_{0}={}^{2}B_{2}(q):}
In this case the conjugacy classes of elements of
G
are determined in
[
74
]
.

•

\mathbf{G_{0}={}^{2}G_{2}(q):}
Detailed information on its conjugacy classes can be found in
[
75
]
.

•

\mathbf{G_{0}={}^{3}D_{4}(q):}
The conjugacy classes of
G
are recorded in
[
37
]
and
[
71
]
.

•

\mathbf{G_{0}={}^{2}F_{4}(q)^{\prime}:}
In this case, the
G_{0}
-classes are described by Shinoda in
[
70
]
.

•

\mathbf{G_{0}=G_{2}(q)^{\prime}:}
If
p\geqslant 5
then the conjugacy classes of
G
are determined in
[
30
]
and if
p<5
, then Enomoto determines the
G
-classes in
[
38
]
.

•

\mathbf{G_{0}=F_{4}(q):}
Information on the semisimple classes of
G_{0}
is recorded in
[
3
, Table 23]
. For unipotent classes the Foulkes functions for
G_{0}
are given to us by Lübeck, and we discuss further in Section
9
how we use those to compute fixed point ratios.

•

\mathbf{G_{0}=E_{6}^{\epsilon}(q),E_{7}(q)}\textbf{ or }\mathbf{E_{8}(q)}
: Our standard reference for semisimple classes is
[
39
]
when
G_{0}=E_{6}^{\epsilon}(q)
and
E_{7}(q)
and
[
40
]
when
G_{0}=E_{8}(q)
, where Fleischmann and Janiszczak describe the conjugacy classes of the simply connected groups of type
E_{6},E_{7}
, and
E_{8}
respectively. For unipotent elements we again use the Foulkes functions given to us by Lübeck in
[
64
]
.

Recall that
\tau
is a
6
-tuple of core-free subgroups of
G
. As with classical groups, our central aim will be to show that
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)<1
, and in particular bound
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)
by a function of
q
that tends to zero as
q
tends to infinity. We adopt a rather different approach on how we derive an upper bound for
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)
, depending on the socle type of
G
and so we will divide the various possibilities for
G_{0}
into the following three collections, according to the proof strategy we use, and we will handle each one in turn:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}

\displaystyle=\{{}^{2}G_{2}(q)^{\prime},{}^{2}B_{2}(q),{}^{2}F_{4}(q)^{\prime},{}^{3}D_{4}(q),G_{2}(q)^{\prime}\}

(7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{2}

\displaystyle=\{E_{6}^{\epsilon}(q),E_{7}(q),F_{4}(q)\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{3}

\displaystyle=\{E_{8}(q)\}

In all the above cases, our proof will rely on obtaining sufficient fixed point ratio estimates on all maximal subgroups of
G
, and we will be working with the bound

\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)\leqslant\sum_{x\in\pi(|G|)}f(x,q),

where

f(x,q)=\max\{{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\,:\,H\in\mathcal{M}_{G}\}

for each choice of prime order element
x\in G
. Note that
f(x,q)
is independent of the choice of conjugacy class representative. That is,
f(x,q)=f(x^{g},q)
for all
g\in G
, so if
x_{1},\ldots,x_{t}
is a list of representatives of the prime order conjugacy classes of
G
, then we will be working with the expression:

\sum_{i=1}^{t}|x_{i}^{G}|\cdot f(x_{i},q).

Let
H
be a core-free maximal subgroup of
G
. The definitive source on bounds on
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)
is
[
51
]
. However, the bounds in
[
51
]
will not always be sufficient for our purposes.

When
H
is non-parabolic, our standard approach will be to obtain sufficient bounds by inspecting the proofs of
[
25
]
and
[
19
]
when
H
. In certain rare cases, we will also need to improve existing bounds in the literature. For example, if
G_{0}=F_{4}(2)
and
|H|>2^{22}
, then we will use the Character Table library in
GAP
to compute the precise value of
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)
for all
x\in G
More details can be found in the proof of Proposition
8.4
.

If
G
has socle in
\mathcal{E}_{2}
, then obtaining fixed point ratio bounds that hold for all non-parabolic subgroups of
G
is slightly more involved than in other cases, due to the larger number of possibilities for
H
, and so we carefully explain how to do this in Section
8
. Surprisingly, if
G_{0}=E_{8}(q)
, then obtaining sufficient bounds is not as troublesome and can be done by careful inspection of
[
25
]
. These bounds may be of independent interest, so we record them in Tables
12
,
13
,
14
,
15
, and
16
for the reader’s convenience.

Our approach when
H
is parabolic is rather different and we give detailed information on how we obtain bounds on
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)
in Section
9
. If
G_{0}\in\mathcal{E}_{1}
, then the bounds in
[
51
, Theorems 1–2]
are always sufficient for unipotent and semisimple elements, and better bounds can be obtained by inspecting the proof of
[
51
, 6.1]
for field and graph-field automorphisms, if necessary. This is however not true if
G_{0}\in\mathcal{E}_{2}\cup\mathcal{E}_{3}
, where we will heavily use
[
51
, 3.2]
and
[
64
]
to derive better estimates for
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)
that are sensitive to
{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}
for a given prime order element
x\in G
. For a unipotent element
x\in G
, it is always possible to compute precise values for
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)
using Lübeck’s values of the Foulkes functions for
G_{0}

[
64
]
. Now suppose that
x\in G
is semisimple. If
G_{0}\in\mathcal{E}_{3}
, then an upper bound on the formula given by
[
51
, 3.2]
will be sufficient for our purposes but if
G_{0}\in\mathcal{E}_{2}
, then we will need to compute the precise value of
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)
to obtain our result. For field and graph-field automorphisms we will always appeal to a statement extracted from the proof of
[
51
, 6.1]
(see Proposition
9.4
). Finally, for graph automorphisms when
G_{0}=E_{6}^{\epsilon}(q)
, we derive appropriate bounds in Proposition
9.5
when
q
is odd using the approach described in
[
51
, 6.4]
, which we record in Table
18
, and for even
q
, we consult
[
25
, Table 6]
and for the reader’s convenience we record this information in Table
17
. We give more details in Section
9
.

Organisation.
The remainder of the paper is organised as follows: In Section
8
we give fixed point ratio bounds for non-parabolic actions for groups with socle in
\mathcal{E}_{2}
. In Section
9
, we explain how we derive fixed point ratio estimates on parabolic subgroups for groups with socle in
\mathcal{E}_{2}
and
\mathcal{E}_{3}
. Finally, in Sections
10.1
,
10.2
, and
10.3
, we prove Theorem
7.1
for groups with socle in
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2}
and
\mathcal{E}_{3}
, respectively.

8.
Fixed point ratios for non-parabolic actions

In this section we establish fixed point ratio bounds for non-parabolic actions of groups with socle in
\mathcal{E}_{2}
. In particular, for every prime order element in
G
we obtain a value
g(x,q)
such that

{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant g(x,q)

for every
H\in\mathcal{M}_{G}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{G}
and we record those values in Tables
12
,
13
,
14
,
15
, and
16
. As mentioned above,
g(x,q)
is readily obtained by inspecting the proofs of
[
25
]
in the majority of cases, and we carry out the necessary computations for the cases where more work is required.

For the remainder of the section, we fix
G
with
G_{0}\in\mathcal{E}_{2}
and
H\in\mathcal{M}_{G}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{G}
, and we proceed by establishing the bounds in question.

Proposition 8.1
.

Suppose that
G_{0}=E^{\epsilon}_{6}(q)
with
q\geqslant 3
, and
x\in G
has prime order
r
. Then
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant g(x,q)
, where
g(x,q)
is recorded in Table
12
.

Proof.

Case 1.

x
is semisimple:
Here,
[
51
, Theorem 2]
implies that
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant 2q^{-12}
, and we claim that
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant q^{-14}
if
{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}\geqslant 66
. Let
L
denote the simply connected group of type
E_{6}^{\epsilon}(q)
and recall that
L=G_{0}.Z
, where
Z=Z(L)
. Also recall that
Z\cong C_{3}
if
q\equiv 1\allowbreak\mkern 4.0mu({\operator@font mod}\,\,3)
and
Z=1
otherwise.

By close inspection of
[
39
]
, we find that

|C_{L}(x)|\leqslant(q^{2}-1)(q^{3}+1)(q^{2}-q+1)(q^{2}+q+1)

when
\epsilon=+
and
{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}\geqslant 66
. Since
|x^{L}|
can split into at most
G_{0}
-classes, we have

\displaystyle|x^{G}|

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{|E_{6}(q)|}{3q^{3}(q^{3}+1)(q^{2}+q+1)(q^{2}-q+1)}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{3}q^{39}(q^{2}-1)(q^{3}-1)(q^{5}-1)(q^{6}+1)(q^{8}-1)(q^{9}-1)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{q^{39}(q^{2}-1)(q^{3}-1)(q^{5}-1)(q^{6}+1)(q^{8}-1)(q^{9}-1)}{3(q^{2}-1)}

so by Lemma
2.1
we have
|x^{G}|>\frac{1}{6}q^{64}
.

Similarly, if
\epsilon=-
, then we find

|C_{L}(x)|\leqslant q^{3}(q^{2}-1)(q^{3}+1)(q+1)^{4}

and again using Lemma
2.1
we find
|x^{G}|>\frac{1}{6}q^{64}
.

If
|H|\leqslant q^{48}
, then we deduce that
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<2q^{-15}<q^{-14}
, as required, so now suppose
|H|>q^{48}
. Here,
[
33
]
tells us that
H
must be of type
F_{4}
, in which case we verify that
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<q^{-14}
by inspecting the proof of
[
25
, 4.18]
.

Case 2.

x
is unipotent:
Here,
x\in G_{0}
. If
p>2
, then we check that the relevant bounds hold by inspecting
[
25
, 4.14–4.20]
, so let us assume that
p=2
. If
{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}<40
, then the claim follows directly by
[
51
, Theorem 2]
, so we may now assume that
{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}\geqslant 40
. That is,
x
is in the
G
-class
A_{1}^{3}
. If
|H|\leqslant q^{32}
, or
H
is of type
E_{6}^{\epsilon}(q^{1/2})
, then the claim is verified in the proofs of
[
25
, 4.14, 4.20]
respectively, so we may assume that
H=N_{G}(\bar{M}_{\sigma})
where
\bar{M}
is a
\sigma
-stable subgroup of
\bar{G}
.

First suppose that
\bar{M}=A_{1}A_{5}
. By Bala-Carter, the
\bar{M}
-class of
x
corresponds to a pair
(L,P_{L^{\prime}})
, where
L
is a Levi subgroup of
\bar{M}
and
P_{L^{\prime}}
is a distinguished parabolic subgroup of
L^{\prime}
. If
L
