is also a Levi subgroup of
\bar{G}
, then the
\bar{G}
-class has the same label as the
\bar{M}
-class, whilst if
L=A_{1}^{4}
, then we deduce that
x
lies in the
G
-class
A_{1}^{3}
via
[
54
, Table 17]
. Now it is shown in
[
25
, 4.15]
that
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<4q^{-\delta(x)}
, where
\delta(x)={\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}-{\rm dim}\,(x^{\bar{G}}\cap\bar{M})
, and so we compute that
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<4q^{-20}
.

Next consider the case
\bar{M}=D_{5}T_{1}
. Here
[
33
, Tables 9 and 10]
give
H_{0}=H\cap G_{0}={\rm P\Omega}_{10}^{\epsilon}(q)\times\frac{q-\epsilon}{e}
, where
e=(3,q-\epsilon)
. Therefore,
[
51
, 1.3]
implies that

|x^{G}\cap H|\leqslant i_{2}(H_{0})=i_{2}({\rm Aut}({\rm P\Omega}_{10}^{\epsilon}(q))<2(q+1)q^{24}

so the claim is satisfied.

Now suppose that
\bar{M}=T_{2}D_{4}.S_{3}
. Here we deduce via
[
33
, Tables 9 and 10]
that
x^{G}\cap H\subseteq\widetilde{H}
, where
\widetilde{H}={\rm O}_{8}^{+}(q)
or
{}^{3}D_{4}(q)
. Hence, we have

|x^{G}\cap H|\leqslant i_{2}(\widetilde{H})\leqslant 2q^{16}

so certainly
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<2q^{-8}
, as required.

Finally, if
\bar{M}=F_{4}
, then we deduce via
[
53
, Table A]
that there are no involutions
x\in H
with
{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}\geqslant 40
.

Case 3.

x
is a graph or field or graph-field automorphism:
If
r
is odd and
|H|\leqslant q^{32}
we observe that
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant q^{32-78(1-1/r)}
, as

|x^{G}|\geqslant|E_{6}^{\epsilon}(q)\,:\,E_{6}^{\epsilon}(q^{1/r})|>\frac{1}{6}q^{78(1-1/r)}.

In all other cases the bounds derived in the proofs of
[
25
, 4.14–4.20]
are again sufficient, so the proof is complete.
∎

Proposition 8.2
.

Suppose that
G_{0}=E^{\epsilon}_{6}(2)
and
x\in G
has prime order
r
. Then
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant g(x,q)
, where
g(x,q)
is recorded in Table
13
.

Proof.

If
H
is almost simple with socle
{\rm Fi}_{22}
, then the fusion of conjugacy classes of
H
in
G
is computable in
GAP
using the Character Table Library, so we can compute precise fixed point ratios for all
x\in G
, as noted in Section
2.2
. The same is true if
G={}^{2}E_{6}(2).2
and
H={\rm SO}_{7}(3)
. So, in view of
[
25
, 4.13]
, we may assume from now on that
|H|\leqslant 2^{32}
or
H=N_{\bar{G}}(\bar{M}_{\sigma})
where
\bar{M}
is some
\sigma
-stable subgroup of
\bar{G}
.

Case 1.

x
semisimple:
If
{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}
, then the claim follows directly by
[
51
, Theorem 2]
, so we may assume that
{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}\geqslant 42
. If
H=N_{\bar{G}}(\bar{M}_{\sigma})
with
\bar{M}=F_{4}
, then we note that
G\leqslant G_{0}.2
, and the character tables of
G_{0}
and
H_{0}=H\cap G_{0}
are both available in
GAP
. Since
x\in G_{0}
, we can compute the fusion of semisimple
H_{0}
-classes in
G_{0}
and verify that the bounds in Table
13
are satisfied for all semisimple elements.

Next, if
H=N_{\bar{G}}(\bar{M}_{\sigma})
with
\bar{M}=D_{5}T_{1}
then the bounds in the proof of
[
25
, 4.16]
suffice.

In all remaining cases, we can infer from
[
33
, Tables 9 and 10]
that
|x^{G}\cap H|\leqslant 2^{38}
and we immediately get
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant 2^{38}/|x^{G}|
when
{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}>42
, so finally let us assume that
{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}=42
, so
C_{\bar{G}}(x)^{0}=T_{1}A_{5}
.

First suppose that
|H|\leqslant 2^{32}
. We can infer from
[
25
, Table 7]
that
|x^{G}|>2^{41}
, so we have
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<2^{-9}
.

Now assume that
H=N_{\bar{G}}(\bar{M}_{\sigma})
where
\bar{M}
is some
\sigma
-stable subgroup of
\bar{G}
. If
\bar{M}=A_{1}A_{5}
, then
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<2^{-14}
according to
[
25
, 4.15]
.

Finally, if
\bar{M}=T_{2}D_{4}.S_{3}
, then inspection of
[
33
, Tables 9 and 10]
gives

|x^{G}\cap H|<|H_{0}|\leqslant 18\cdot|{\rm P\Omega}_{8}^{+}(2)|<2^{33}

where
H_{0}=H\cap G_{0}
, so the claim follows.

Case 2.

x
is unipotent:
If
x
is in class
A_{1}
, then
[
51
, Theorem 2]
gives
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant 2^{-5}
, so next assume that
x
is in class
A_{1}^{2}
or
A_{1}^{3}
. If
|H|\leqslant 2^{32}
, then the claim is proved in
[
25
, 4.14]
, so we may now assume that
H=N_{\bar{G}}(\bar{M}_{\sigma})
where
\bar{M}
is some
\sigma
-stable subgroup of
\bar{G}
.

If
\bar{M}=A_{1}A_{5}
, then the bound
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant 4\cdot 2^{-\delta(x)}
is still valid, as highlighted in
[
25
, 4.15]
, and we find that
\delta(x)=16
if
x
is in
A_{1}^{2}
and
\delta(x)=20
if
x
is in
A_{1}^{3}
, so the claim holds.

If
\bar{M}=D_{5}T_{1}
, then we note as above that
x^{G}\cap H\subseteq{\rm\Omega}_{10}^{\epsilon}(2)
, and so using Magma we find

|x^{G}\cap H|\leqslant i_{2}({\rm\Omega}_{10}^{\epsilon}(2))=21999615.

One can check that this bound is sufficient in both cases.
Now suppose that
\bar{M}=T_{2}D_{4}.S_{3}
. As above we have

|x^{G}\cap H|\leqslant\max\{i_{2}({}^{3}D_{4}(2).3),i_{2}(\Omega_{8}^{+}(q).S_{3})\}\leqslant 2^{15}

which is sufficent.

Case 3.

x is an involutory graph automorphism:
Here, the bounds in Table
13
can be verified by inspecting
[
25
, 4.14–4.20]
, so the proof is complete.

∎

\begin{array}[]{lll}\hline\cr x&g(x,q)&\text{Conditions}\\

&q^{-6}&32\leqslant{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}<40,\,p>2\\

&12q^{-26(1-1/r)}&r\geqslant 3\\

\hline\cr\end{array}

and

non-parabolic.

Proposition 8.3
.

Suppose that
G_{0}=E_{7}(q)
and
x\in G
has prime order
r
. Then
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant g(x,q)
, where
g(x,q)
is recorded in Table
14
.

Proof.

Appealing to
[
25
, 4.6]
we find that either
|H|\leqslant q^{46}
, or
H
is of type
E_{7}(q^{1/2})
, or
H=N_{G}(\bar{M}_{\sigma})
, where
\bar{M}=T_{1}E_{6}.2,A_{1}D_{6},A_{7}.2
, or
A_{1}F_{4}
.

Case 1.

x
is semisimple:
We first consider the case
q\geqslant 3
, as
q=2
needs special attention. Here,
[
51
, Theorem 2]
implies that
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<q^{-19}
, so the desired bound holds if
{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}\leqslant 70
. We may now thus assume that
{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}>70
. Recall that if
L
denotes the simply connected group of type
E_{7}(q)
, then
L=G_{0}.Z
, where
Z=Z(L)
and
Z\cong C_{2}
if
q
is odd and
Z=1
if
q
is even. By carefully inspecting
[
39
]
, we find that

C_{L}(x)\leqslant q^{21}(q+1)(q^{2}-1)(q^{3}+1)(q^{4}-1)(q^{5}+1)(q^{6}-1)(q^{7}+1)

when
{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}>70
, so

\displaystyle|x^{L}|

\displaystyle>q^{42}(q^{3}-1)(q^{4}+1)(q^{5}-1)(q^{6}+1)(q^{7}-1)(q^{17}+q^{16}+\cdots+1)

\displaystyle>q^{69}(q^{3}-1)(q^{5}-1)(q^{7}-1)

and using Lemma
2.1
, we find
|x^{L}|>\frac{1}{2}q^{82}
. Since
x^{L}
can split into at most
2

G_{0}
-classes, we get
|x^{G}|>\frac{1}{4}q^{82}
.

If
|H|\leqslant q^{46}
, then the trivial bound
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant|H|/|x^{G}|
is sufficient. Next, assume that
H
is of type
E_{7}(q^{1/2})
and let
H_{0}=G_{0}\cap H
. First suppose
q
is odd. Inspecting
[
34
, Table 4.1]
tells us that
H_{0}=E_{7}(q^{1/2}).2
. Let
\xi:\alpha\mapsto\alpha^{q^{1/2}}
, so
H_{0}=\bar{G}_{\xi}
. If
C_{\bar{G}}(x)
is connected, then a well-known corollary to the Lang-Steinberg theorem
[
72
, I, 2.7]
gives
x^{\bar{G}}\cap\bar{G}_{\xi}=x^{\bar{G}_{\xi}}
and similarly
x^{\bar{G}}\cap\bar{G}_{\xi^{2}}=x^{\bar{G}_{\xi^{2}}}
. Hence, in view of Proposition
3.5
we have
x^{G_{0}}\cap H=x^{H_{0}}
. On the other hand, if
C_{\bar{G}}(x)
is disconnected, then the fact that
|C_{\bar{G}}(x)\,:\,C_{\bar{G}}(x)^{0}|=2
, implies that
x^{\bar{G}}\cap H_{0}
might be a union of two distinct
H_{0}
-classes and the same holds for
G_{0}
. We therefore get

(8)

{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant\frac{2\max\{|y^{H_{0}}|\,:\,y\in x^{\bar{G}}\}}{\min\{|y^{G_{0}}|\,:\,y\in x^{\bar{G}}\}}

We can now inspect Lübeck’s online data on semisimple classes
[
65
]
to inspect which elements have disconnected centraliser in
\bar{G}
and compute
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)
using (
8
). For example, suppose that
C_{\bar{G}}(x)
is of type
E_{6}T_{1}
. Then
[
65
]
tells us that
C_{\bar{G}}(x)
is disconnected, and in particular we find that

|x^{G_{0}}\cap H|\leqslant 2\frac{|E_{7}(q^{1/2})|}{2|{}^{2}E_{6}(q^{1/2})|(q^{1/2}+1)}<q^{27}

and

|x^{G_{0}}|>\frac{|E_{7}(q)|}{2|E_{6}(q)|(q-1)}=\frac{1}{2}q^{54}

so the claim holds. The other cases are all similar, so we leave the details to the reader. If
q
is even, then
G_{0}=\bar{G}_{\sigma}
and again inspecting
[
34
, Table 4.1]
we find that
H_{0}=E_{7}(q^{1/2})
. Using
[
72
, I, 2.7]
once again, we deduce that
x^{G_{0}}\cap H_{0}
, so the bounds derived in the case where
q
is odd are again sufficient.

Finally, if
H=N_{G}(\bar{M}_{\sigma})
for some
\sigma
-stable subgroup
\bar{M}
of
\bar{G}
, then one can check that the bounds in Table
14
hold by inspecting the proofs of
[
25
, 4.9, 4.10]
and
[
19
, 3.4]
.

Now suppose that
q=2
. In this case the prime order
\bar{G}_{\sigma}
-classes and the possible centraliser types are recorded in
[
8
, Table 2]
. If
{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}\leqslant 66
, then the claim follows from
[
51
, Theorem 2]
, so we may assume that
{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}>66
.

If
|H|\leqslant 2^{46}
, then one can can obtain much stronger bounds than the ones in Table
14
by arguing as in the case
q\geqslant 3
, so we may assume that
H=N_{G}(\bar{M})
for some
\sigma
-stable subgroup
\bar{M}
of
\bar{G}
. If
66<{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}\leqslant 84
, then we can check that
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<2^{-19}
for all the remaining possibilities for
H
by inspecting the proofs of
[
25
, 4.9, 4.10]
and
[
19
, 3.4]
. Now if
{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}>84
, then by inspecting the same proofs, we again obtain sufficient fixed point ratio bounds that this time depend on the value of
z={\rm dim}\,Z(\bar{D}^{0})
, where
\bar{D}=C_{\bar{G}}(x)
. In particular, we find that
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<2^{-21}
if
z\leqslant 3
and
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<2^{-24}
if
z>3
. The authors derive those bounds depending on
z
by appealing to
[
51
, 4.5]
, which implies

{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<\frac{|W(\bar{G})\,:\,W(\bar{M})|\cdot|\bar{M}/\bar{M^{0}}|\cdot 2\cdot(q+1)^{z}\cdot 2}{q^{\delta(x)+z-l}(q-1)^{l}}

where
\delta(x)={\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}-{\rm dim}(x^{\bar{G}}\cap\bar{M})
and
l
is the semisimple rank of
\bar{M}
.

Case 2.

x
is unipotent:
We first consider the case
p=2
. If
{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}\leqslant 54
, then the claim follows directly by
[
51
, Theorem 2]
, so we may assume that
{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}>54
. In this case,
x
does not lie in the class
A_{1}
or
A_{1}^{2}
, and so inspecting
[
59
, Table 22.2.2]
we find that
|x^{G}|>q^{64}
.

If
|H|\leqslant q^{46}
, then we have
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<|H|/q^{64}<q^{-18}
. Next suppose that
H
is of type
E_{7}(q^{1/2})
. Since
x
is unipotent, we must have
x\in H_{0}=H\cap G_{0}
, and note that both the
H_{0}
-class and the
G_{0}
-class of
x
are determined by the labelling of its class in
\bar{G}
. Therefore,
|x^{G_{0}}\cap H_{0}|=|x^{H_{0}}|
, and in particular,
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)=|x^{H_{0}}|/|x^{G_{0}}|<q^{-18}
.

Finally, assume that
H=N_{G}(\bar{M}_{\sigma})
for some
\sigma
-stable subgroup
\bar{M}
of
\bar{G}
. If
\bar{M}=A_{1}D_{6}
, then the fusion of
H
-classes in
G
is described in
[
25
, Table 3]
and from this we can compute precise fixed point ratios and verify that the claim holds. For example, suppose that
x
is in class
A_{1}^{3^{\prime\prime}}
, and write
x=uy
, where
u\in A_{1}
and
y\in D_{6}
. Then
[
25
, Table 3]
tells us that either
u=1
and
y
embeds in
D_{6}
as an
a^{\prime}_{6}
involution (see
[
22
, Section 3.5.4]
for more information on unipotent involutions in orthogonal groups), or
u\neq 1
(so
u
has Jordan decomposition
[J_{2}]
on
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2}
), and
y
embeds in
D_{6}
as a
c_{2}
involution. We therefore get

|x^{G_{0}}\cap H|\leqslant\frac{1}{2}q^{6}(q^{6}-1)(q^{8}-1)(q^{10}-1)+q^{4}(q^{2}-1)(q^{6}-1)(q^{10}-1)<q^{30}

and so
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<q^{30-64}<q^{-18}
. The other cases are all similar, so we omit the details.

Finally, one can check that
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<q^{-18}
for all other possibilities of
\bar{M}
by inspecting the proofs of
[
25
, 4.9, 4.10]
and
[
19
, 3.4]
.

Now assume that
p>2
. If
{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}\leqslant 66
, then the bound once again follows from
[
51
, Theorem 2]
. Now suppose that
{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}>66
. If
H
is of type
A_{1}D_{6}
, then we verify the claim by inspecting
[
25
, 4.10]
, and in every other case the claim is proved by arguing exactly as in the case
p=2
.

Case 3.

x
is a field automorphism:
If
r=2
, then
[
51
, Theorem 2]
gives
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant q^{-22}
, so now assume that
r
is odd. Here Lemma
2.1
gives

|x^{G}|=|E_{7}(q)\,:\,E_{7}(q^{1/r})|>\frac{1}{2}q^{-133(1-1/r)}\geqslant\frac{1}{2}q^{-88}.

If
|H|\leqslant q^{46}
, then we get
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<\frac{1}{2}q^{-42}
, so the claim holds. If
H
is of type
E_{7}(q^{1/2})
, then
x
also acts as a field automorphism on
E_{7}(q^{1/2})
, and so

{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant\frac{|E_{7}(q^{1/2})\,:\,E_{7}(q^{1/2r})|}{|E_{7}(q)\,:\,E_{7}(q^{1/r})|}<4q^{-44}.

In all other cases, one can verify that the bounds in Table
14
are satisfied by inspecting the proofs of
[
25
, 4.9, 4.10]
and
[
19
, 3.4]
, so the proof is complete.
∎

\begin{array}[]{lll}\hline\cr x&g(x,q)&\text{Conditions}\\
\hline\cr x=s&q^{-19}&{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}\leqslant 70,\,q\geqslant 3\\
&q^{-24}&{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}>70,\,q\geqslant 3\\
&2^{-12}&{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}\leqslant 66,\,q=2\\
&2^{-19}&66<{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}\leqslant 84,\,q=2\\
&2^{-21}&{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}>84,\,q=2,\,\text{and }z\leqslant 3\\
&2^{-24}&{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}>84,\,q=2,\,\text{and }z>3\\
x=u&2q^{-12}&{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}<66,\,p>2\\
&q^{-22}&{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}\geqslant 66,\,p>2\\
&2q^{-12}&{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}<54,\,p=2\\
&q^{-18}&{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}\geqslant 54,\,p=2\\
x=\phi&q^{-22}&r=2\\
&8q^{-32}&r\geqslant 3\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 14.
Bounds on
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)
for
G_{0}=E_{7}(q)
and
H
non-parabolic.

Proposition 8.4
.

Suppose that
G_{0}=F_{4}(q)
and
x\in G
has prime order
r
. Then
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant g(x,q)
, where
g(x,q)
is recorded in Table
15
if
q\geqslant 3
and Table
16
if
q=2
.

Proof.

First note that either
|H|\leqslant q^{22}
, or
H
is one of the possibilities recorded in
[
25
, 4.22]
. The case
q=2
will be dealt with using different methods, so for now we will assume
q\geqslant 3
.

Case 1.

x
is semsimple:
If
C_{\bar{G}_{\sigma}}(x)=B_{4}
, then the bound immediately follows from
[
51
, Theorem 2]
, so it is left to deal with the remaining possibilities for
x
. First assume that
|H|\leqslant q^{22}
. If
C_{\bar{G}_{\sigma}}(x)
has a
B_{3}
or
C_{3}
factor, then
{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}\geqslant 28
, and inspecting
[
65
]
gives

|x^{G}|\geqslant\frac{|F_{4}(q)|}{|{\rm Sp}_{6}(q)||{\rm L}_{2}(q)|}=q^{14}(q^{8}+q^{4}+1)(q^{4}+1)(q^{2}+1)>q^{28}.

It follows that
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<q^{-6}
, as required. In all remaining cases we find that
|x^{G}|>q^{31}
, so we get
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<q^{-9}
.

If
H
is of type
F_{4}(q^{1/2})
, then we refer to
[
33
, Table 8]
and we find that
H_{0}=H\cap G_{0}=F_{4}(q^{1/2})
. Now we note that
\bar{G}
is simply connected, and so
C_{\bar{G}}(x)
is connected. Therefore, appealing to
[
72
, I, 2.7]
we find that
x^{G}\cap H_{0}=x^{H_{0}}
. We can therefore compute precise fixed point ratios for all semisimple elements and verify that the bounds in Table
15
are satisfied for this choice of
H
. In all other cases we appeal to the proofs of
[
25
, 4.24–4.27]
and we verify that the claim holds.

Case 2.

x
is unipotent, field or graph-field automorphism:
The bounds in Table
15
can be directly verified by inspecting
[
25
, 4.23–4.27]
, unless
H
is of type
F_{4}(q^{1/2})
. If
x
is unipotent, then we claim that
x^{G}\cap H=x^{H_{0}}
. This follows from the fact that both the
G_{0}
and
H_{0}
-class of
x
are determined by the labelling of its class in
\bar{G}
, and so one can verify that the bounds in Table
15
are satisfied. Finally, if
x
is a field (respectively, graph-field) automorphism, then
x
also acts as a field (respectively, graph-field) automorphism on
H
, and so we get

{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)=\frac{|F_{4}(q^{1/2})\,:\,F_{4}^{\epsilon}(q^{1/2r})|}{|F_{4}(q)\,:\,F^{\epsilon}_{4}(q^{1/r})|}

which is sufficient.

Now assume that
q=2
, so
G=G_{0}
, or
G_{0}.2
. If
x
is an involutory graph-field automorphism, then
[
51
, Theorem 2]
implies that
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<2^{-6}
, so we may now assume that
x\in G_{0}
.

Recall that if
|H|>2^{22}
, then the possibilities for
H
are determined in
[
25
, 4.22]
, and so we can check that the character tables of both
G_{0}=F_{4}(2)
and
H_{0}=H\cap G_{0}
are computable in
GAP
. It follows that we can determine the fusion of
H_{0}
-classes in
G_{0}
and we note that
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant\frac{|x^{G_{0}}\cap H_{0}|}{|x^{G_{0}}|}
by Lemma
2.4
. Now assume that
|H|\leqslant 2^{22}
. If
{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}<28
, then as noted in
[
25
, 4.23]

x
is an involution belonging to one of the
\bar{G}
-classes labelled
A_{1},\tilde{A}_{1}
, or
\tilde{A}_{1}^{(2)}
, and we have
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant(2^{4}-2^{2}+1)^{-1}=13^{-1}
by
[
51
, Theorem 1]
if
x\in A_{1}
or
\tilde{A}_{1}
, and
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<2^{-4}
if
x\in\tilde{A}_{1}^{(2)}
. If
{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}\geqslant 28
, then
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant 2^{22}/|x^{G}|
We now set

g(x,q)=\max\{{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\,:\,H\in\mathcal{M}_{G}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{G}\}

and we record this value in Table
16
for each prime order class. For semisimple elements we denote
x
by its centraliser type, whereas for unipotent elements, we use the notation in
[
59
]
.
∎

\begin{array}[]{lll}\hline\cr x&g(x,q)&\text{Conditions}\\
\hline\cr x=s&2q^{-5}&C_{\bar{G}}(x)=B_{4}\\
x=s&q^{-6}&C_{\bar{G}}(x)=B_{3}T_{1},\text{ or }C_{3}T_{1}\\
x=s&2q^{-9}&C_{\bar{G}}(x)=A_{2}\tilde{A}_{2},A_{1}\tilde{A}_{2}T_{1},\tilde{A}_{2}T_{2},\text{ or }B_{2}T_{2}\\
x=s&q^{-9}&C_{\bar{G}}(x)\text{ other}\\
x=u&(q^{4}-q^{2}+1)^{-1}&{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}\leqslant 22\\
x=u&q^{-8}&22<{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}\leqslant 28,\,p>2\\
x=u&3q^{-8}&28<{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}\leqslant 30,\,p>2\\
x=u&3q^{-10}&{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}>30,\,p>2\\
x=u&q^{-6}&{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}>22,\,p=2\\
x=\phi\text{ or }\phi\tau&q^{-6}&r=2\\
x=\phi&4q^{-16(1-1/r)}&r\geqslant 3\\

,

.

9.
Fixed point ratios for parabolic actions

In this section, we describe how we obtain sufficient fixed point ratio estimates for groups with socle in
G_{0}
over a given parabolic subgroup
H
of
G
when
G_{0}=\mathcal{E}_{2}\cup\mathcal{E}_{3}
. We will be using the same methods that are used and discussed in
[
51
]
. In particular, if
\Omega=G/H
, then we have

{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)=\frac{\chi(x)}{|\Omega|},

where
\chi(x)=1_{\bar{H}_{\sigma}}^{\bar{G}_{\sigma}}
is the corresponding permutation character. We divide the section into four subsections, where we discuss our approach for semisimple elements, unipotent elements, field and graph-field automorphisms, and graph automorphisms respectively.

9.1.
Semisimple elements

Let
x\in\bar{G}_{\sigma}
be a semisimple element and let
\Omega=G/H
. As described above,
|C_{\Omega}(x)|=\chi(x)
, where
C_{\Omega}(x)=\{\omega\in\Omega\,:\,\omega^{x}=\omega\}
is the fixed point set of
x
in
\Omega
.

Let
W
denote the Weyl group of
\bar{G}
and let
W_{\bar{H}}
denote the Weyl group of
\bar{H}
, so
W_{\bar{H}}
is a standard parabolic subgroup of
W
. We write
\Phi
for the root system of
\bar{G}
with respect to a fixed maximal torus, we let
\Pi
be a simple system of roots for
\bar{G}
and
\alpha_{0}
to be the highest root in
\Phi
with respect to
\Pi
. The possible centraliser types of semisimple elements in
\bar{G}_{\sigma}
are in one-to-one correspondence with pairs
(J,[w])
, where
J\subsetneq\Pi\cup\{\alpha_{0}\}
and is determined up to conjugacy in
W
and
[w]=W_{J}w
is a conjugacy class representative of
N_{W}(W_{J})/W_{J}
, where
W_{J}
denotes the subgroup of
W
generated by reflections in the roots in
J
. This is all described in
[
36
,
39
,
40
]
.

Recall that
W
is
\sigma
-stable, and so
\sigma
induces permutation of the elements of
W
. We say that two elements
w,w^{\prime}\in W
are
\sigma
-conjugate if there exists
x\in W
such that
x^{-1}w\sigma(x)
. Now let
C_{1},\ldots,C_{l}
be the
\sigma
-conjugacy classes of
W
. As explained in
[
51
, Section 3]
, the
\sigma
-stable maximal tori of
\bar{G}
are parametrised by the
\sigma
-classes of
W
, so we will write
T_{i}
for a representative of the
\bar{G}_{\sigma}
-class of maximal tori in
\bar{G}
corresponding to
C_{i}
for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,l\}
, so that
(T_{1})_{\sigma},\ldots,(T_{l})_{\sigma}
is a list of representatives of the
\bar{G}_{\sigma}
-classes of maximal tori of
\bar{G}_{\sigma}
.

We will also write
\epsilon_{T_{i}}=(-1)^{r_{T_{i}}}
, where
r_{T_{i}}
is the relative rank of
T_{i}
(that is the multiplicity of
q-\epsilon
as a divisor of
|(T_{i})_{\sigma}|
) and similarly
\epsilon_{C_{\bar{G}}(x)^{0}}=(-1)^{r_{C_{\bar{G}}(x)^{0}}}
. Finally,
w^{*}
will denote the longest word of
W
if
G_{0}={}^{2}E_{6}(q)
and
w^{*}=1
otherwise and
|(C_{\bar{G}}(x)^{0})_{\sigma}|_{p^{\prime}}
will denote the largest factor of
|(C_{\bar{G}}(x)^{0})_{\sigma}|
not divisible by
p
.

Let
x
be in the
\bar{G}_{\sigma}
-class corresponding to the pair
(J,[w])
. Using the above description of
\bar{G}_{\sigma}
-classes, Lawther, Liebeck, and Seitz provide an explicit formula for the number of fixed points of
x
in
\Omega
in
[
51
, 3.2]
, which we record below:

Proposition 9.1
.

Let
x\in G
be semisimple. With the notation above, we have

|C_{\Omega}(x)|=\chi(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{l}\frac{|W|}{|C_{i}|}\cdot\frac{|W_{\bar{H}}\cap C_{i}{w^{*}}^{-1}|}{|W_{\bar{H}}|}\cdot\frac{|W_{J}w\cap C_{i}|}{|W_{J}|}\cdot\frac{\epsilon_{C_{\bar{G}}(x)^{0}}\cdot\epsilon_{T_{i}}|(C_{\bar{G}}(x)^{0})_{\sigma}|_{p^{\prime}}}{|(T_{i})_{\sigma}|}

Now using Proposition
9.1
, we will derive an upper bound on
|C_{\Omega}(x)|
that will be of use to us when
G_{0}=E_{8}(q)
. Let
\{[w_{1}],\ldots,[w_{t}]\}
be a complete set of conjugacy class representatives in
N_{W}(W_{J})/W_{J}
and order the
\sigma
-classes
C_{1},\ldots,C_{l}
of
W
so that
\epsilon_{T_{i}}=1
for
i\leqslant s
and
\epsilon_{T_{i}}=-1
for
i>s
for some
s\in\{0,\ldots,l\}
. Moreover, for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,l\}
, let
L_{i}=\max_{j\in[t]}|W_{J}w_{j}\cap C_{i}|/|W_{J}|
and
R_{i}=\min_{j\in[t]}|W_{J}w_{j}\cap C_{i}|/|W_{J}|
. We then obtain the following estimate:

Corollary 9.2
.

With the notation above, we have

C_{\Omega}(x)\leqslant|A-B|,

where

A=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\frac{|W|}{|C_{i}|}\cdot\frac{|W_{\bar{H}}\cap C_{i}{w^{*}}^{-1}|}{|W_{\bar{H}}|}\cdot\frac{|(C_{\bar{G}}(x)^{0})_{\sigma}|_{p^{\prime}}}{|(T_{i})_{\sigma}|}\cdot L_{i}

and

B=\sum_{i=s+1}^{l}\frac{|W|}{|C_{i}|}\cdot\frac{|W_{\bar{H}}\cap C_{i}{w^{*}}^{-1}|}{|W_{\bar{H}}|}\cdot\frac{|(C_{\bar{G}}(x)^{0})_{\sigma}|_{p^{\prime}}}{|(T_{i})_{\sigma}|}\cdot R_{i}

Proof.

This follows almost immediately from Proposition
9.1
. In particular, we note that
\epsilon_{C_{\bar{G}(x)^{0}}}
is independent of
i
, so we can omit it from all the summands, and take an absolute value of the summation at the end. Moreover, for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,l\}
, we check which conjugacy class of
N_{W}(W_{J})/W_{J}
gives the largest intersection with
C_{i}
and which class gives the smallest intersection in
C_{i}
, and substituting for
|W_{J}w\cap C_{i}|/|W_{J}|
in the expression gives the required bound.
∎

Remark 9.3
.

We can use the polynomial functionality in
Magma
to estimate
\chi(x)
using Proposition
9.1
and Corollary
9.2
.

The benefit of the upper bound in Corollary
9.2
is that it is not sensitive to the choice of
w
. This is particularly useful when working with groups with socle
E_{8}(q)
, as the number of possible pairs
(J,[w])
, which are listed in
[
40
]
is extremely large, and it would be an incredibly laborious task to consider them all separately. It turns out that the upper bound from Corollary
9.2
is sufficient for our purposes, apart from the case
q=2
. When
q=2
, the possibilities for the centraliser type for
x
are very restricted and they are all given in
[
6
, Section 3]
, so we can use Proposition
9.1
to compute the precise value of
|C_{\Omega}(x)|
.

However, the upper bound in Corollary
9.2
is not sufficient when
G_{0}\in\mathcal{E}_{2}
, in which case we use Proposition
9.1
to compute
|C_{\Omega}(x)|
precisely in all cases.

We finish the discussion on semisimple elements by giving a description of how one can compute fixed point ratios of semisimple elements in
Magma
using Proposition
9.1
and Corollary
9.2
. Suppose that we want to compute the fixed points for a semisimple element
x\in\bar{G}_{\sigma}
corresponding to the pair
(J,[w])
. The key ingredient is the function
fprSemisimple
that takes as input a group of Lie type
G
, a set encoding a parabolic subgroup
P
of
G
in
Magma
(which in our case will be
H
), a set
J
, where
J
is as defined above, a polynomial
poly
, which is the
p^{\prime}
-part of the centraliser of the element in question, and a word
wrd
in standard generators for
W
that encodes
w
in
Magma
. Below we provide the relevant version of
fprSemisimple
for the case where
\bar{G}_{\sigma}
is untwisted. If
\bar{G}_{\sigma}
is twisted, then one can obtain a similar function with very minor modifications. Similarly, if
G_{0}=E_{8}(q)
and we want to use the approximation of
|C_{\Omega}(x)|
given by Corollary
9.2
, it is possible to do so only with slight modifications of the function provided.

fprSemisimple := function(G, P, J, poly, wrd)
W := WeylGroup(G);
id := Identity(W);
R := Roots(G);
Q := RationalField();
L<q> := PolynomialRing(Q, 1);
S := RationalFunctionField(L);
C := Classes(W);
A := TwistedToriOrders(G);
T := [];
for i in [1 .. #A] do
t := A[i];
b := 1;
for l in t[1] do
b := b*Evaluate(l, q);
end for;
Append(~T, b);
end for;

E := [];
for i in [1 .. #T] do
f := Factorization(T[i]);
e := 0;
if f[1][1] eq q - 1 then
e := f[1][2];
end if;
Append(~E, e);
end for;

WJ := ReflectionSubgroup(W, J);

w := wrd;

WP := StandardParabolicSubgroup(W, P);
CP := Classes(WP);

k := L!poly;
f := Factorization(k);
F := 0;
if f[1][1] eq q - 1 then
F := f[1][2];
end if;
k := S!k;

c := Classes(WP);
A := [0: i in [1 .. #C]];
for i in [1 .. #c] do
x := c[i][3];
a := exists(j){j : j in [1 .. #C] | IsConjugate(W, x, C[j][3])};
A[j] := A[j] + c[i][2];
end for;

B := [0: i in [1 .. #C]];
U := [x*w : x in WJ];
for i in [1 .. #U] do
x := U[i];
a := exists(j){j : j in [1 .. #C] | IsConjugate(W, x, C[j][3])};
B[j] := B[j] + 1;
end for;
z := S!0;
for i in [1 .. #C] do
z := z + (#W/C[i][2]) * (A[i]/#WP) * (B[i]/#WJ) * (-1)^(F+E[i]) * k/T[i];
end for;

return z;

end function;

On the right input, the function
fprSemisimple
will return
|C_{\Omega}(x)|
and in this way one can compute
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)
.

Let us see a concrete example when
G_{0}=E_{7}(q)
and
H
is a
P_{1}
parabolic subgroup of
G
. Here we write
\Pi=\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{7}\}
for the simple roots of
\bar{G}
and the possible pairs
(J,[w])
parametrising the
\bar{G}_{\sigma}
-classes are recorded in
[
39
]
. Note that Fleischmann and Janiszczak adopt a labelling of the simple roots of
W
that is different to the standard Bourbaki labelling
[
11
]
. However, the labelling of simple roots in
Magma
agrees with the Bourbaki labelling, so one needs to translate the sets
J
to Bourbaki labelling, when working with
Magma
. In this paper, we will always adopt the Bourbaki labelling.

Let
x\in G
be a semisimple element whose
\bar{G}_{\sigma}
-class corresponds to the pair
(J,[w])
for
J=\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\}
and
w=a_{1}a_{3}
, as given in
[
39
, p.123]
. Translating the Fleischmann and Janiszczak to Bourbaki notation, we find that
a_{1}
and
a_{3}
are the reflections corresponding to
\alpha_{7}
and
\alpha_{5}
in
W
respectively. We also see that
C_{\bar{G}_{\sigma}}(x)
is of type
A_{2}A_{1}T_{4}
, and in particular
|C_{\bar{G}_{\sigma}}(x)|=q^{4}(q^{2}-1)^{4}(q^{3}-1)
. Finally, noting that the
P_{1}
parabolic of
G
is given by
StandardParabolicSubgroup(W, {2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7})
in
Magma
, we can run the following lines of code:

G := GroupOfLieType("E7", 3);
P := {2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7};
W := WeylGroup(G);
id := Identity(W);
R := Roots(G);
Q := RationalField();
L<q> := PolynomialRing(Q, 1);
S := RationalFunctionField(L);
J := {1, 2, #R};
a1 := Reflection(W, 7);
a3 := Reflection(W, 5);
poly := (q^2-1)^4 * (q^3-1);
fprSemisimple(G, P, J, poly, a1*a3);

and we find that
|C_{\Omega}(x)|=q^{3}+8q^{2}+9q+8
. Then dividing by the index of
H
in
G
in
Magma
we can also obtain an expression for
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)
in
q
. We can perform a similar computation to obtain the fixed point ratio of any semisimple element in
\bar{G}_{\sigma}
and any parabolic action.

9.2.
Unipotent elements

Let
x\in\bar{G}_{\sigma}
be a unipotent element and let
W
,
W_{\bar{H}}
, and
\Omega
be as in Section
9.1
. Note that we again have
|C_{\Omega}(x)|=\chi(x)
, where
\chi=1^{\bar{G}_{\sigma}}_{\bar{H}_{\sigma}}
. Now if
\widehat{W}
denotes the set of (ordinary) irreducible characters of
W
, then
[
51
, 2.4]
gives

(9)

\chi(x)=\sum_{\phi\in\widehat{W}}n_{\phi}R_{\phi}(x)

where

(10)

n_{\phi}=\langle 1^{W}_{W_{\bar{H}}},\phi\rangle=\langle 1_{W_{\bar{H}}},\phi|_{W_{\bar{H}}}\rangle_{W_{\bar{H}}}=\frac{1}{|W_{\bar{H}}|}\sum_{w\in W_{\bar{H}}}\phi(w)

and
R_{\phi}(x)
are the
Foulkes functions
of
\bar{G}_{\sigma}
. The values
n_{\phi}
are recorded in
[
51
, pp. 413–415]
when
\bar{G}_{\sigma}
is untwisted and
\bar{H}
is a maximal parabolic subgroup of
\bar{G}
, and we can find them in
[
27
, Table 8]
if
G_{0}=E_{6}(q)
and
H=P_{1,6}
, or
P_{3,5}
, or
G_{0}=F_{4}(q)
and
H=P_{1,4}
or
P_{2,3}
. Finally, if
G_{0}={}^{2}E_{6}(q)
, then the values
n_{\phi}
are recorded in
[
25
, p. 14]
.

The values
R_{\phi}
of unipotent elements of order
p
have been computed by Lübeck
[
64
]
and are given to us in
GAP
-readable form, so we can compute
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)
precisely for all groups with
G_{0}\in\mathcal{E}_{2}\cup\mathcal{E}_{3}
, all unipotent elements and all maximal parabolic subgroups of
G
. We take the opportunity to thank him for making this data available to us.

9.3.
Field and graph-field automorphisms

Let
x\in G
be a field automorphism of prime order
r
. We write
\bar{G}_{\sigma}=\bar{G}(q)
,
\bar{H}_{\sigma}=\bar{H}(q)
and
C_{\bar{G}_{\sigma}}(x)=\bar{G}^{\epsilon}(q^{1/r})
. As explained in the proof of
[
51
, 6.1]
, we have
x^{\bar{G}_{\sigma}}\cap\bar{H}_{\sigma}x=x^{\bar{H}_{\sigma}}
, and moreover
C_{\bar{H}_{\sigma}}=\bar{H}^{\epsilon}(q^{1/r})
, where
\bar{H}^{\epsilon}(q^{1/r})
is the corresponding parabolic subgroup of
C_{\bar{G}_{\sigma}}(x)
and
\epsilon=-
if
x
is a graph-field automorphism and
\epsilon=+
otherwise. In particular, by inspecting the proof of
[
51
, 6.1]
, we get the following statement:

Proposition 9.4
.

With the notation above we have:

{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)=\frac{|\bar{G}^{\epsilon}(q^{1/r})\,:\,\bar{H}^{\epsilon}(q^{1/r})|}{|\bar{G}(q)\,:\,\bar{H}(q)|}.

We will be using this result repeatedly.

9.4.
Graph automorphisms

Here
G_{0}=E_{6}^{\epsilon}(q)
and there are two classes of graph automorphisms, namely those of type
F_{4}
, and those of type
C_{4}
. We record an upper bound
t(H,\tau,q)
for every
H\in\mathcal{P}_{G}
and every involutory graph automorphism
\tau\in G
. If
p=2
, then
[
5
, Section 19]
implies that either
C_{\bar{G}}(x)=F_{4}
or
C_{F_{4}}(t)
for a long root element
t\in F_{4}
. The value
t(H,\tau,q)
can be obtained via
[
51
, 2.6]
when
C_{\bar{G}}(x)=F_{4}
, and if
G_{\bar{G}}(x)=C_{F_{4}}(t)
, then a bound
t(H,\tau,q)
is recorded in
[
25
, Table 6]
. For the reader’s convenience, we record these values in Table
17
.

\begin{array}[]{lll}\hline\cr H&C_{\bar{G}}(\tau)&t(H,\tau,q)\\
\hline\cr P_{2}&F_{4}&(q^{6}-q^{3}-1)^{-1}\\
&C_{F_{4}}(t)&q^{-10}(q-1)^{-1}\\
P_{4}&F_{4}&q^{-6}(q^{2}-1)^{-1}(q-1)^{-1}\\
&C_{F_{4}}(t)&q^{-14}(q-1)^{-2}\\
P_{1,6}&F_{4}&q^{-8}(q-1)^{-1}\\
&C_{F_{4}}(t)&q^{-12}(q-1)^{-1}\\
P_{3,5}&F_{4}&q^{-10}(q-1)^{-1}\\
&C_{F_{4}}(t)&q^{-15}(q-1)^{-2}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 17.
The value
t(H,\tau,q)
when
p=2
.

If
p>2
, then
C_{\bar{G}}(\tau)=F_{4}
or
C_{4}
. Sufficient fixed point ratio bounds can be obtained by working as in the proof of
[
51
, 6.4]
. In the following proposition we carry out these computations to obtain
t(H,\tau,q)
. Note that we will only need an upper bound over all parabolic subgroups for our purposes, but those bounds might be of independent interest, and so we record separate upper bounds for each choice of parabolic subgroup. The value
t(H,\tau,q)
for each distinct pair
(H,\tau)
is recorded in Table
18
.

Proposition 9.5
.

Let
G_{0}=E_{6}^{\epsilon}(q)
, where
q
is odd, and
\tau\in G
be an involutory graph automorphism. If
H\in\mathcal{P}_{G}
, then
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant t(H,\tau,q)
, where
t(H,\tau,q)
is recorded in Table
18
.

Proof.

First observe that
H=N_{G}(P)
, where
P
is a
\tau
-stable parabolic subgroup of
\bar{G}=E_{6}
. Moreover, as shown in
[
51
, 6.4]
,
C_{P}(\tau)
is a parabolic subgroup of
C_{\bar{G}}(\tau)
. Write
P=QL
, where
Q
is the unipotent radical of
P
and
L
is a Levi subgroup, and we treat each possibility for
P
in turn.

First suppose that
P=P_{2}
, in which case
L=A_{5}T_{1}
. Here
\tau
acts as a graph automorphism on
L^{\prime}=A_{5}
, which implies that
C_{L^{\prime}}(\tau)
must either be of type
C_{3}
or
A_{3}
.

If
C_{\bar{G}}(\tau)=F_{4}
, then we note that no proper parabolic subgroup of
F_{4}
has a Levi factor containing
A_{3}
, so
C_{L^{\prime}}(\tau)=C_{3}
and thus
C_{P}(\tau)
is the
P_{1}
parabolic subgroup of
F_{4}
. This implies that

{\rm fpr}(\tau,G/H)=\frac{|H\,:\,C_{H}(\tau)|}{|G\,:\,C_{G}(\tau)|}\leqslant\frac{1}{q^{6}-q^{3}+1}.

If
C_{\bar{G}}(\tau)=C_{4}
, then either
C_{L^{\prime}}(\tau)
is of type
C_{3}
, which corresponds to the
P_{1}
parabolic of
C_{4}
, or
C_{P}(\tau)
is of type
A_{3}
, in which case
C_{L^{\prime}}(\tau)
is the
P_{4}
parabolic of
C_{4}
, and so we find
{\rm fpr}(\tau,G/H)<2q^{-11}
.

Next assume that
P=P_{4}
, in which case
L=A_{2}^{2}A_{1}T_{1}
. In this case,
\tau
must swaps the two
A_{2}
factors of
L^{\prime}
and it either centralises the
A_{1}
factor, or acts on it as an involution with centraliser
T_{1}
. It follows that
C_{L^{\prime}}(\tau)
is either
A_{2}A_{1}
or
A_{2}T_{1}
.

First suppose that
C_{\bar{G}}(\tau)=F_{4}
. If
C_{L^{\prime}}(\tau)=A_{2}A_{1}
, then
C_{P}(\tau)
is the
P_{2}
or
P_{3}
-parabolic subgroup of
F_{4}
. We therefore get
{\rm fpr}(\tau,G/H)<2q^{-9}
. On the other hand, if
C_{L^{\prime}}(\tau)=A_{2}T_{1}
, then the Levi factor of
C_{P}(\tau)
must contain
A_{2}
, and we compute
{\rm fpr}(\tau,G/H)<3q^{-8}
for all choices of parabolic where this is possible.

Now let us assume that
C_{\bar{G}}(\tau)=C_{4}
. If
C_{L^{\prime}}(\tau)=A_{2}A_{1}
, then
C_{P}(\tau)
must be the
P_{3}
parabolic subgroup of
C_{4}
and we compute
{\rm fpr}(\tau,G/H)<2q^{-17}
. On the other hand, if
C_{L^{\prime}}(\tau)=A_{2}T_{1}
, then the Levi factor of
C_{P}(\tau)
must contain
A_{2}
, and by checking all possibilities for
C_{P}(\tau)
, we deduce that
{\rm fpr}(\tau,G/H)<2q^{-16}
.

We now consider the case
P=P_{1,6}
, in which case
L^{\prime}=D_{4}
and
\tau
acts as an involutory graph automorphism on
D_{4}
, and so
C_{L^{\prime}}(\tau)=B_{3}
or
B_{2}A_{1}
.

Since
F_{4}
has no parabolic subgroups with Levi factor containing
B_{2}A_{1}
, it follows that if
C_{\bar{G}}(\tau)=F_{4}
, we must have
C_{L^{\prime}}(\tau)=B_{3}
, and so
C_{P}(\tau)
must be the
P_{4}
parabolic of
F_{4}
. This gives
{\rm fpr}(\tau,G/H)<2q^{-9}
.

On the other hand, if
C_{\bar{G}}(\tau)=C_{4}
, then we must have
C_{L^{\prime}}(\tau)=B_{2}A_{1}
, and so
C_{P}(\tau)
is the
P_{2}
parabolic of
C_{4}
. From this we get
{\rm fpr}(\tau,G/H)<2q^{-12}
.

Finally let us assume that
P=P_{3,5}
, so
L^{\prime}=A_{2}A_{1}A_{1}
. Here
\tau
swaps the two
A_{1}
factors and it either centralises the
A_{2}
factor, or acts on it as an involution with centraliser
A_{1}T_{1}
. Hence, we must have
C_{L^{\prime}}(\tau)=A_{2}A_{1}
, or
A_{1}^{2}T_{1}
.

If
C_{\bar{G}}(\tau)=F_{4}
and
C_{L^{\prime}}(\tau)=A_{2}A_{1}
, then as above we get
{\rm fpr}(\tau,G/H)<2q^{-11}
. If
C_{L^{\prime}}(\tau)=A_{1}^{2}T_{1}
, then the semisimple part of the Levi factor of
C_{P}(\tau)
contains
A_{1}^{2}
, and by checking all possibilities for which this containment is satisfied, we find
{\rm fpr}(\tau,G/H)<4q^{-9}
.

If
C_{\bar{G}}(\tau)=C_{4}
and
C_{L^{\prime}}(\tau)=A_{2}A_{1}
, then as above we get
{\rm fpr}(\tau,G/H)<2q^{-19}
. On the other hand, if
C_{L^{\prime}}(\tau)=A_{1}^{2}T_{1}
, then the semisimple part of the Levi factor of
C_{P}(\tau)
must contain
A_{1}^{2}
, and by inspecting all possibilities for
C_{P}(\tau)
we find that
{\rm fpr}(\tau,G/H)<3q^{-17}
, as required.
∎

\begin{array}[]{lll}\hline\cr H&C_{\bar{G}}(\tau)&t(H,\tau,q)\\
\hline\cr P_{2}&F_{4}&(q^{6}-q^{3}+1)^{-1}\\
&C_{4}&2q^{-11}\\
P_{4}&F_{4}&3q^{-8}\\
&C_{4}&2q^{-16}\\
P_{1,6}&F_{4}&2q^{-9}\\
&C_{4}&2q^{-12}\\
P_{3,5}&F_{4}&4q^{-9}\\
&C_{4}&3q^{-17}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 18.
The value
t(H,\tau,q)
when
p
is odd.

10.
Proof of Theorem
7.1

10.1.
Proof of Theorem
7.1
for groups with socle in
\mathcal{E}_{1}

In this section we prove Theorem
7.1
for the groups with socle in
\mathcal{E}_{1}
. For any prime order element
x\in G
our aim is to derive an upper bound
f(x,q)
, where we recall

f(x,q)=\max\{{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\,:\,H\in\mathcal{M}_{G}\}.

Then noting that

(11)

\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)\leqslant\sum_{x\in\pi}f(x,q)^{6}

where by
\pi
we denote the set of prime order elements in
G
, we can show that the probability in question is strictly less than some function of
q
that tends to zero as
q
tends to infinity and prove the required claim.

In most cases we refer to
[
51
]
to obtain an upper bound on
f(x,q)
. In the cases where the bounds in
[
51
]
are not sufficient, sometimes trivial bounds on the orders of core-free maximal subgroups of
G
are good enough, but in most cases we use bounds derived in
[
25
]
.

Proposition 10.1
.

The conclusion to Theorem
7.1
holds when
G_{0}={}^{2}G_{2}(q)^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Here
q=3^{2m+1}
for some non-negative integer
m
. In view of the isomorphism
{}^{2}G_{2}(3)^{\prime}\cong{\rm L}_{2}(8)
, we may assume that
q\geqslant 27
. In this case we can infer from
[
51
, Theorem 1]
that
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant(q^{2}-q+1)^{-1}
for all prime order elements
x\in G
and
H\in\mathcal{M}_{G}
, and therefore

\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)<|G|\cdot(q^{2}-q+1)^{-6}<q^{-1},

since
|G|<q^{7}\log_{3}(q)
.
∎

Proposition 10.2
.

The conclusion to Theorem
7.1
holds when
G_{0}={}^{2}B_{2}(q)
.

Proof.

Here
q=2^{m+1}
for some
m\geqslant 1
. Observe that
[
51
, Theorem 1]
asserts that
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant(q^{2/3}+1)/(q^{2}+1)
for all prime order elements
x\in G
and
H\in\mathcal{M}_{G}
, and thus

\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)<|G|\cdot\left(\frac{q^{2/3}+1}{q^{2}+1}\right)^{6}<q^{-1}

since
|G|<q^{5}\log_{2}(q)
.
∎

Proposition 10.3
.

The conclusion to Theorem
7.1
holds when
G_{0}={}^{2}F_{4}(q)^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Here
q=2^{2m+1}
for some non-negative integer
m
. The claim can easily be verified using
Magma
for
q=2
using the function
RegOrbitsPlus
given in
[
1
]
, so we may assume that
q\geqslant 8
for the remainder. In particular,
G_{0}={}^{2}F_{4}(q)
.

As noted in
[
25
, Section 4.6]
, we can infer from
[
70
]
that
G
has two classes of involutions with respective representatives
t_{2}
and
t^{\prime}_{2}
, where

|{t_{2}}^{G}|<q^{14}\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,|{{t^{\prime}_{2}}^{G}}|<q^{11}.

Moreover,
[
51
, Theorem 1]
implies that
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant q^{-4}
for all involutions
x\in G
and all
H\in\mathcal{M}_{G}
, so the contribution to
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)
from unipotent elements is less than
2q^{14}\cdot(q^{-4})^{6}=2q^{-10}
.

On the other hand, if
x\in G
is semisimple or a field automorphism of prime order, then
[
51
, Theorem 2]
gives
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant 2q^{-6}
, so we have

\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)<2q^{-10}+|G|\cdot(2q^{-6})^{6}\leqslant(2+64\log_{2}q)\cdot q^{-10}<q^{-1}.

∎

Proposition 10.4
.

The conclusion to Theorem
7.1
holds when
G_{0}={}^{3}D_{4}(q)
.

Proof.

Let
x\in G
be an element of prime order
r
. We claim that if
{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}>20
or
x
is a field automorphism of prime order
r\geqslant 5
, then
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<(q^{2}(q^{3}-2))^{-1}
for any
H\in\mathcal{M}_{G}
. If
H
is parabolic, then the claim follows directly by
[
51
, Theorem 2]
, so assume that
H
is non-parabolic. If
H_{0}={}^{3}D_{4}(q^{1/2})
, then we verify that the bound holds by inspecting the proof of
[
25
, 4.42]
, and in all other cases
|H_{0}|<q^{14}
, so the bound is again satisfied.

Now assume that
{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}\leqslant 20
, or
r\in\{2,3\}
and
x
is either a field or triality graph automorphism. In this case
[
51
, Theorem 1]
implies that
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<(q^{4}-q^{2}+1)^{-1}
and we claim that there are at most
3q^{22}
elements in
G
with
{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}\leqslant 20
.

By inspecting
[
37
, Table 4.4]
, we deduce that there are at most
q^{2}+q+1
such semisimple classes, and we find that there are
4
such unipotent classes by inspecting
[
71
]
. Moreover, if
x
is a field automorphism, then either
r=2
and we have
|x^{G}|<2q^{14}
, or
r=3
and
|x^{G}|<2q^{56/3}<q^{20}
. Note that
G
has at most two classes of order
3
field automorphisms. Finally there are fewer than
4q^{20}+2q^{14}
triality graph automorphisms, so in total we get

(q^{2}+q+11)\cdot q^{20}+4q^{14}\leqslant 3q^{22}

elements with
{\rm dim}\,x^{G}\leqslant 20
.

Hence, we obtain

\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)<3q^{22}\cdot(q^{4}-q^{2}+1)^{-6}+|G|\cdot(q^{2}(q^{3}-2))^{-6}<3q^{-1}

if
q\geqslant 5
, since
|G|<3q^{28}\cdot{\rm log}_{2}q
.
If
q=3
, then we note that there are no field automorphisms and so we can replace
|G|
with
|\bar{G}_{\sigma}|
in the bound above and we obtain the result.
∎

Proposition 10.5
.

The conclusion to Theorem
7.1
holds when
G_{0}=G_{2}(q)^{\prime}
.

Proof.

In view of the isomorphism
G_{2}(2)^{\prime}\cong{\rm U}_{3}(3)
, we may assume that
q\geqslant 3
. In particular,
G_{0}=G_{2}(q)
. Let
x\in G
be a prime order element. If
x
is an involutory field or graph field automorphism, or a long root element, then
[
51
, Theorem 1]
asserts that
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant(q^{2}-q+1)^{-1}
for any
H\in\mathcal{M}_{G}
and we can check that there are at most
q^{8}
such elements in
G
. For all other elements, we claim that
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant(q^{2}(q-1))^{-1}
.

First suppose that
x
is a field automorphism of order
r\geqslant 3
. If
|H_{0}|\leqslant q^{6}
, then the claim trivially holds, since
|x^{G}|>\frac{1}{2}q^{28/3}
as noted in
[
25
, 4.29]
, and so

{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)<\frac{|H|}{|x^{G}|}<q^{-10/3}\log_{2}{q}.

Next, if
H
is of type
{\rm SL}_{3}^{\epsilon}(q)
or
{}^{2}G_{2}(q)
, then we confirm the required bound holds by inspecting the proofs of
[
25
, 4.30, 4.31]
respectively, and if
H
is of type
G_{2}(q^{1/2})
, then
x
acts on
H_{0}
as a field automorphism, so we obtain

{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant\frac{|G_{2}(q^{1/r})\,:\,G_{2}(q^{1/2r})|}{|G_{2}(q)\,:\,G_{2}(q^{1/2})|}<q^{-6(1-1/r)}\leqslant q^{-4}

and the claim holds.

It now remains to show that the bound holds if
H
is parabolic. In this case Proposition
9.4
implies that

{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant\frac{(q^{5}|{\rm GL_{2}}(q)|)/(q^{5/r}|{\rm GL}_{2}(q^{1/r})|)}{q^{14(1-1/r)}}<2q^{-5(1-1/r)}\leqslant 2q^{-10/3}

which is sufficient. For all other possibilities for
x
, the claim follows immediately from
[
51
, Theorem 2]
.

It now follows that

\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)<q^{8}(q^{2}-q+1)^{-6}+\log_{2}q\cdot q^{14}\cdot(q^{2}(q-1))^{-6}<q^{-1}

and the result follows.
∎

10.2.
Proof of Theorem
7.1
for groups with socle in
\mathcal{E}_{2}

In this section we prove Theorem
7.1
for the groups with
G_{0}\in\mathcal{E}_{2}
. In view of (
11
), it suffices to show that

\sum_{x\in\mathcal{P}}f(x,q)^{6}<R(q)

where
\mathcal{P}
denotes the set of prime order elements in
G
and
F
is a function with the properties that
R(q)<1
for all
q
, and
R(q)\rightarrow 0
as
q\rightarrow\infty
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{U}\cup\mathcal{F}
, where
\mathcal{S}
denotes the set of semisimple elements of prime order in
G
,
\mathcal{U}
denotes the class of prime order unipotent elements in
G
, and
\mathcal{F}
denotes the prime order graph, field, and graph field automorphisms of
G
, then we may write

\sum_{x\in\mathcal{P}}f(x,q)^{6}=\alpha+\beta+\gamma,

where

(12)

\alpha=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}}f(x,q)^{6},\,\,\,\beta=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{U}}f(x,q)^{6},\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\gamma=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{F}}f(x,q)^{6}.

Our aim is to derive suitable bounds on
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\gamma
to prove Theorem
7.1
for
G
, which is the strategy that we will be adopting for all groups with socle in
\mathcal{E}_{2}
.

Remark 10.6
.

Let us now record an important remark about semisimple elements. Note that Fleischmann and Janiszczak work with simply connected groups of type
E_{6}
and
E_{7}

[
39
]
, whereas we are interested in adjoint type groups. However, for a semisimple element
x\in\bar{G}_{\sigma}
, we are only using their results on centraliser orders to compute
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)
. As described in Section
9
, we do this by appealing to Proposition
9.1
, and so we are only interested in
C_{\bar{G}_{\sigma}}(x)^{0}
, which is the same both in the adjoint and in the simply connected case.

Proposition 10.7
.

The conclusion to Theorem
7.1
holds when
G_{0}=E_{6}^{\epsilon}(q)
.

Proof.

For now we will assume that
q\geqslant 3
, as
q=2
needs separate consideration. We will derive sufficient bounds for
\alpha,\beta
and
\gamma
, as defined in (
12
), and to do this, we need to compute upper bounds on
m_{x}
for each prime order element
x\in G
. First note that

(13)

f(x,q)\leqslant\max\left(\{g(x,q)\}\cup\{{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\,:\,H\in\mathcal{P}_{G}\}\right)=:F(x,q),

where
g(x,q)
is determined in Proposition
8.1
. It therefore remains to consider fixed point ratio bounds for parabolic subgroups.

We first consider semisimple elements. Recall that the possible centralisers of semisimple elements in
\bar{G}_{\sigma}
are parametrised by pairs
(J,[w])
, where
J
and
w
are defined in Section
9.1
. The possibilities for
(J,[w])
are listed in
[
39
]
, and the number of
\bar{G}_{\sigma}
-classes with centraliser type corresponding to a fixed pair
(J,[w])
is recorded in
[
65
]
. Therefore, if
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{t}\}
is a complete set of representatives of
\bar{G}_{\sigma}
-classes of each centraliser type and
c_{i}
denotes the number of
\bar{G}_{\sigma}
-classes corresponding to a pair
(J,[w])
, then we can write

(14)

\alpha\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{t}c_{i}\cdot|x_{i}^{\bar{G}_{\sigma}}|\cdot f(x_{i},q)^{6}\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{t}c_{i}\cdot|x_{i}^{\bar{G}_{\sigma}}|\cdot F(x_{i},q)^{6}.

Now recall that using
Magma
, we can compute the exact value of
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)
for every parabolic subgroup
H\leqslant G
, which is given by the formula in Proposition
9.1
, and so we can compute the value of
F(x_{i},q)
for every
i\in[t]
. Moreover, we can read off
c_{i}
for every
i\in[t]
from
[
65
]
, and using
Magma
we can compute the polynomial expression

(15)

\sum_{i=1}^{t}c_{i}\cdot|x_{i}^{\bar{G}_{\sigma}}|\cdot F(x_{i},q)^{6}

and check that it is less than
q^{-1}
for all values of
q\geqslant 3
.

Let us see an example. Assume
\epsilon=+
and consider the
\bar{G}_{\sigma}
-class corresponding to the pair
(J,[w])
with
J=\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{6}\}
and
w=a_{1}a_{2}
as given in
[
39
]
. Let
y
be a representative of a
\bar{G}_{\sigma}
-class corresponding to this pair. Inspecting
[
39
]
tells us that
C_{\bar{G}_{\sigma}}(y)
is of type
A_{2}^{2}T_{2}
, and in particular,
|C_{\bar{G}_{\sigma}}(y)|=|{\rm SL}_{3}(q)|^{2}\Phi_{3}(q)
, where
\Phi_{3}(q)=q^{2}-q+1
denotes the third cyclotomic polynomial. We now turn to
[
65
]
and we find that the entry corresponding to the class of
y
is the one with label
(i,j,k)=(12,1,5)
, and from there we can read the number of classes.

We will now derive an upper bound for
\beta
. As described in Section
9.2
, in this case we can compute precise fixed point ratios for parabolic subgroups of
G
using the data in
[
64
]
, and so using
GAP
, we can compute the expression

\beta\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{r}|x_{i}^{\bar{G}_{\sigma}}|\cdot F(x_{i},q)^{6}

where
x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}
represent the prime order unipotent classes of
G
. In this way, we find that

\displaystyle\beta<\begin{cases}0.43\text{ if }q=3,4\\
q^{-1}\text{ if }q\geqslant 5\end{cases}

We finally compute a bound on
\gamma
. Let
\gamma_{1}
denote the contribution from involutory field and graph-field automorphisms,
\gamma_{2}
denote the odd order field automorphisms and
\gamma_{3}
denote the contribution from graph automorphisms.

If
\bar{H}\leqslant\bar{G}
is parabolic and
x
is a field or graph-field automorphism of prime order
r
, then Proposition
9.4
gives

{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant\frac{|E_{6}^{\epsilon}(q^{1/r})\,:\,\bar{H}^{\epsilon}(q^{1/r})|}{|E_{6}(q)\,:\,\bar{H}(q)|}.

where we recall that
\bar{H}(q)=H_{\sigma}
and
\bar{H}(q^{1/r})=C_{\bar{H}_{\sigma}}(x)
.
Note that this implies that
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)
is maximised when
|\bar{H}(q)|
(and consequently
|\bar{H}(q^{1/r})|
) is maximised, and we certainly have
|\bar{H}(q)|\leqslant|P_{1}(q)|
, where
P_{1}
is the
P_{1}
-parabolic subgroup of
E_{6}
. Moreover, if
r=2
, then we note that the fixed point ratio of a graph-field automorphism is greater than the fixed point ratio of a field automorphism. We therefore find

\gamma_{1}\leqslant\frac{3+\epsilon}{2}\cdot\left(\frac{|E_{6}^{\epsilon}(q)|}{|{}^{2}E_{6}(q^{1/2})|}\right)^{-5}\left(\frac{|P_{1}(q)|}{|P_{1}(q^{1/r})|}\right)^{6}

and

\gamma_{2}\leqslant\sum_{r\in\pi^{\prime}_{13}}(r-1)\left(\frac{|E^{\epsilon}_{6}(q)|}{|E^{\epsilon}_{6}(q^{1/r})|}\right)^{-5}\left(\frac{|P_{1}(q)|}{|P_{1}(q^{1/r})|}\right)^{6}+q^{78}\log_{2}{q}\cdot\left(\frac{|E^{\epsilon}_{6}(q^{1/17})\,:\,P_{1}(q^{1/17})|}{|E_{6}^{\epsilon}(q)\,:\,P_{1}(q)|}\right)^{6}

where recall that
\pi^{\prime}_{13}
denotes the set of odd prime numbers at most
13
.

Finally, we infer from Propositions
8.1
,
8.2
, and
9.5
that if
x
is an involutory graph automorhpism, then
f(x,q)\leqslant q^{-5}
if
C_{\bar{G}}(x)=F_{4}
and
f(x,q)\leqslant 12q^{-10}
if
C_{\bar{G}}(x)=C_{4}
. If
q>4
, then we find

\displaystyle\gamma_{3}\leqslant q^{26}(q^{-5})^{6}+q^{42}(12q^{-10})^{6}

If
q\geqslant 5
, then one checks that

\gamma=\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}+\gamma_{3}<q^{-1}.

Finally, if
q=3
or
4
, then by only considering the contribution by involutory graph automorphisms (and field and graph-field automorphisms if
q=4
), we easily compute
\gamma\leqslant 0.025
using
Magma
.

It follows that
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)<1
for all
q
, and moreover,
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)<3q^{-1}
for all
q\geqslant 5
, so
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)\rightarrow 0
as
q\rightarrow\infty
.

Now assume that
q=2
. Here, the value
g(x,q)
can be obtained by Proposition
8.2
. For parabolic subgroups, the unipotent
\bar{G}_{\sigma}
-classes and the tables of Foulkes functions are given to us again by Lübeck in
[
64
]
, so we can compute precise fixed point ratios for involutions in parabolic actions using
GAP
.

The odd order semisimple classes are listed in
[
25
, Table 9]
. From this table we can recover the pair
(J,[w])
corresponding to each semisimple
\bar{G}_{\sigma}
-class via
[
39
]
. More specifically, the centraliser type for each class is listed in
[
25
, Table 9]
, so we can immediately recover
J
by consulting
[
39
]
, and by inspecting the possible centraliser orders for this particular choice of
J
we can then recover
w
. For example, assume that
\epsilon=+
and let us look at the fourth column in
[
25
, Table 9]
. In this case our element
y
has centraliser type
A_{3}T_{3}
. We then turn to
[
39
]
and we find that
J=\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{4}\}
and by computing all possible centraliser orders we deduce that
w=a_{1}a_{17}a_{18}
. In this way we can compute precise fixed point ratios for semisimple elements, as in the case
q\geqslant 3
.

Finally, sufficient fixed point ratio bounds for involutory graph automorphisms are given by Proposition
9.5
.

If
\epsilon=-
, then we can show that
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)<1
for any
6
-tuple
\tau
of
G
using almost the same strategy as in the case
q\geqslant 3
. The only difference is that we only considering prime order elements when finding upper bounds for
\alpha
and
\beta
. In particular, (
13
) still holds, where, as mentioned above,
g(x,q)
is obtained via Proposition
8.2
. Then using (
15
), we find that
\alpha\leqslant 0.1
and by using Lübeck’s data on unipotent elements we find that
\beta\leqslant 0.055
. Finally, the contribution from involutory graph automorphisms is no more than
0.125
, and hence
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)<1
.

The case
\epsilon=+
requires special attention. As pointed out in
[
25
]
, if
G_{0}=E_{6}(2)
and
\tau
is a conjugate
6
-tuple containing copies of
P_{1}
or
P_{6}
, then
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)>1
. Therefore, it is not possible to prove the claim using the same methods as in the case
q\geqslant 3
, so we adopt a slightly different approach. We find that it is possible to construct
P_{1}
and
P_{6}
in
Magma
, and using random search, one can show that every
6
-tuple of
G
only containing copies of
P_{1}
and
P_{6}
is regular. We may therefore assume that
\tau
contains at least one entry not isomorphic to
P_{1}
or
P_{6}
.

For
x\in G
, we define

l(x,q)=\{{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\,:\,H\in\mathcal{M}_{G}\setminus\{P_{1},P_{6}\}\},

and we note that

l(x,q)\leqslant\max\left(\{g(x,q)\}\cup\{{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\,:\,H\in\mathcal{P}_{G}\setminus\{P_{1},P_{6}\}\}\right)=:L(x,q)

Further, observe that our assumption on
\tau
gives

\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)\leqslant\sum_{x\in\mathcal{P}}f(x,q)^{5}l(x,q)\leqslant\alpha^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime},

where

\alpha^{\prime}=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}}f(x,q)^{5}l(x,q),\,\,\,\beta^{\prime}=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{U}}f(x,q)^{5}l(x,q),\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\gamma^{\prime}=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{F}}f(x,q)^{5}l(x,q).

We can now estimate
\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}
from above using the methods described above, and we find
\alpha^{\prime}\leqslant 0.053
,
\beta^{\prime}\leqslant 0.6
, and
\gamma^{\prime}\leqslant 0.125
, so
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)<1
, and the proof is complete.
∎

Proposition 10.8
.

The conclusion to Theorem
7.1
holds if
G_{0}=E_{7}(q)
.

Proof.

We will again estimate
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
, as defined in (
12
) to derive a bound on
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)
. Our approach here is almost the same as in the case
G_{0}=E_{6}^{\epsilon}(q)
for
q\geqslant 3
. In particular, (
13
) applies here as well, where
n_{x}
is obtained this time via Proposition
8.3
for each choice of prime order element
x\in G
.

We first consider semisimple elements. The pairs
(J,[w])
encoding the possible centraliser types of semisimple elements in
\bar{G}_{\sigma}
are again determined in
[
39
]
and so we can compute precise fixed point ratios for each centraliser type using
Magma
, as described in Section
9.1
. Now let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{t}
be representatives of the semisimple
\bar{G}_{\sigma}
-classes of each centraliser type and
c_{i}
be the number of
\bar{G}_{\sigma}
-classes of centraliser type
C_{\bar{G}_{\sigma}}(x_{i})
for each
i\in[t]
. Then we get

\alpha\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{t}c_{i}\cdot|x_{i}^{G}|\cdot f(x_{i},q)^{6}\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{t}C_{i}\cdot|x_{i}^{G}|\cdot f(x_{i},q)^{6}

where
C_{i}
is an upper bound on
c_{i}
for all
i\in[t]
, so it is left to compute sufficient bounds
C_{i}
for all
i\in[t]
to estimate
\alpha
. Note that the exact number of classes corresponding to a given pair
(J,[w])
is given to us by
[
65
]
. However, crude bounds are good enough for most cases, and due to the large number of possible pairs, we will be working with upper bounds instead.

We find in
[
65
]
that the total number of semisimple classes in
G_{\sigma}
is no more than
q^{7}+q^{4}
and unless
C_{G}(x)
has an
E_{6},D_{6}
, or
E_{7}
factor, it suffices to take
C_{i}=q^{7}+q^{4}
. In the final few cases we can estimate
c_{i}
by inspecting
[
65
]
and we give a bound
C_{i}
in Table
19
. In particular, we record the type of
C_{\bar{G}}(x_{i})
, the set
J
and
w
and an upper bound on the number of classes of such type. Recall that we use the standard Bourbaki labelling
[
11
]
when recording the set
J
in this paper, instead of the notation used in
[
39
]
. Now using
Magma
, we can compute the expression

\alpha\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{t}C_{i}\cdot|x_{i}^{G}|\cdot F(x_{i},q)^{6}

and we find that
\alpha<q^{-1}
, and in particular
\alpha\leqslant 0.25
if
q=2
.

For unipotent elements, we can again use the
GAP
-readable data provided by Lübeck in
[
64
]
to compute precise fixed point ratios. If
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\}
is a complete set of representatives of the
\bar{G}_{\sigma}
-classes of elements of order
p
, then using
GAP
we compute

\beta\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{r}|x_{i}^{G}|\cdot F(x_{i},q)^{6}<\begin{cases}0.61\,\text{ if }\,q=2\\
1/q\,\text{ if }\,q\geqslant 3\end{cases}

Finally, if
x
is a field automorphism of prime order
r
, then sufficient fixed point ratio bounds on non-parabolic subgroups are obtained in Proposition
8.3
, and if
H\leqslant G
is parabolic, then Proposition
9.4
gives

{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant\frac{|E_{7}(q^{1/r})\,:\,\bar{H}(q^{1/r})|}{|E_{7}(q)\,:\,\bar{H}(q)|}.

where
\bar{H}(q)
and
\bar{H}(q^{1/r})
are defined as in Section
9.3
. By computing a bound for each parabolic subgroup and comparing with
g(x,q)
, we find that
f(x,q)
is achieved when
\bar{H}
is the
P_{7}
parabolic of
\bar{G}
for all
r
, and so we get

\gamma\leqslant\sum_{r\in\pi_{11}}(r-1)\left(\frac{|E_{7}(q)|}{|E_{7}(q^{1/r})|}\right)^{-5}\left(\frac{|P_{7}(q)|}{|P_{7}(q^{1/r})|}\right)^{6}+q^{133}\log_{2}{q}\cdot\left(\frac{|E_{7}(q^{1/13})\,:\,P_{7}(q^{1/13})|}{|E_{7}(q)\,:\,P_{7}(q)|}\right)^{6}<q^{-1}

for all
q\geqslant 4
.

It follows that
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)<1
for all
q
, with
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)<3q^{-3}
for all
q\geqslant 3
.
∎

\begin{array}[]{llll}\hline\cr\text{Type of $C_{\bar{G}_{\sigma}}(x_{i})$}&J&w&C_{i}\\
\hline\cr E_{6}T_{1}&\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{6}\}&w_{1}&q+2\\
&&w_{2}&q+2\\
D_{6}T_{1}&\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4},\alpha_{5}\}&w_{1}&q/2\\
&&w_{2}&q/2\\
D_{6}A_{1}&\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{7}\}&w_{1}&1\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 19.
The value
C_{i}
for
E_{7}(q)
-classes that contain an
E_{6}
or
D_{6}
factor, as defined in Proposition
10.8
.

Proposition 10.9
.

The conclusion to Theorem
7.1
holds when
G_{0}=F_{4}(q)
.

Proof.

We use the same approach we used in the proof of Proposition
10.7
when
q\geqslant 3
. In particular, (
13
) holds in this case as well, where
g(x,q)
is obtained this time via Proposition
8.4
for each choice of prime order element
x\in G
, so we can estimate
\alpha,\beta
, and
\gamma
, as defined in (
12
) to show that
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)<1
.

First let us consider semisimple elements. In this case the pairs
(J,[w])
encoding the possible centraliser types of semisimple elements can be found in
[
3
, Table 23]
and the number of classes of each centraliser type can be found in
[
65
]
as usual, so we can compute a bound on
\alpha
via (
15
) and we find that
\alpha<q^{-1}
.

The contribution to
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)
from unipotent elements can be estimated in the usual way. Sufficient fixed point ratio bounds for non-parabolic subgroups can be obtained via Proposition
8.4
and
GAP
-readable data that allow us to compute precise fixed point ratios for parabolic subgroups are again given to us by Lübeck
[
64
]
. Therefore, if
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\}
is a complete list of representatives for the conjugacy classes of prime order unipotent elements in
G
, we can compute the expression

\sum_{i=1}^{r}|x_{i}^{G}|\cdot F(x_{i},q)^{6}

using
GAP
and we deduce that
\beta<q^{-1}
.

It is only left to obtain a sufficient bound for
\gamma
. Let
x
be a field or graph-field automorphism of prime order
r
. We have already obtained a sufficient fixed point ratio bound for non-parabolic subgroups in Proposition
8.4
, and for a parabolic subgroup
H
of
G
we can once again appeal to Proposition
9.4
to get

{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant\frac{|F_{4}^{\epsilon}(q^{1/r})\,:\,\bar{H}^{\epsilon}(q^{1/r})|}{|F_{4}(q)\,:\,\bar{H}(q)|}.

In this way, we find that
f(x,q)\leqslant q^{-6}
if
r=2
, whilst
f(x,q)
is achieved when
\bar{H}=P_{1}
if
r\geqslant 3
. Noting that
|x^{G}|<2q^{26}
if
x
is an involutory field or graph-field automorphism, we find

\gamma\leqslant 4q^{26}(q^{-6})^{6}+\sum_{r\in\pi^{\prime}_{17}}(r-1)\left(\frac{|F_{4}(q)|}{|F_{4}(q^{1/r})|}\right)^{-5}\left(\frac{|P_{1}(q)|}{|P_{1}(q^{1/r})|}\right)^{6}+2q^{52}\log_{2}{q}\cdot\left(\frac{|F_{4}(q^{1/19})\,:\,P_{1}(q^{1/19})|}{|F_{4}(q)\,:\,P_{1}(q)|}\right)^{-6}<q^{-1},

where we recall that
\pi^{\prime}_{17}
is the set of odd primes at most 17. Therefore
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)<1
and if
q\geqslant 3
, then
\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)<3q^{-1}
.
∎

10.3.
Proof of Theorem
7.1
for groups with socle in
\mathcal{E}_{3}

In this final section we prove Theorem
7.1
for groups with socle
G_{0}=E_{8}(q)
, which completes the proof of Theorem
1
.

Proposition 10.10
.

The conclusion to Theorem
7.1
holds if
G_{0}=E_{8}(q)
.

Proof.

We will divide the prime order elements of
G
into five collections
\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{5}
. In particular, we have
x\in\mathcal{L}_{i}
if and only if
x\in\bar{G}_{\sigma}
and
a_{i}\leqslant{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}<b_{i}
, or
x
is a field automorphism with
q^{a_{i}}<|x^{G_{0}}|<q^{b_{i}}
, where
a_{i}
and
b_{i}
are listed in Table
20
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,5\}
. We claim that if
x\in\mathcal{L}_{i}
, then
f(x,q)\leqslant c_{i}
, where
c_{i}
is listed in Table
20
for all
i
. Moreover, we claim there are no more than
d_{i}
prime order elements in
\mathcal{L}_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,5\}
, where
d_{i}
is again listed in Table
20
for all
i
. This would in turn imply that

\widehat{Q}(G,\tau)<\sum_{i=1}^{5}d_{i}c_{i}^{6}<q^{-1}

which proves the claim.

First let us record some lower bounds on
|x^{G_{0}}|
for a prime order element
x\in G
, which we will need below. In particular, we claim that if
x\in\mathcal{L}_{i}
for some
i\in\{1,\ldots,5\}
, then
q^{88}/c_{i}<|x^{G}|<q^{b_{i}}
. All cases are similar, so we look at the case
i=2
, and we leave the remaining cases for the reader to check. If
x\in\mathcal{L}_{2}
is a field automorphism, then the claim follows straight from the definition, so we may assume that
x\in\bar{G}_{\sigma}
. First suppose that
x
is semisimple. In this case, inspecting
[
40
]
tells us that the possible centraliser types for
x
are

D_{5},A_{4}^{2},A_{6},D_{5}A_{1},A_{6}A_{1},D_{5}A_{1}^{2},D_{5}A_{2},D_{5}A_{3},A^{\prime}_{7},A^{\prime\prime}_{7}\text{ and }A_{7}A_{1}

and from this we deduce that

|x^{G_{0}}|\geqslant\frac{|E_{8}(q)|}{|{\rm SU_{8}(q)}||{\rm SL}_{2}(q)|}>q^{139}(q^{28}+q^{27}+\dots+q^{2}+1)(q^{3}-1)(q^{7}-1)(q^{5}-1)>q^{125}.

Similarly, we find that

|x^{G_{0}}|\leqslant\frac{|E_{8}(q)|}{|{\rm SO}_{10}^{-}(q)|(q-1)^{3}}<2q^{200}

by Lemma
2.1
, so the claim follows.

Now suppose that
x
is unipotent. In this case inspecting
[
59
, Table 22.2.1]
tells us that the smallest class of elements in
\mathcal{L}_{2}
is
A_{3}A_{2}A_{1}
and the largest is
D_{5}
. We therefore find

|x^{G_{0}}|=\frac{|E_{8}(q)|}{q^{60}|{\rm SL}_{2}(q)|^{2}}>q^{125}

Similarly, we get

|x^{G_{0}}|\leqslant\frac{|E_{8}(q)|}{q^{27}|{\rm Sp}_{6}(q)|}<q^{202}

We now show that
f(x,q)\leqslant c_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,5\}
. Fix an
i\in\{1,\ldots,5\}
, a prime order element
x\in G
such that
a_{i}\leqslant{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}<b_{i}
and
H\in\mathcal{M}_{G}
. If
i=5
, then
[
51
, Theorem 1]
implies that
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant 2q^{-24}=c_{5}
, so we may assume that
i\leqslant 4
. First assume that
H
is non-parabolic. If
|H|\leqslant q^{88}
, then the claim holds from the claim above as
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant|H|/|x^{G}|\leqslant c_{i}
.

Now assume that
|H|>q^{88}
. The possibilities for
H
are described in
[
25
, 4.1]
and one can check that
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant c_{i}
by inspecting the proofs of
[
25
, 4.3–4.5]
.

Finally assume that
H
is parabolic. If
x
is unipotent, then using
[
64
]
, we can compute precise fixed point ratios in
GAP
for each conjugacy class and verify the claim. Now suppose that
x
is semisimple. If
q\geqslant 3
, then using
Magma
, we compute the upper bound for
\chi(x)
derived in Corollary
9.2
, we verify that
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant c_{i}
in all cases. If
q=2
, then the prime order semisimple classes of
G
are listed in
[
6
, Tables 3–15]
, and by calculating precise fixed point ratios for each class, we again deduce that
{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant c_{i}
in all cases.

Finally, if
x
is a field automorphism, then Proposition
9.4
gives us

{\rm fpr}(x,G/H)\leqslant\frac{|E_{8}(q^{1/r})\,:\,\bar{H}(q^{1/r})|}{|E_{8}(q)\,:\,\bar{H}(q)|}<c_{i}.

It now remains to show that the number of prime order elements
x\in G
with
a_{i}\leqslant{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}<b_{i}
is bounded above by
d_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,5\}
. We treat each possibility of
i
in turn.

Case 1:

i=1
.

In this case
d_{1}\leqslant|G|
, so the claim about
d_{1}
is trivially true.

Case 2:

i=2
.

Note that if
182\leqslant{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}<202
, then
x
is either semisimple or unipotent, or a field automorphism of order
5
. It follows from
[
65
]
that
G
has fewer than
2q^{8}
semisimple classes and we verify using
[
64
]
that there are exactly 24 unipotent classes with
182\leqslant{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}<202
. Finally, there are no more than 4 classes of field automorphisms of order
5
, and
|x^{G_{0}}|<q^{202}
, as shown above, so we get

q^{202}(2q^{8}+28)<3q^{210}=d_{2}

as required.

Case 3:

i=3
.

Here
154\leqslant{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}<182
, and so
x
is either semisimple or unipotent, or a field automorphism of order
3
. By inspecting
[
65
]
we deduce that there are at most
2q^{2}+3q+6
semisimple classes with
154\leqslant{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}<182
and it follows from
[
64
]
that there are exactly 11 unipotent classes whose dimension is in this range. Finally there are no more than
2
classes of involutory field automorphisms. It is also clear that
|x^{G}|<q^{182}
, and so we get

q^{182}(2q^{2}+3q+19)<q^{188}=d_{3}

as claimed.

Case 4:

i=4
.

By inspecting
[
64
]
and
[
65
]
one deduces that there are only
4
unipotent classes with dimension in this range. Moreover, one can verify that
|x^{G}|<q^{154}
for all such classes, and so we get
4q^{154}<q^{156}=d_{4}
, as claimed.

Case 5:

i=5
.

Inspecting
[
65
]
tells us that there are at most
q+2
semisimple classes such that
{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}<129
and it follows from
[
64
]
that there are
6
such unipotent classes. Finally, there is a unique class of involutory field automorphisms in
G
, and
|x^{G}|<q^{129}
if
{\rm dim}\,x^{\bar{G}}<129
, so we find

q^{129}(q+9)<q^{133}=d_{5}

which completes the proof.

\begin{array}[]{llll}\hline\cr a_{i}&b_{i}&c_{i}&d_{i}\\
\hline\cr 202&242&q^{-42}&q^{248}\log_{2}(q)\\
182&202&q^{-37}&2q^{211}\\
154&182&q^{-32}&q^{188}\\
129&154&q^{-29}&q^{156}\\
58&129&2q^{-24}&q^{133}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 20.
The values
a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i}
as defined in Proposition
10.10
.

∎
